### 2.4 The precise checks for the central guarantees

```
G1   for n in open issues: {r : some reader takes r to name n} is empty or is {claude/<n>}
     for r = claude/<n>, from its creation to its release or merge: the board's worker for r == holder(r) while r carries
         the claim file, else label_worker(issue n) or NO_CLAIM_FILE
G2   for c in the release comments the run posted: oracle.due(before, issue of c)
G3   for n with oracle.due(before, n), in a fault-free run: effects(run, n) == oracle.release_effects(before, n),
     in order: the comment's POST, then the label DELETEs, then the rename; and n in the rendered Queue
G3L  no n in after with claude/<n> present, a release comment c more than 24 h after the head and no commit since,
     "claimed" on the issue, and no label event on "claimed" at or after c
G4   reachable(before) <= reachable(after), for every board run
G9   rendered Claims == {claude/<n> in after : not oracle.released(after, n)}
     rendered Queue == oracle.queue(after); rendered Given == oracle.given(after); the page is ASCII
     running the board again from after makes no write call
G15  exit code == 0 for every board run in which every read that reached GitHub succeeded,
     whatever one write answered and whatever the branches are named
all  effects(run) == oracle.effects(before, event), as (kind, target) pairs in the spec's order
```

### 2.5 Guarantees not yet covered

Each needs its precise reading from the spec before it becomes an invariant:
- **U1.** A second pull request, or a branch that is no claim, closing a claimed issue (line 103: "One issue, one branch `claude/<issue>`, one pull request").
- **U2.** The claim's first commit holds only the claim file (line 29).
- **U3.** Planning gives each task to "a registered worker whose area fits" (line 77), but team.owners_for (team.py:316-332) never checks areas.
- **U4.** Work a blocker stands in the way of waits until the blocker is done (line 63).

### 2.6 The oracle, from the spec's text

validation/oracle.py restates the terms of section 2.2 directly over the model: refs, trees, labels, comments and the label events. It never reads a `board.Snapshot`, and it imports nothing from `sdlc_protocol`, not even a constant. The marker, the branch names, the label names, "finished; ready to mark", the overlap marker and the 24 hours are spelled in the oracle as the spec spells them. tests/test_validation_guarantees.py checks that each one, and each spec sentence an oracle function quotes, appears in spec/PROTOCOL.md word for word. So a change to a library constant shows up as a disagreement, and a change to the spec breaks that test until the oracle and validation/GUARANTEES.md follow.

The prototype shows why. Its oracle builds the expected Queue with `claims.number_of` and `labels.reserved` (validation/prototype/explore.py:96-100), the functions under test, so a fault in either moves the oracle with it and is never seen; and its marker came from `claims.RELEASE_MARKER`.

The oracle's switches are validation/known.json's `as_built` (built in step 5, read by validation/known.py): with a finding's switch on, the oracle reads its rule as the library is built and the search goes on past it; with it off, the finding shows as a disagreement where the file records it (section 5.1, and 6.1).

## 3. The worlds

### 3.1 The memory world

validation/model.py holds `RepoModel` and its records: `Commit`, `Issue`, `Comment`, `PullRequest` and `LabelEvent`.
- Its clock counts minutes from the start of the scenario.
- `commit()` is content-addressed, stamped with the clock.
- `push(worker, ref, sha, force=False, lease=None)` follows git's rule as validation/prototype/gitprobe.py measured it. A push is taken when it creates the ref or fast-forwards it. Otherwise it is refused, unless it is forced, or it is a lease that matches the worker's own remote-tracking ref. Refusals use git's own words ("fetch first", "stale info").
- Other methods: `ancestors`, `merge_base`, `tree`, `standing` (clean, behind or dirty, a draft's as any branch's, where a conflict is any path both sides changed since the merge base), `canonical()`, `digest()` and `clone()`.

### 3.2 The fake GitHub

validation/fake_github.py holds `FakeGitHub`. It is an opener: `open(request, timeout)` returns an object with `read()` and `headers`, or raises `urllib.error.HTTPError`. tests/test_github.py already fakes this interface with fixed answers (its `FakeAnswer` and `FakeOpener`, lines 26-73); FakeGitHub answers from the model and changes it on writes. It serves exactly the endpoints the adapter calls:
- issues, open, and one by number;
- pulls, open and closed, one by number, and its files;
- branches;
- the contents of the claim file at a ref;
- `git/commits/{sha}`, `git/ref/heads/{b}`, and `git/refs/heads/{b}` to delete;
- issue comments, read and post;
- `collaborators/{login}/permission`;
- an issue's label, delete;
- `compare/{base}...{sha}`;
- `branches/{b}/rename`;
- `rules/branches/{b}` and `rulesets/{id}`.

It is faithful to the transport, which the prototype's fake was not:
- **The request line is ASCII.** Before it answers, the fake encodes the request line (method, `call.selector`, protocol) as ASCII and raises `UnicodeEncodeError` as `http.client` does before it connects. The adapter puts some branch names into paths raw (adapters/github.py:270, 303, 396), and `GitHub.request` turns that error into "the answer was not JSON" (150-151). The prototype's fake parsed `full_url` and unquoted it (validation/prototype/model.py, `FakeGitHub.open` and `answer`), so it could never show F6's live failure.
- **Pagination.** List endpoints answer 100 items a page with a `Link` header naming the next page, so `GitHub.pages` and `next_link` (77-84, 157-166) run.
- **Mergeability.** A pull request's `mergeable` is null on the first read of its detail and computed on the next, so `settled` (246-255) runs.

`Fault(method, path_prefix, status, times=1)` makes chosen calls fail, on every write endpoint and on the reads that matter: a comment's POST answering 500, or 403 with the message a secondary rate limit sends (github/docs content/rest/using-the-rest-api/rate-limits-for-the-rest-api.md:112), once with a `retry-after` of 60 (`comment-rate-limited`) and on every try the client makes with none (`comment-limited-for-the-run`, and `comment-limited-then-unread`, whose read of the issue's comments after it is refused for a rate limit too), and once with a `retry-after` of 150 seconds, longer than a run waits, having written the comment, which the read after it lists late (`comment-limited-late`, F23's review), a label's DELETE or POST answering 500, the rename answering 403 and 422, and 403 for a secondary rate limit on every try the client makes (`rename-limited`), a ref's DELETE answering 422, compare answering 404, contents answering 404, the read of the branches answering 429 once (`branches-429`) and once 403 with a `retry-after` of ten seconds, the token's hour spent and ending a hundred seconds on, and a message that names no rate limit (`branches-limited-for-the-hour`; a fault's reset_after writes the hour's end by the client's clock, which the fake keeps as the model's minute, FakeGitHub.clock), and the read of a claim's push answering 429 on every try (`pushes-429-for-the-run`) and 403 for a secondary rate limit whose `retry-after` of 150 seconds is longer than a run waits (`pushes-limited-too-long`). A fault marked applied makes the write and loses its answer, a 502 after the change, as GitHub can: a comment's POST (`comment-lost` in verbs.FAULTS) and, since F15's review, a label's (`label-post-lost`), and, since F23's review, a comment's POST GitHub refuses for a rate limit (`comment-limited-late`), as it would were C4 to find it acting on a call it refuses so. Since the measurement pass's third pull request the client waits as GitHub asks for a rate limit and makes the call again, twice at most (adapters.github.rate_limited and rate_wait, README.md, What a board run costs), so a rate limit's fault answers one try of a call: `comment-rate-limited`'s comment is written in the same run after the wait and `branches-429`'s branches are read after it, and a fault refuses a call for the run only with times for every try, as `comment-limited-for-the-run`'s three, whose waits of a minute and two spend the three minutes a client waits in all, so the read `comment-limited-then-unread` refuses after it is not waited out either. The client's sleep is the fake's, which keeps each wait the client makes on the try it makes next (FakeGitHub.sleep, Call.waited) and none for a pause of the library's own, head_since's or settled's, after which a read is a read of its own. The harness judges a call the client made again after a wait by its last try (invariants.answered): a read GitHub answered in the end excuses no run that failed after it (G15), and a read of a claim's push is all its tries, read again after a pause only where its last try may tell more (G2), never after a rate limit's refusal (github.may_answer_later), whether the client waited it out or gave up at once, on a wait longer than a run waits (`pushes-limited-too-long`) or with the run's waits spent. Until the second review of the client's waits the harness folded every run of identical calls after a rate limit's refusal into one, so it took a read the library made again after the client gave up for a try, and saw a library that read a rate limit's refusal again only where the client had made all its tries. Each wait the client makes is held to the one GitHub asks for, read from the fault as GitHub's documentation reads and never from the library's rule, the longest of retry-after's seconds and a second past the hour's end, or a minute and then two where it asks for neither, and each call the client owed a wait is made again after it, none where the wait is longer than a run waits or would pass the three minutes a run waits in all (invariants.waits_as_asked, under G15), in a run that failed as in one that did not. A write the client makes again is held to once in a run by a check of the run's writes (invariants.written_once: one release comment to an issue and one overlap comment to a piece of open work), since a run a fault touched has its writes compared with nothing else, and it holds a run that failed too, and the mutant "a 5xx retried as a rate limit" shows the check bites, under `comment-lost`. A comment the run cannot write is `comment-500`'s, `comment-500-then-unread`'s and the two that outlast the client's tries.

The fake encodes several things about GitHub. Each is documented, or is assumed until a canary check settles it:
- The issues list includes pull requests: documented (github/docs src/rest/data/fpt-2022-11-28/issues.json, "List repository issues": "GitHub's REST API considers every pull request an issue").
- A draft's mergeable_state is the word its branch's standing gives, as any pull request's: settled by C0 on 2026-10-14, read by hand on two live drafts, which answered "clean" for a clean draft and "dirty" for a conflicting one, never "draft". C0 read no branch that is behind: the fake's "behind" for one is assumed, and holds under a rule that requires a branch to be up to date before merging, as the template's main and organization rulesets do (strict). "blocked" is what a rule that holds the pull request gives: a required check pending or red, as on every copy's default branch while a pull request's check runs or after it failed, or a review not yet given, as in crowd mode. So the fake answers "blocked" for every pull request not in conflict when a scenario's `opaque_pulls` is on, as for a draft whose check has not passed, with mergeable true and the comparison carrying behind_by, which the adapter reads into a standing (F9); with it off, the standing's own word. Under a rule that does not require an up-to-date branch, as the template's crowd ruleset (docs/setup/ruleset-crowd.json, strict false), or under none, GitHub calls a branch that is behind "clean", as scripts/canary.py's C2 notes, or "unstable", GitHub's word for a pull request that may merge while a check that is not required fails, which no check here has read. So the fake answers "clean" for a branch that is behind when a scenario's `lenient_rules` is on, as F16's trace and one search of the lane have it, and never "unstable", which the adapter reads as it reads "clean" (section 13, F16).
- Renaming a branch closes the open pull request whose head it is: documented (github/docs, content/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-branches-in-your-repository/renaming-a-branch.md:19: "If the renamed branch is the head branch of an open pull request, this pull request is closed").
- Deleting a branch closes the open pull requests whose head it is: the web UI's page says so of its delete step (github/docs, content/pull-requests/how-tos/commit-changes/managing-branches-within-your-repository.md:71: "If the branch is associated with at least one open pull request, deleting the branch closes the pull requests"); assumed for the REST delete the board makes (github.free_name, `DELETE /git/refs/heads/<name>`), whose reference says nothing of pull requests; C6 settles it, opening a draft from the second push of the freed name and reading its state once the board has deleted the branch. The board deletes a branch pushed again at the head a release kept, and the model's delete left its pull request open: the soak's G9 lead met it, a second run with nothing new telling the draft idle (validation/runs/2026-10-14-partial/README.md), and F17's work met it when G9 held the page's open pull requests to the oracle's; each fix closes it (model.delete_ref).
- A rename onto a name that already exists answers 422: assumed. The reference gives 422 only as "Validation failed, or the endpoint has been spammed" (github/docs src/rest/data/fpt-2022-11-28/branches.json, "Rename a branch"), and github.free_name relies on it (adapters/github.py:570-586); C6 settles it.
- The board's comments are posted as `github-actions[bot]`, with author_association NONE: assumed; C6 reads both off the bot's release comment.
- A pull request's updated_at moves with pushes and comments: assumed; C6 reads it after the draft opens, then after a comment and after a push, waiting up to a minute for each to move it, and comments at least two seconds after the first read, since GitHub keeps the value in whole seconds.
- A call GitHub refuses for a rate limit is one it did not act on: assumed. GitHub's documentation says how long to wait and when to stop asking ("Exceeding the rate limit") and nothing of whether a refused call took effect; the client makes a refused write again on this assumption (adapters.github.GitHub.request), and the fake's rate limit refuses before it writes, but for `comment-limited-late`'s (below). C4 would settle it, a burst of comments on a throwaway issue until GitHub refuses one for a secondary rate limit and then a read of the issue's comments for the refused one, and is not run, since no check trips the limit on purpose (section 11). Were GitHub to write a call it refuses, a run that met the limit would post that comment twice: the check of a run's writes (invariants.written_once) shows the library doing so, and nothing here would show GitHub doing so, short of C4 or two such comments seen on a live issue. The assumption reaches every write the client makes, not only the board's comments: were GitHub to act on a call it refuses for a rate limit, the clerk's `POST /issues` (team.open_task, through adapters.github.GitHub.create_issue) would open a task twice, and the merge gate's `PUT /pulls/N/merge` (adapters.github.GitHubWorld.merge) would ask GitHub again to merge a pull request it had merged, whose refusal the gate would report for a merge it did not make. The design stays as it is: GitHub's documentation gives a rate limit's refusal as a wait and a call made again, and a write the client gave up on fails the call as any refusal does. What follows such a failure assumes nothing since F23's review, which found the release's 4xx rule resting on this assumption unsaid: a release comment refused for a rate limit whose re-read lists none leaves the labels as the release left them, as after a server error (adapters.github.wrote_nothing), so where GitHub wrote it after all the next run counts it. `comment-limited-late` is the one fault that writes the comment it refuses, with a `retry-after` of 150 seconds, longer than a run waits, so the client makes one try and the fault puts the assumption to no write the client makes again, and F23's trace shows the rule bites at step 28 (section 13, F23).
- A 403 that names a wait in `retry-after` is a rate limit's refusal whatever its message says: assumed, by the library (adapters.github.rate_limited) and by the harness (invariants.rate_limited, and `branches-limited-for-the-hour`, whose message names no rate limit). GitHub's documentation names the header for a secondary rate limit's refusal: "If you exceed a secondary rate limit, you will receive a `403` or `429` response and an error message that indicates that you exceeded a secondary rate limit. If the `retry-after` response header is present, you should not retry your request until after that many seconds has elapsed" ("Exceeding the rate limit"); it never says that no other refusal sends the header. Were a refusal for good to send it, the client would wait as it asks and make the call again, twice at most and within the three minutes a run waits, and the refusal would then fail the call as it does now.

### 3.3 The board's run, as the workflow makes it

**One sequence.** `cli.board_live_run` runs collect, coordinate, render, writes STATE.md, then renames and posts the comments, in that order, on the wall clock. The harness has to run the same order on its own clock. Build step 3 made the extraction that keeps behaviour: two functions in cli.py that take `now`, `cli.plan_board` (collect, coordinate, render; the snapshot and the page) and `cli.act_on_board` (renames and comments, from that snapshot). `board_live_run` calls both with `datetime.now(timezone.utc)` and writes STATE.md between them; validation/cycle.py calls them with the model's clock, as `cycle.start_run` and `cycle.finish_run`, and the checker puts other verbs between them, as a workflow run of half a minute allows, when the scope's `split_board` is on (section 6.2). The alternative, copying the lines into validation/cycle.py, would have let a change of order in cli.py past the harness unseen; tests/test_validation_cycle.py requires that the cycle runs the cli's own halves. Between the halves the clock stands and no other run starts, and the run's writes are judged by the states its halves read, the first half's being the calls made by the time `plan_board` returned and the second half's the rest, whatever their kind: the first half's writes, its labels and its page against the state it started from and the state it left, as soon as it is made; the second half's against the state the run started from, less the rename of a branch that moved between the halves, which the second half rightly leaves alone (S09, spec: "a commit pushed to the branch before the rename, its holder coming back, keeps the claim"); a second run's quiet only when nothing changed between the halves. The mutation lane's twentieth mutant, `github.free_name` renaming without reading the branch again, is caught only by a search that splits the run.

**The concurrency group.** symphony-template's board.yml:83-85 puts every run in the group `board` with `cancel-in-progress: false` and no `queue`. GitHub's documentation (github/docs, data/reusables/actions/actions-group-concurrency.md) says that "by default, any existing `pending` job or workflow in the same concurrency group will be canceled and the new queued job or workflow will take its place". The model therefore queues a board run for each event, keeps at most one pending while one runs, and has a transition in which a newer event's run replaces the pending one. The push trigger at board.yml:62 has no branch filter, so a worker's push to a claude/ branch also queues a run, whose job board.yml:94 then skips; the concurrency is the workflow's, not the job's, so the skipped run enters the group and holds it for the seconds its skip takes. Whether such a skipped run still takes the pending slot is inferred, not measured: canary C2 counts the runs by event and conclusion, which can show a slot taken only in a copy whose board.yml has no `queue: max` (C2's record `board.yml queues its runs` says which); under `queue: max`, which the template's board.yml has had since F12's fix, GitHub replaces no pending run and the question is moot. As built (step 3): every verb says which of board.yml's events it fires, as GitHub names them (a push, and `create` when it made the branch; `pull_request` when the branch has an open pull request, when one is opened, reopened or merged; `issues` for a label put on or taken off and an issue closed; a merge fires `push` to main, `pull_request`, `issues` and `delete` at once; a comment fires none, and neither does marking a pull request ready, which board.yml does not take), and `RepoModel.fire` queues the runs of the events the scope lists, as GitHub's rule has it: the first run into an idle group is `queued` and starts before anything else, since GitHub cancels no run in progress and the gap between queued and in progress is seconds, so while a run is queued the only verbs offered are its starts (`verbs.enabled`); a run queued behind it is `pending`, and the newest act's runs replace whatever is pending; of several fired at once, every one is kept as a candidate for the one that starts and for the one left pending, since which GitHub picks is not known (`RepoModel.starts`, `finished`). A push to any branch but the default queues a run that holds the group and does nothing: where the scope splits runs it is kept as such a run, which the checker starts and leaves in progress (`cycle.SkippedRun`), since something can happen while it holds the group; where runs are made whole it is left out, which is exact there, since nothing happens while a run is in progress: into an idle group it runs and is gone, and behind a run it takes the pending slot, so whatever waited is cancelled. The board verb starts the run queued, or, with the group idle, the schedule's, which the checker fires when it likes; the schedule never arrives behind a run, since there it would do what any other event does, replace the run pending. The queue and the run between its halves are in the state key. The board's own writes, made with GITHUB_TOKEN, fire nothing (section 3.6). So F12's mechanism needs a run in progress, which only the split scope gives, and the commonest run in progress is the worker's own: its push to the claim branch, with the pull request marked ready, holds the group while its job is skipped, main moves behind it, the worker's next push replaces the run that waited, and the runs that follow owe nothing as built; reading the spec, the pull request is owed the word (tests/test_validation_invariants.py, the F12 case). The faults a scope names are met on each run that can start, whichever event started it.

**The board branch** is written only when the run exits 0: the step that writes it (board.yml:132-134) runs only after the render step succeeded.

### 3.4 The git world

validation/git_store.py (built in step 6) holds `GitStore`: a bare repository in a temporary directory with one clone per worker, built the way tests/test_bare_git.py (`SharedRemote`, 46-97) and tests/test_board.py (its git helpers and `ReleasedClaimReturns`, 640-765) already build them.
- Workers' verbs run real git: switch, commit, push, fetch, rebase and merge. GIT_AUTHOR_DATE and GIT_COMMITTER_DATE come from the scenario clock.
- FakeGitHub reads refs, trees, committer dates, standings and the files a branch changes from the bare repository (`for-each-ref`; one `log --format=...%ct...` per new tip for every commit it reaches, with `ls-tree -r -z` and `cat-file --batch` for each new commit's tree; `merge-tree --write-tree` for the standings, as bare_git.read_standing does at adapters/bare_git.py:280-295; `merge-base` and `diff --name-only` for the files), and makes the rename with `git branch -m` and the delete with `update-ref -d` there. A pull request from a fork (F17) is the one exception: its head is a commit the model makes on main's tip, which no ref here names and the bare repository never holds, so its standing and files are the model's rule's, in both worlds.
- Issues, labels, comments and pull requests stay in the model, since git has none.

So the git world differs from the memory world only where git decides: whether a push is taken and, at line level, whether two changes conflict. validation/prototype/gitprobe.py measured git 2.43 with worker b pushing `claude/5` after a had pushed it:
- b's plain push was refused: `! [rejected] claude/5 -> claude/5 (fetch first)`.
- b's `--force-with-lease` with no remote-tracking ref was refused: `(stale info)`.
- After `git fetch`, b's `--force-with-lease` was taken, and the remote's claim file then said `- Worker: b` (F7).

The git world is kept for S01, S05 and the conformance replays. A board run makes 14 to 29 API calls (validation/prototype/count_calls.py), about 30 to 70 ms over git, so all the scenarios in both worlds beside the checker would not fit the fast lane's budget. As built, a model attached to a store mirrors the repository: after each push the store reads the refs back with every commit they reach, under git's own sha, so the fake GitHub answers from the model as before; a copy of the model (the run in two halves, the dealing lane's looks, the oracle's state before a board run, G9's second run) is detached and answers from the mirror, since a repository cannot be copied per state, and for a branch's standing with the word git gave the attached model for that head and main, which every copy shares, else by the model's rule (model.standings; a commit made on a copy, as a look's change makes one, is no git object, and a branch at one is judged by the rule): git's word and the model's rule part where three pieces of work change one file, behind against dirty, and under lenient rules (F16) a branch behind main is owed nothing where a dirty one is owed main moved, so a copy that answered by the rule had the oracle owe, and G9's second run post, a notice git's word owes not (the review of F16's merge, and its second review for the states a run in two halves keeps); and a conflict git meets where a verb brings main in, carries work on or merges is resolved the way the memory verb builds its tree, each side's text read with `show :<stage>:<path>` in the worker's clone, so the trees agree wherever git and the model agree a conflict stands. A scenario costs 0.8 to 2.8 seconds, S05q, S05a and S05b the longest, over git on the container's 4 processors in the run of record (validation/runs/2026-10-14/deep.json, git.scenarios); the required check plays S01, S01c and S05a and the traces of F7 and F12 there, the deep lane all of them (validation/README.md, step 6).

### 3.5 The gate's worlds

MemoryWorld (adapters/memory.py) and BareGitWorld (adapters/bare_git.py) are used as they are. BareGitWorld's clock is the wall clock (adapters/bare_git.py:166-167), which the gate's lane never reads, since due_for_release is not among its acts, so it is used as it is. MemoryWorld.push overwrites a branch and never refuses (adapters/memory.py:60-64): it models what the gate sees, not the claim lock. That is why the claim lock is checked by G1 in the model and in git, and the gate's own lock by G13 in its Queue.

### 3.6 Where the worlds differ

This is the harness's own parity table.

| Behaviour | Memory | Git | GitHub |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A second claim push from main | refused: not a fast-forward | refused by git ("fetch first"), measured | refused; a simultaneous race is canary C1 |
| A lease push after a fetch, onto someone else's claim | taken | taken, measured | taken unless a ruleset covers `claude/**` (canary C7) |
| A push that builds on someone else's branch | taken (fast-forward) | taken | taken |
| Conflicts | at file level, from the merge base | git's own, at line level | GitHub's mergeability |
| Rename | ref moved; the head's open pull request closed | `git branch -m` in the bare repository; the model closes the pull request | REST rename, which closes the pull request (renaming-a-branch.md:19) |
| Rename under a ruleset over `claude/**` | an injected 403 or 422 | the same | an administrator's only (renaming-a-branch.md:31); canary C7 |
| Delete of a branch pushed again at a released head | ref gone; the head's open pull request closed | `git update-ref -d` in the bare repository; the model closes the pull request | REST delete; that it closes the pull request is assumed from the web UI's page (managing-branches-within-your-repository.md:71); canary C6 |
| Committer date | the scenario clock | GIT_COMMITTER_DATE from the clock | whatever the person pushing sets (F8) |
| When a push came (F8) | the scenario clock's minute of each push that moves a head, with the head, the kind, branch_creation, push or force_push, and the head it moved the branch from (model.pushed), and the head each branch's making set (model.made_heads), which the activity's entry of the making names; a push of the head the branch already has records nothing; a rename keeps the old name's records and a delete drops them; the faults pushes-lagging answer a read with the activity as it stood before the last push, pushes-moved with a push after the run read the branch and pushes-moved-twice with two, the newer naming the head on neither side (fake_github.Fault.answer), pushes-deleted with the branch's delete after the run read it, and pushes-500-then-moved, pushes-lagging-then-500, pushes-500-then-lagging and pushes-catching-up, whose first read is two pushes behind and whose second one, answer the first read with one fault and the second with another, a chain's links firing in their order (Fault.then, FakeGitHub.fault_for) | the same, recorded by the git world's push (GitStore.pushed) | the activity's `timestamp`, `before` and `after`, GitHub's own, as canary C5 read on 2026-10-15; that a rename keeps them is assumed, which canary C6 now records (section 11, What C6 records of the activity), and that a lagging read lists the activity before the push is assumed, which no canary check reads |
| mergeable_state | computed: clean, behind or dirty, a draft's as any pull request's; "blocked" for every one not in conflict when the scenario's `opaque_pulls` is on, with behind_by in the comparison; "clean" for one that is behind when its `lenient_rules` is on | from merge-tree | GitHub's, computed late; "clean" or "dirty" for a draft, never "draft", as C0 read on 2026-10-14; "blocked" under a rule that holds the pull request, and "behind" for a branch that is behind only under a rule that requires an up-to-date branch, "clean" under one that does not or under none, neither read by C0 (F9, F16) |
| The gate's standing of a pull request's branch | computed: clean is level with main and merging cleanly | the same | GitHub's word where it is not blocked or draft, so clean under a rule that does not ask for an up-to-date branch may be behind main; the world's standing says so (gate.Standing.level), and the gate's sentence for its clean says only that the branch merges cleanly as it stands, where git's and the memory world's say up to date (gate.standing_sentence; F16's review) |
| A pull request from a fork (F17) | its head a commit the model makes on main's tip, which no ref here names; standing and files by the model's rule | the same commit, kept by the model and never in the bare repository, judged by the model's rule | the fork's own branch, in the fork; board.yml's job skips the run it starts |
| A non-ASCII path | refused before any answer, as http.client refuses it | the same | canary C3 |
| Board runs | queued by event; at most one pending; a newer run replaces the pending one; can be split in two halves | the same | the `board` concurrency group (board.yml:83-85); canary C2 |
| A push made with GITHUB_TOKEN starts no workflow run | not modelled: board runs are queued only by the scenario's events | the same | canary C6, monthly |

The checker runs only in memory. Every counterexample, and a sample of explored paths, is replayed in git (section 6.6), so a fault in the memory model shows up as a disagreement with git.

## 4. Verbs and scripted workers

### 4.1 Atomic verbs

The verbs are GitHub's real operations, one each, never bundles. The prototype's `ready` verb deleted the claim file, pushed, marked the pull request ready and labelled the issue in-review in one step (validation/prototype/explore.py:204-211). The label then hid a mutant that released ready pull requests, since `may_be_due` checks in-review (adapters/github.py:316-322).

- **Branches:** push the claim commit; push a commit; push a commit that deletes the claim file; bring main in by a merge; bring main in by a rebase pushed with a lease; someone else moves main; after a release, push one's old head again as `claude/<n>`.
- **Labels:** put claimed on; put one's own worker label on; take another worker's label off; take claimed off; take a worker label off; label the issue in-review.
- **Comments:** the plan; "finished; ready to mark"; a release marker by the owner, by a collaborator, by a writer GitHub shows as a contributor, whom only the permission API vouches for, or by a stranger with no write access.
- **Pull requests:** open a draft; mark it ready; merge it (the owner); close the issue without a merge; the tidy workflow deletes the branch.
- **Undo a release:** put claimed back; rename `released/<n>-<sha7>` back; reopen the pull request.
- **Time:** wait 24 hours; wait 25 hours. With only 25, every gap was 0 or at least 25 hours and never sat on the threshold, so `>` against `>=` and a 12-hour idle time went unseen (validation/prototype/explore.py:152-153; section 7).
- **The board:** an event queues a run; the pending run starts; its first half; its second half; any of these with one injected fault.

### 4.2 Scripted workers

These live in validation/policies.py. A policy sees only what a worker sees: the last STATE.md, its own clone, and the comments on its own issues and pull requests. It never sees the model, so a worker that acts on a wrong board acts wrongly, and the scenario shows it. Each policy is `decide(view, rng) -> Verb | None`, deterministic, or seeded with `random.Random(seed)` in the soak runs. Before running any git command a policy issues, the harness passes it through `guard.run` (G11a), as the template's PreToolUse hook does.

| Policy | Behaviour |
| --- | --- |
| EagerClaimer | Takes a Given to workers row first, otherwise the first Queue row in its area. Claims, announces, opens a draft, commits every `pace` hours, and finishes after `k` commits: deletes the claim file, marks ready, labels in-review. |
| Racer | An EagerClaimer that acts on the same render as another worker in the same step, so both pick one issue before either pushes. |
| IdleHolder(returns_at=None) | Claims, announces, opens a draft and stops. With `returns_at`, it pushes one more commit at that time, without announcing again. |
| Abandoner | Claims and stops. One variant also opens a draft. |
| Finisher | Does the work but may not mark ready (a cloud session whose `gh pr` calls are refused). Comments `finished; ready to mark` on its draft and stops. |
| PersonByHand | At given times, posts the release marker and takes claimed and the worker labels off. Can also undo: puts claimed back, renames `released/<n>-<sha7>` back to `claude/<n>`, and reopens the pull request. |
| MainBringer(how) | On "main moved", or when its pull request is behind or conflicting: with `merge`, merges main in, resolves and pushes; with `rebase`, rebases, resolves and pushes with `--force-with-lease`. |
| Owner | Merges ready pull requests. For a Finisher, also completes the finish: deletes the claim file, marks ready, labels in-review. |
| Fault models | CarelessRebaser fetches, then pushes `--force-with-lease` onto a claim it does not hold. Stranger has no write access and posts the marker. ClockSkewed commits dated -48 h or +72 h. NonCanonical pushes `claude/0<n>`. HandMade pushes `claude/\u00b2` or `claude/caf\u00e9`. |

## 5. Scenarios and regression traces

### 5.1 How a scenario is written and run

Scenarios live in validation/scenarios.py (built in step 4), as tables of steps; S02's release step, as written there:

```
Scenario("S02", "an idle claim is released once after a day, goes back to the queue, its work is kept, "
         "and someone else claims it", ("G2", "G3", "G4", "G6", "G9", "G10"), {1: ("todo",)},
         {"a": policies.IdleHolder(), "c": None},
         (..., board(26, comments={1: {"claim-released": 1, "released": 1}}, labels={1: {"todo"}},
                     refs={"claude/1": False}, released={1: 1}, pulls=[], queue=[1],
                     mentions={1: {"claim-released": ["held by a", "2026-10-01T01:00:00Z", "#100"]}}), ...))
```

validation/run.py provides `run_scenario(scenario, known=oracle.AS_BUILT) -> RunRecord`, in the memory world; the git world comes with git_store.py (section 3.4, step 6), which adds an argument for it. The record holds each step with its verbs, their outcomes, the violations met and the expectations missed, every page the board rendered, the count of guarantee checks and a timeline a person reads. tests/test_validation_scenarios.py has one test over every scenario and one per finding a step names.

Expectations name structure: who holds what, which markers were posted, which rows the board lists. They never name whole sentences. The library's CLAUDE.md treats a rendered string as part of its interface, so when a wording changes on purpose, only the parsers follow, and they read board.py's own heading constants.

validation/known.json (built in step 5) records each result that is red on purpose, with its finding, for example, until their fixes, `{"scenario": "S05a", "step": 12, "guarantee": "G7", "finding": "F2"}` or `{"trace": "F4", "step": 9, "guarantee": "G14", "finding": "F4"}`, and names in `as_built` the findings the oracle reads as built (none since F2's fix; F2, and F9, F12, F13 and F14, until their fixes): such an entry is red with its finding's switch off and green as built, and any other (F4 and F7, until their fixes; none now) is red as built. Its third part, `fixed_after`, names each finding fixed on main with the last version that still has it (F2, F4, F7, F8, F9, F12, F13, F14, F15, F17, F18, F19 and F20: 0.4.1; F21: 0.5.0); only the live canary reads it, since it runs the release the canary repository vendors and not main (section 11), and an entry stays, true of every copy at that version. The suite passes when every result is what the file records (tests/test_validation_scenarios.py and test_validation_traces.py, which also hold each step a scenario or trace says shows a finding to an entry). A fix that turns a result green removes its entry in the same pull request, and any new red result fails the suite. Nothing is skipped.

### 5.2 The five scenarios

Times are hours from 2026-10-01T00:00Z. "board" means one board run, made the way the workflow makes it (section 3.3). After every step the state guarantees run (G1, G8, G12); after every board run the cycle guarantees run (G2, G3, G3L, G4, G5, G6, G7, G9, G10, G15); G11a runs on every command. The Expected column is what the spec says; "on cf3bdb0" marks a step the code at that commit answers otherwise.

**S01 `race`: two workers race for one issue, and a worker's label names its issue.** Guarantees: G1, G8, G9, G11a. Workers: a (EagerClaimer), b (Racer, reading the same render as a), c (EagerClaimer). Issues: #1 todo, #2 todo, #3 todo plus worker:c. Memory and git.

| # | Time | Who | Does | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 0 | board | run (schedule) | Queue: #1, #2. Given to workers: #3 (c). Claims: none. |
| 2 | 0 | a, b | read the same render; both pick #1 | - |
| 3 | 0 | a | `git switch -c claude/1 origin/main`; commit `docs/agents/claims/1.md` (Worker: a); `git push origin claude/1` | The guard passes it. The push is taken. |
| 4 | 0 | b | the same, with Worker: b | Refused. Memory: not a fast-forward. Git: `[rejected] (fetch first)`. b deletes its local branch. |
| 5 | 0 | b | takes the next Queue row from the same render (#2) and claims it | taken |

Variant S01c, under the careless fault model: b runs `git fetch origin && git push --force-with-lease origin claude/1`. Git would take it (measured), and until F7's fix the guard passed it, because a lease on any `claude/` branch was allowed: the claim file then named b, every reader named b and G1's second half broke, which known.json recorded as F7. Since the fix the guard reads the claim file on `origin/claude/1`, which names a, and the one on the branch pushed, which names b, and refuses the push (guard.other_holder); the scenario expects the refusal, and the mutant that passes the lease shows the old failure (G11a at the lease, G1 at the board).

**S02 `idle-holder`: an idle claim is released once after a day, goes back to the queue, its work is kept, and someone else claims it.** Guarantees: G2, G3, G4, G6, G9, G10. Workers: a (IdleHolder that never returns), c (EagerClaimer). Issue: #1. Memory.

| 2 | 1 | a | commits src/1.py and pushes, then stops for good | taken |
| 3 | 1 | board | run (pull_request) | Claims: #1 a. Open pull requests: #100. Queue: none. |
| 4 | 24 | board | run | Nothing posted or renamed: idle for 23 h. |
| 5 | 25 | board | run | Still nothing. Exactly 24 h is not "more than 24 hours": claims.is_idle uses `>` (claims.py:187-191). |
| 6 | 26 | board | run | The release, in six parts. (1) One release comment on #1 by `github-actions[bot]`: the marker on the first line; it names a, the head's date 2026-10-01T01:00:00Z, "its pull request #100 is still a draft", and the plan (coordinator.release_text, 111-134). (2) claimed and worker:a taken off, after the comment. (3) `claude/1` renamed to `released/1-<sha7>`. (4) #100 closed by the rename. (5) One "released:" comment naming the new branch (github.release_claim, 547-567). (6) Queue: #1. #100 gets no idle comment, because a released claim's pull request hears nothing (board.py:463-467). |
| 7 | 27 | board | run | No write call (G9). |
| 8 | 27 | c | claims #1 afresh from main, cherry-picks a's work commit from `released/1-<sha7>` after its claim commit, announces | Push taken: the name is free. Labels: claimed, todo, worker:c. |
| 9 | 27 | board | run | Claims: #1 c. No release: the head is fresh. |

Expected outcome: exactly one release comment; a's commits reachable from `released/1-<sha7>` (G4); c holds #1.

**S03 `by-hand`: a person releases too soon, a stranger tries, the holder comes back, and the label undoes a release.** Guarantees: G2, G3, G5. Workers: a (IdleHolder), b (EagerClaimer that comes back at 58), owner (PersonByHand), s (Stranger). Memory.

| 3 | 10 | board | run | Not released: the comment came 10 h after the last commit (claims.was_released, 262-272). Claims: #1 a. Queue: none. The labels are off, but the branch still holds the issue. |
| 4 | 30 | s | posts the marker comment (author_association NONE, no write access) | - |
| 5 | 30 | board | run | The coordinator posts its own release comment, because neither the owner's early comment nor the stranger's counts (claims.is_release_comment; github.may_write answers no for s, 332-350). Then the rename. Queue: #1. |

| 13 | 120 | board | run | Still no second release while b stays idle: this idle period has already been released once (claims.release_due with `released`, 194-208). |

Expected outcome: the release comments are the owner's (10 h, frees nothing), the stranger's (30 h, frees nothing), the bot's (30 h, releases a), the owner's (58 h, undone by b's commit) and the bot's (85 h, releases b). There are no others, and b holds #1 at the end.

Variant S05g runs the gate over BareGitWorld under coordbench's arm policy, in which the gate merges, main is merged in, and no check is required:
- `mark_ready(#1)` merges, exit 0.
- `mark_ready(#2)` answers main moved, exit 3. Main is unchanged, and the conflicts are named: `("app.py",)`, the one file the lane's branches change.
- r merges `team/main` in and pushes; `mark_ready(#2)` then merges.
- Main has moved forward twice, and both issues are closed.

tests/test_bare_git.py:326-365, in `GateOverBareGitTest`, covers the same flow; here it runs under the scenario's guarantees.

### 5.3 One regression trace per finding

The first design listed thirteen more scenarios, S06 to S18. None stays a scenario of its own, and each lands in one of four places:
- **A replayable trace per finding,** in validation/traces/ (built in step 5, one file per finding, F1 to F23 and F25 to F28, played by validation/replay.py and `python3 scripts/validate.py replay <file>`), each red in known.json until its fix lands (the table below), for the five that showed one: S12 (F4), S13 (F5 and F7), S14 (F6; its `claude/\u0663` is F5's, which the property lane's Unicode classes also cover, section 9.2), S15 (F1) and S16 (F8). A trace of a finding fixed in 0.4.1 is green, and tests/test_validation_traces.py shows it bites: the mutant that undoes the fix turns it red at the step and for the guarantee the table names.
- **Three table-driven tests.** S06, the gate's presets: a ready pull request waits for the owner under ACCOUNT_POLICY and lands by auto-merge on green under TEAM_POLICY, red and pending checks refuse, exit codes are checked, and three agents race under the caller's lock as tests/test_gate.py:163-186 has them do. S11, the protocol check's cases: every pull request state crossed with the claim file present or not, the body's closing forms, and a head in a fork. S10, the clerk's planning, which no other lane runs: team.route's answer to each event (an epic labelled, before and after it is planned; a task labelled blocked; the last task closing, two closing in one run, and an integration already claimed; team.py:152-185), then team.seed over a team.Client that reads and writes the model (team.py:70-111), after which each task is given to a registered worker, or goes to the open queue when none is registered, and the board rendered from the model lists every given task under Given to workers and not in the Queue (spec lines 17 and 77; team.py:316-393). Section 10.1 puts the clerk's planning under the coverage floor.
- **The checker,** through verbs (section 4.1) and situations it must reach (section 6.9). S07, a released head pushed again: the old holder pushes its head again as `claude/<n>`, and the next run deletes the name, since `released/<n>-<sha7>` already keeps that head (github.free_name, adapters/github.py:570-586). S08, a rename refused: the rename faults of section 3.2, in E-faults, with G10's "rename refused" part. S09, the holder back during a run: the board run split in two halves (section 6.2), where a commit lands between the release comment and the rename, and free_name's second read keeps the claim. S18, a writer whose author_association does not vouch: a marker by a writer GitHub shows as a contributor, which counts through the permission API (github.may_write, adapters/github.py:332-350).
- **Variants and a check,** for S17, a worker's label in its three spellings: S01's variant S01l, in which #3 carries `account:c` and then `agent:c` in place of `worker:c` and is listed under Given to workers and kept off the Queue the same way; S02's variant S02l, in which #1 also carries `account:a` and `agent:a` when the release comes, so the release must take all three off (G8); and the property lane's label sets, which hold all three spellings (section 9.2).

## 6. The exhaustive checker

### 6.1 Search

validation/explore.py provides `explore(start, scope, depth, known=oracle.AS_BUILT) -> Exploration`, the breadth-first search of the fast lane; validation/deep.py provides `search(start, scope, depth, known, budget)`, the depth-first search of the deep scopes (built in step 6). The search starts from the state it is given, the seeded state after one board run in the tests, and applies, to a copy, every verb enabled for every worker and person, and every fault the scope names. It checks each new state's guarantees and deduplicates states by their key. Breadth first, the first trace that breaks a guarantee is a shortest one.

**No pruning at a violation.** The prototype stopped exploring at every violating state (validation/prototype/explore.py:353-357), so with entries in known.json nothing past a known finding would ever be explored. The harness records the violation and goes on; each known finding is a switch in the oracle (section 2.6), so a known red neither stops the search nor hides a second fault behind it.

**Deep scopes** search depth first and keep 16-byte digests rather than whole states. A plain seen-set is incomplete for a depth-bounded search, since a state first reached deep is then never expanded when it is reached again shallower; the set therefore keeps, for each digest, the shallowest depth at which it was reached, and expands a state again when it is reached shallower. validation/shrink.py (built in step 6) shortens a failing depth-first trace by removing steps while it still fails (delta debugging: chunks first, then single steps, then windows of neighbouring steps, since a verb and the board run its event queued can only go together: the first run's three leads, met 160 to 185 verbs into their walks and left by that run's shrink at 112, 148 and 124 verbs, shorten now to 23, 38 and 42, validation/runs/2026-10-14-partial/README.md). A search a budget stops before it is done is red in the deep lane, since it has not checked its space; the lane runs with no budget by hand and under five hours a search in validate.yml, so the artifact is uploaded before the job's six run out. The budget counts from the search's first start, the checkpoint's seconds included, so a stopped search goes on under `--resume` only with a larger budget or none: resumed under the budget that stopped it, it expands nothing and is red again.

**Known findings a search meets.** A finding no switch covers is met by every search whose scope reaches it; validation/known.json's part `searches` records each such meeting by space, guarantee and the detail as a pattern, and the lane counts a violation an entry matches as known and fails when an entry matches nothing, as the traces' and scenarios' entries work. F7, the careless lease, was its one entry, met by E-faults at depth one, until the guard's fence refused the lease; the part is empty since.

### 6.2 Scope

```
Scope(workers=("a", "b"), issues=(1,), people=("owner",), faults=("label-delete-500",),
      careless=False, split_board=True, waits=(24, 25), events=("schedule", "push", "pull_request"))
```

Inside a scope, any worker may take any verb its role allows, in any state: the verbs of section 4.1, with the policies taken away.

### 6.3 The state key

The key holds everything any guarantee or the oracle reads: refs, trees, issue and pull request comments with their whole bodies, every commit that was ever a claim branch's head, the label events, the queued and pending board runs, and the faults still armed. The prototype's key (validation/prototype/model.py:145-156) keyed pull requests without their comments, so the "finished" verb was invisible and F10 never reached; it cut issue comment bodies at 60 characters; and it left out `ever_heads` and `label_events` (validation/prototype/model.py:81-82), which the work-lost and half-release checks read. Rule coverage showed the first of these: the ordering in `claims.said_finished` never ran in the prototype's runs, since every state after a "finished" comment was folded into the state before it (section 10.1). With a sound key the same scope reaches 1,979 states at depth 5 where the prototype's reached 1,599, and 41,147 at depth 7 where it reached 26,295.

### 6.4 Reductions, and why each is exact

- **Time.** Every clock rule in the library either orders instants (latest_release, said_finished, merged_rows) or compares the gap between two instants with the one constant IDLE_AFTER: claims.is_idle (claims.py:187-191), behind release_due and was_released, and board.pull_is_idle (board.py:428-432). The reduction sorts the state's instants (commit dates, comment times, pull request updates, now) and caps each gap between neighbours at 25 hours. Order is kept, so every ordering rule answers the same. Take a test of whether the gap from t_i to t_j is more than 24 hours: either no capped gap lies between them, and the difference is unchanged, or one does, and then both the old difference and the capped one exceed 24 hours. So the capped state answers every rule as the real state does, and waiting for ever reaches only finitely many states. The rendered text quotes times (release_text, the Last update column), so the key leaves rendered text out and the oracle compares structure.
- **Workers.** Workers with the same role are interchangeable, so the key is the least key over every permutation of the workers' names; "renamed in order of first appearance", the first design's reduction, is not well defined. That gives up to 2 times fewer states for 2 workers and up to 6 times fewer for 3.
- **Commits** are content-addressed over the capped times, so the same history gives the same key; `released/<n>-<sha7>` is keyed by the commit it names.
- **Board runs** run one at a time, as the concurrency group runs them; a run is split into its two halves only when the scope asks for it.

### 6.5 What is checked on each state and each run

State guarantees run on every reached state, and cycle guarantees on every board transition, given the state before and after. On every board transition the checker also:
- compares the run's effects with the oracle's effects, computed from the state before the run (the "all" line of section 2.4);
- requires that the run did not raise over a refused write or a branch name (G15);
- runs the board again from the state after a fault-free run and requires no write call (G9).

Liveness is checked as a response within one run: a due claim plus a fault-free run must give a released claim (G3), and a half-made release plus a fault-free run must give a completed one (G3L). That turns "eventually" into a safety check that needs no fairness assumption.

### 6.6 Counterexamples you can replay

A violation is written to `validation-out/traces/<guarantee>-<space>-<n>.json`, a soak walk's to `<guarantee>-soak-<seed>-<step>-<n>.json`, numbered within its walk, since one board run can show two details under one guarantee (built in step 6, in replay.py's shape, with the board run and the prefix as its first steps and a released branch named symbolically; the sketch below is the idea, the files hold the full scope and steps):

```
{"library": {"version": "0.4.0", "commit": "a996ec9"},
 "scope": {"workers": ["a", "b"], "issues": [1], "faults": ["label-delete-500"]},
 "violated": "G3L a release no rule completes: #1",
 "steps": [["claim", "a", 1], ["announce", "a", 1], ["wait", 25],
           ["board", {"fault": ["DELETE", "/issues/", 500]}], ["board"]]}
```

It is also printed as one line naming its file, which `python3 scripts/validate.py replay <file> --world memory|git` plays (the command exists since step 5; the counterexample files and the `--world` argument came with the deep lane in step 6). The replay runs the steps again, checks every guarantee and draws the timeline. Every counterexample, and 200 sampled paths, are replayed in git in the deep lane; any difference in what git and the model observe (a push taken or refused, the refs afterwards, the board's rows, the markers posted) is a fault in the memory model and is reported as one.

### 6.7 Measured on the prototype

With a sound key (validation/prototype/explore_variants.py, scopes.py and ext_verbs.py; "reduced" means time capping plus symmetry), 2 workers unless stated:

| Scope | Depth | States | Time | Reduced states | Reduced time |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 issue | 5 | 1,979 | 1.7 s | 830 | 1.0 s |
| 1 issue | 6 | 8,985 | 7.6 s | 3,494 | 3.9 s |
| 1 issue | 7 | 41,147 | 37.5 s | 14,920 | 19.5 s |
| 1 issue, board split in two halves | 7 | 56,290 | 58 s | 20,917 | 25 s |
| 3 workers, 1 issue | 6 | 13,960 | 13.6 s | 3,494 | 6.3 s |
| 2 issues | 6 | 54,343 | 56 s | 23,353 | 28.5 s |
| 1 issue, plus four verbs: main brought in by a merge, a stranger's marker, a refused comment POST, the issue closed without a merge | 6 | 22,821 | 21.6 s | 9,583 | 12.3 s |
| 1 issue, plus the 24-hour wait (mutants_wait24.py with no mutant) | 6 | 25,975 | 20 s | | |

- States grow about 4.5 times per level of depth, 5.1 times with the four extra verbs, and, by estimate, about 6 times with the full verb list and five fault kinds.
- With the four extra verbs, 116 of the board runs with a refused comment POST raised at depth 6, before the reductions (F11).
- The prototype's own lossy key, for comparison (validation/prototype/explore.py and measure.py): 1,599 states at depth 5 (2 workers, 1 issue), 26,295 at depth 7, 106,267 at depth 8, in under two minutes and about 470 MB; 10,144 for 3 workers at depth 6 in 62 MB; 41,215 for 2 issues at depth 6 in about 220 MB.
- The gate (validation/prototype/gate_explore.py, 2 agents, 2 issues, open queue, MemoryWorld, depth 7): 23,712 states and 256,592 transitions under the account and the team policies, 24,920 and 261,580 under the arm's, 21,848 and 243,020 under a gated check, about 20 s each, no violation.
- Costs: one board run over the fake makes 14 calls for two claims, a draft and a commit, and 29 when two releases are due, in about 2 ms (validation/prototype/count_calls.py); one model copy costs 0.13 ms; one git call in a bare repository costs 3.3 ms, so a board run over git costs 30 to 70 ms.

### 6.8 Scopes and budgets

| Scope | Lane | Expected time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| E-fast: 2 workers, 1 issue, the full atomic verb list, both waits, one fault kind at a time, reductions | fast | depth 5: a few seconds; depth 6: about 15 to 20 s (estimated from the growth above) |
| E-prefix: depth 3 to 4 from each scenario's interesting prefix (S02 at step 5, S03 at step 7, S04 at step 3, S05 at step 7 of the 5.2 tables; in the built scenarios' own step counts, which spell the announcements out, S02 after step 6, S03 after 12, S04 after 6 and S05 after 23) | fast | seconds each |
| E-three: 3 workers, 1 issue, depth 5 | fast | a few seconds |
| E-guard: the guard against git's recorded answers (section 8) | fast | under a second |
| E-deal: 3 workers asking the clerk (section 9.1) | fast | under a second |
| E-two: 2 workers, 2 issues, depth 7 | deep | measured in step 6: 18,787,930 states in 9,494 s (2 h 38 min), no violation |
| E-deep: 2 workers, 1 issue, depth 8, depth first with the depth-aware digest set, and shrinking | deep | measured in step 6: 16,706,180 states in 7,563 s (2 h 6 min), no violation (the estimate of 8 to 10 minutes assumed the reductions, which are not applied) |
| E-faults: every fault kind, careless workers, clock skew, hand-made names; activity-500, on the activity's record of a branch's making alone, never fires there, since one issue has no Overlaps, while activity-500-all, on every read of the activity, fires on the read of the push that set a claim's head (F8), as the thirteen faults on that read alone do, and the two on posting an issue's labels fire nowhere in the deep lane, since every issue starts with todo (validation/deep.py, above SPACES) | deep | measured in step 6: 41,415 states in 92 s (2 min), no violation |
| E-activity: 2 workers, 2 issues on one file, from both claims, the faults on the repository's activity: activity-500 on a branch's making, which the board reads only for the work in the Overlaps (F13), activity-500-all on every read, and, since F8's fix and its reviews, eight on the push that set a claim's head | deep | measured in step 6: 1,035,026 states in 1,040 s (17 min), no violation |
| E-gate: four policies, depth 8, over MemoryWorld; every part but 4 over BareGitWorld | deep | measured in step 6: 51,424 to 63,780 states a policy in memory, 40 sequences over git, no violation |
| Soak: 1,000 seeds, 200 steps each, 5 workers, 6 issues, seeded random walks | deep | measured in step 6: 1,000 walks, 582 s of walking, no violation |
| Conformance: every counterexample plus 200 sampled paths, replayed in git | deep | measured in step 6: 200 paths, 0 counterexamples, 0 disagreements, 204 s |
| Git's answers for the guard grammar | deep | measured in step 6: 699 lists, 0 differing, 45 s; 1,165 lists since F21's fix added two of git's global options, recorded in 69 s, and 1,398 since its third review added a third, recorded in 85 s (section 8) |

No deep space has a pull request from a fork (F17), lenient rules (F16), or a stranger's pull request from a claim's own branch or a login GitHub will not answer for (F19), none a task given by a label alone, where the two faults on posting an issue's labels fire (F15), and none asks the clerk, whose rules of F18 (a task given to several workers, a claim file naming nobody, a released claim's holder, a run of part of the team) the picking lane meets in the memory world alone and the deep lane only at the two deals of F18's trace over git: the four came after the run of record, and the fast lane, the mutation lane's searches and lanes and the traces of F15 to F18 meet them. Nor does any space, deep or fast, reopen an issue (the scope's reopens, F22), whose rules F22's trace meets, in memory and over git, and two of the mutation lane's searches, in memory, since F22's review, which each catches every mutant of the fix's. A deep space for each is the lane's next step.

Depth 7 with the full verb list would be about 60,000 to 90,000 reduced states, minutes rather than seconds, so the fast lane stops at depth 5 or 6 and reaches deeper behaviour from scenario prefixes. Depth 10 would be about ten million states, hours, so the deep lane stops at depth 8.

### 6.9 Situations

validation/situations.py (planned) names the states that matter, and the report lists for each scope which it reached and which no scope reached:
- an idle draft; an idle claim with no pull request;
- a finished draft gone idle; a ready pull request gone idle; a finished draft released by hand (F10);
- released and renamed; a rename refused; the holder back before the rename; a release undone; an issue claimed again;
- two claims meeting in a file; a claim meeting a pull request;
- behind main; conflicting with main; a run replaced while pending (F12);
- a released head pushed again;
- an untrusted marker; an early marker; a marker by a writer only the permission API vouches for;
- a half release (F1); a refused comment (F11);
- a hand-made claim name (F5, F6).

## 7. The mutation lane

A guarantee the harness never sees break is not shown to hold, so the harness is tested the way it tests the library. A mutant is a function that changes one rule of the library inside the harness's own process, never in the files, the way a careless change could; validation/prototype/mutants.py holds eight, and validation/mutants.py the lane's twenty at step 3, the twentieth `github.free_name` renaming without reading the branch again, twenty-five from step 4 and twenty-nine from step 5, whose four undo fixes of 0.4.1 and are caught by the regression traces. The lane's five searches are breadth-first, each from a short prefix: a released claim under every fault, careless workers on a held claim, two claims on one file, a held claim with the clock and a release by hand, and, from step 3, the holder back between the halves of a run, the only search that splits each board run and so the only one that catches the twentieth mutant. Two more came later: a stranger's marker on overlapping claims (step 4), and, with F9's fix, a draft GitHub calls blocked, the one search with the model's `opaque_pulls` on, from a claim and its draft to depth 3 (918 states), so the adapter's reading of a held branch is searched and not only traced; since the lane credits a mutant to its first catcher, the traces for both of F9's, tests/test_validation_mutants.py holds the search to them. Six more came with F15's fix, each on an issue seeded with labels other than todo, since a release puts no todo on an issue that has it. Five start from a due claim: a released task given by a label alone, under every fault to depth 2, and a released epic and a released blocker to depth 1, from the fix's review; a released blocked task to depth 1, from its second review; and a released blocker under every fault to depth 2, from its fourth review, which meets a release GitHub refuses on a blocker, the one issue a page lists with its labels while its claim is held. The sixth, a held blocker to depth 2, from its third review, claims its blocker and announces it with no wait, so a page shows a blocker held. The faulted blocker's search runs after the held blocker's, since the two blocker searches without faults are cheaper and so are credited with the mutants they were added for (F15's fifth review), and the same test holds each search to every mutant the lane credits it with. One more came with F16's answer, a draft GitHub calls clean while it is behind, the one search with the model's `lenient_rules` on, from a claim and its draft to depth 3, which a test of its own holds to the mutant "a clean word compared". One more came with F17's fix, a stranger's pull request from a fork on a due claim, the one search with the scope's forks on, from a due claim to depth 3, where sam opens a pull request from a fork whose branch bears the claim's name, as a draft or ready, and says a draft finished, and, since F17's review, the owner may move main; it comes after the drafts' searches and before the last two, which keep the mutants they were added for, and a test of its own holds it to all nine of F17's mutants, six of which the lane credits to the traces. One more came with F18's fix, for a rule F15's fifth review found no catcher of: two claims on one file, the later a blocker, from a's claim and b's on one file, the owner labelling b's issue a blocker, to depth 1, so the board's landing order is searched with a blocker in it, which lands first though it began last; it runs after the first search on one file, and a test of its own holds it to the mutant "the Overlaps landing a blocker after other work". One more came with F19's fix, a stranger's pull request from a due claim's own branch, the one search with the scope's strangers on, from a due claim to depth 3, where sam, who may not write, opens a pull request from the claim's branch itself, as a draft or ready, and says a draft finished, and the owner may move main; it runs right after the fork search, and a test of its own holds it to all nine of F19's mutants, eight of which the lane credits to the traces and the ninth, a stranger's finished draft holding a claim, to this search, since the trace's stranger marks its pull request ready. One more came with F19's review, a stranger's pull request from a due claim's own branch, GitHub refusing to say who may write, the one search that seeds the model's unanswered (Search.unanswered), the logins the fake answers the permission API 403 for on every call, as a token without the right meets: sam and dana, so sam's pull request stays the claim's and dana's release marker counts for nothing (SPEC["unanswered"]); it runs right after the first stranger search, and a test of its own holds it to the two mutants that read a refused answer the other way, which the first search, where GitHub answers, cannot tell from the library. That makes eighteen. Two lanes come after them, each wider and slower than any search. The first, from F18's fix, is the clerk's deal against Pick (dealing.picking_lane, section 9.1), 40,960 deals from one look each, in about 21 seconds on the machine that measured the 30,720 there were before F8's review at 16 (30,720 in about 11 on the laptop before that review added a claim committed ahead and a kept head, and 20,480 in about 7 before F18's review added the nameless and the released claim); it catches the two rules of the clerk F15's fifth review found no catcher of, a blocker dealt no sooner than the todos before it and an issue labelled blocked dealt, which F18's trace does not reach, and, since F8's review, the clerk reading a release by the committer date and with no head kept (github.claim_released). The second, from the fourth review: every label mix on one issue (mutants.labels_lane), a board run and a due claim's release on an issue carrying each of the 2,048 mixes of labels the domain gives (domains.label_sets, section 9.2), each step checked as a search checks one, in about 10 seconds. It comes last, since it is the slowest catcher and a mutant reaches it only past every search, and it catches what no search seeds a mix for: a section or a row that leaves out a blocker, an epic, a blocked issue or a given one, and a release that takes off a bare worker:. The mutation lane runs each mutant against those searches and requires every one to be caught: to make the searches report something the unchanged library does not. A mutant no lane catches fails the lane, unless validation/equivalent.json (planned) names it with the reason it cannot change behaviour. The lane is the acceptance test of the harness's foundations (section 14, step 2). It needs 10 to 20 mutants (step 4 brought the count to 24, for the four rules only its lanes reach, and widened the test's bound to 30), about 4 seconds each at depth 6 with reductions, and runs in validate.yml: weekly, by hand, and on every pull request that changes `validation/` or the library.

Measured on the prototype checker with a sound key, 2 workers, 1 issue, depth 6 (8,985 states) unless stated (validation/prototype/mutants.py and mutants_wait24.py). The letters are the prototype's own checks (validation/prototype/explore.py, `state_problems` and `transition_problems`): T1 a release that was not due, T2 a due claim not released, T3 the board against the oracle, T4 a second run that changed something, L1 a half release, S1 two branches for one issue, S2 work lost, S8 overlaps told again.

| Mutant | Caught | With the 24-hour wait | Why it slipped |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| idle at exactly 24 hours (`>=` for `>`) | no | yes (T1, T2) | waits of 25 hours never put a gap on the threshold |
| a 12-hour idle time | no | yes (T2) | the same |
| any comment after the last commit releases | no | yes (T2) | the same |
| the worker label left on at release | no | no | no check read the labels a release takes off, and the board-against-oracle check compared after-states only (validation/prototype/explore.py:322-326), on which any oracle agrees with the board: by spec line 17 an issue that keeps worker:a is a's alone and is left out of the Queue, whether or not the oracle's Queue calls `labels.reserved`. Only G8 and comparing the run's label DELETEs with the oracle's (section 6.5) see this mutant |
| a release ignores the label claimed | no | no | the board-against-oracle check compared after-states only, and a wrongly renamed claim is gone from both (validation/prototype/explore.py:322-326) |
| ready pull requests released | no | no | the bundled `ready` verb also labelled in-review, which `may_be_due` checks |
| an idle comment to a released claim's pull request | no | no | no check read the comments sent to a released claim's pull request |
| overlaps told on every run (2 issues, depth 5) | yes (T4) | | |

It caught 1 of 8, and 4 of 8 with the extra wait. The harness's answers are the corrections above: effects compared with the oracle's (section 6.5), both waits (section 4.1), atomic verbs (section 4.1), and G8 and G10 reading labels and comments after a release. More mutants to add, so that each guarantee has at least one: labels taken off before the comment is posted; an overlap marker read from any line; a release comment accepted from any author; `comment_once` without its marker check; "main moved" posted after any event; the rename skipped; a claim branch from a fork accepted by the protocol check; the clerk dealing without its taken set; the guard accepting `--force`. Build step 2 added the order of the labels and the comment, the release comment from any author, `comment_once` without its marker check (`has_marker`), "main moved" after any event and the rename skipped, with others (validation/README.md); the overlap marker read from any line, the fork's claim branch, the clerk and `--force` wait for worlds and verbs of later steps.

## 8. The guard grammar, with git as its oracle

### 8.1 What is generated

validation/guard_grammar.py (built in step 4) generates push commands from parts: a prefix (none, `VAR=value`, `env`, `command`, `nice`, `time`); a wrapper (none, `bash -c '...'`, an and-list, a subshell, a command substitution, and, since F25's fix, the session's here-documents, a commit message by `git commit -F - <<'EOF'` with a body Don't stop before the push and `cat <<'EOF'` with a body It's done after it); git's global options (none, `-C .`, `-C ""`, `-c push.default=current`, `--config-env user.name=HOME`, `--shallow-file x`, the empty `-C` and `--config-env` since F21's fix and `--shallow-file` since its third review, each a shape the guard read as no push until then); push options, plain, bundled and abbreviated (`--force`, `-f`, `-fu`, `--force-with-lease`, `--delete`, `--all`, `--al`, `--branches`, `--mirror`, `--mirr`, `-u`, `--no-verify`); and refspecs, the current claim branch, another worker's, the default and the board branch under every spelling (`main`, `HEAD:main`, `refs/heads/main`, `heads/main`, `+HEAD:main`, `:main`), and a branch that is no claim; and, since F26's fix, four more prefixes, each a wrapper given a long option by a start of its name (`env --uns FOO`, `env --def`, `nice --adj 5`, `timeout --sig KILL 60`), taken with the command alone, no wrapper or global option around it (guard_grammar.ABBREVIATED); and, since F28's fix, three that give env a word it reads as an assignment where the shell would not (`env 1x=2`, `env -- - A.B=1`, `nice env x-y=1`, guard_grammar.ENV_ASSIGNED) and a wrapper that gives env the command as its -S string (`env -S '...'`, guard_grammar.SPLIT), each taken with the command alone too. Each command is judged twice: by the guard as the hook runs it (guard.refused_push, from the scratch world's clone, which every directory a command names is), and by what git would do with it.

### 8.2 Git as the oracle

The first design's oracle read the meaning of each part from a table its author wrote, so it knew only the meanings its author thought of, and never generated the shapes git really accepts in F3. Git is the oracle instead. Each distinct git argument list runs in two scratch worlds whose bare remote has a pre-receive hook that records every ref update it is offered, with its kind (create, delete, fast-forward or forced), and refuses them all, so nothing ever moves. In the fresh world the clone's remote-tracking refs match the remote; in the stale world the remote's claim branch, and a branch that is no claim the clone has diverged from too, have moved behind the clone's back, where git refuses a `--force-with-lease` itself ("stale info") but still offers a plain force. The board rules are read off what git offered:
- an update of `refs/heads/main` or `refs/heads/board` is a push to the default or the board branch;
- a forced update offered in the stale world is a plain force, since a lease would have been refused there;
- a forced update offered only in the fresh world, on a ref outside `refs/heads/claude/`, is a lease outside claude/;
- more than one ref offered is a wholesale push;
- a deletion is judged by whose branch it is, which only the second fence knows (G11b).

The prefix and the wrapper change whether the guard can read a command, never what git does, so git runs once per distinct argument list. A refusal of a push git shows harmless is listed by shape; it fails the lane only for the shapes the rules name as allowed (a plain push of one's own claim branch, its lease after a rebase, and a plain push of a branch that is no claim), since the rules forbid some shapes whatever their effect: after a rebase "your own claim branch takes `git push --force-with-lease`, and nothing else ever does" (symphony-template's .claude/rules/board.md).

validation/prototype/guard_git_oracle.py tries the method on ten shapes. In both worlds git offered `-f`, `-fu` and `+claude/7` as forced updates of `claude/7`, so each is a plain force, while it offered `--force-with-lease origin claude/7` in the fresh world alone; `--al` offered `main`, since git refused `claude/7` itself as not a fast-forward; `--mirr` offered both branches and the remote-tracking refs; and `heads/main` and `HEAD:main` offered `main`. The guard passed five of the forbidden shapes (`-fu`, `+claude/7`, `--al`, `--mirr`, `heads/main`). A push cost about 36 ms, so the whole grammar's distinct argument lists, twice each, take about a minute: the deep lane runs git and writes its answers, keyed by git's version, to validation/guard_git.json (recorded in step 4), and the fast lane judges the guard against those answers in under a second, failing when the file lacks an answer for a command the grammar generates.

### 8.3 Measured, and the causes

validation/prototype/guard_grammar.py, with the hand-written oracle, judged 10,800 commands: 8,550 forbidden, 7,734 of those let through, and no false refusal, in 0.6 s. That count is four root causes multiplied by the grammar's fan-out (6 prefixes, 5 wrappers, 3 global options), and 1,596 of them are another worker's claim, which the guard cannot know by design; so the report gives causes, not counts. validation/prototype/gitflags.py showed that git 2.43 really performs four plain shapes the hand-written grammar never generated, and that the guard passes each: `git push -fu origin claude/7` made a forced update, `--al` and `--mirr` were taken as `--all` and `--mirror`, and `git push origin heads/main` pushed `main -> main`.

| Cause | Where | Shapes | Can the command show it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A `+` refspec is not counted as a force | guard.py:171 takes the `+` off and goes on | `git push origin +claude/7` | yes |
| A command is read as git only when its first word is git | guard.py:125 | `FOO=1 git push origin main`, and `env`, `command`, `nice`, `time` | yes |
| `-f`, `--force`, `--all`, `--branches` and `--mirror` are matched exactly, and every other word that starts with a dash is skipped | guard.py:150-151, 154 and 158-159 | `git push -fu origin claude/7`, `git push --al origin`, `git push --mirr origin` | yes |
| Only `refs/heads/` is taken off a destination | guard.py:174 | `git push origin heads/main` | yes |
| The command is inside a string another program runs | guard.py:14-17 leaves what it cannot read to the second fence | `bash -c '...'`, `sh -c "..."`, `xargs git push` | no: G11b |
