| The destination is another worker's claim | whose a branch is, is not in the command; for a lease the guard reads it from the claim files (F7's fix), and a plain push git refuses itself | `git push origin claude/8` | the lease: yes, by the claim files on origin and on the branch pushed; the plain push: no, G11b |
| A push is judged in the session's clone, wherever it runs | guard.run of 0.5.0 asks git in the hook's working directory alone | `git -C ../other push --force-with-lease origin claude/1`, `cd ../other && git push origin HEAD` | yes, where the directory can be told (F21's fix), after a move also where the command starts, as if no move ran (its third review), and by a relative -C after a move, or by a -C from a `~`, in the session's own clone too (its fourth); after `cd -` or `cd -P`, through a path the shell expands or one that starts with bash's `~+`, `~-` or `~N`, or with `--git-dir`, `--bare`, `GIT_DIR` or `GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND`, on the push or set before it: what only that clone shows and the session's own does not, no, G11b |
| `--config-env`'s and `--shallow-file`'s value is read as git's verb, and an empty word ends the command | guard.py of 0.5.0, GIT_VALUE_OPTIONS and simple_commands | `git --config-env user.name=HOME push origin main`, `git --shallow-file x push origin main`, `git -C '' push origin main` | yes (F21's fix; `--shallow-file` since its third review) |
| A command is read whole or not at all, a quote that never closes hiding every push in it, and a here-document's body with an ordinary line's quoting | guard.py of 0.5.0, plain_shell and shell_parts | `git commit -F - <<'EOF'`, Don't stop, `EOF`, the push, `cat <<'EOF'`, It's done, `EOF`; the push, then `echo "unclosed` | yes (F25's fix) |
| A wrapper's long option is read only by its whole name, and every other word that starts with -- as a flag | guard.py of 0.5.0, Wrapper.long_values, past_options and env_directory | `env --uns HOME git push origin main`, `nice --adj 5 git push origin main`, `timeout --sig KILL 60 git push origin main`, `time --output-file x git push origin main`, `env --ch ../other git push origin HEAD` | yes (F26's fix) |
| After env's options only a shell name's assignment is read as one, a lone - as the command, and -S's string as one word; and env's lone assignment as the shell's | guard.py of 0.5.0, ASSIGNMENT in launch, past_options and moved_to | `env 1x=2 git push origin main`, `env -- - git push origin main`, `env -S 'git push origin main'`, `env GIT_DIR=x; git -C ../other push --force-with-lease origin claude/1` | yes (F28's fix) |

guard.py:14-17 does not name `bash -c`; it says that anything the module cannot read passes, with branch protection on GitHub as the second fence.

Since F21's review the grammar holds the last row's shapes, `--shallow-file` since its third review: judged against the same recorded answers, the guard of 0.5.0 lets 11,304 of the grammar's forbidden pushes through outside `bash -c`, 3,768 written with each of `-C ""`, `--config-env` and `--shallow-file`, and the guard of the fix none of them. The grammar never moves the shell or names the repository by a variable, since every directory a command names is the scratch world's one clone, so it says nothing of which clone a push is judged in; F21's trace (section 5.3) and tests/test_guard.py hold the moves, the variables and the tildes, and F21's entry in section 13 says which pushes the guard judges where.

Since F26's fix the grammar holds the new row's shapes too, 932 commands over the same 1,398 argument lists, a prefix changing what the guard reads and never git's words: judged against the same recorded answers, the guard of 0.5.0, and main's at ff75657 after F21's fix, lets 471 of their forbidden pushes through, 157 behind each of `env --uns FOO`, `nice --adj 5` and `timeout --sig KILL 60`, whose word after the option it read as the command, and none behind `env --def`, which it read on as a flag, as getopt_long does with a value that may be given only attached; the guard of the fix lets none of them through. Every directory the grammar names is the scratch world's one clone, so which clone `env --ch` sends a push to is F26's trace's and tests/test_guard.py's to hold.

Since F25's fix the grammar holds the session's here-documents as a sixth wrapper, 8,388 commands, one for each command of the other wrappers but the abbreviated prefixes, which are taken with the command alone: 51,260 commands over the same 1,398 argument lists. Judged against the same recorded answers, the guard of 0.5.0, and main's at 73619e3 after F26's fix, lets 5,670 of their forbidden pushes through, every one, since the bodies' two apostrophes pair around the push, the command reads whole and the push is a quoted word; the guard of the fix judges each as the bare command and lets none through, 41,448 agreed in all, the 5,670 let through all inside `bash -c` (G11b) and 4,142 harmless refusals listed, 810 of them the new wrapper's, each a refusal the bare command has too. tests/test_validation_guard_grammar.py holds both counts.

Since F28's fix the grammar holds that row's shapes too, 932 commands over the same 1,398 argument lists, three prefixes that give env a word it reads as an assignment where the shell would not (`env 1x=2`, `env -- - A.B=1`, `nice env x-y=1`) and one wrapper that gives env the command as its -S string (`env -S '...'`), each with the command alone (guard_grammar.ENV_ASSIGNED, SPLIT): 52,192 commands. Judged against the same recorded answers, the guard of 0.5.0, and main's at fcbe822 before the fix, lets 628 of their forbidden pushes through, 157 behind each prefix and inside `env -S`, the word or the string read as the command env runs; the guard of the fix lets none through, 42,288 agreed in all, the 5,670 let through all inside `bash -c` (G11b) and 4,234 harmless refusals listed, 92 of them the new rows', each a refusal the bare command has too. Which clone a push after `env GIT_DIR=x` alone runs in is F28's trace's and tests/test_guard.py's to hold, since every directory the grammar names is the scratch world's one clone.

## 9. Dealing, and the property lanes

### 9.1 Dealing

validation/dealing.py (built in step 4, over validation/clerk.py's client) gives three workers' legs their moments at any interleaving of the state changes that matter (a peer's pull request falls behind, a peer claims), deals them as the workers' workflow deals since F4's fix (clerk.workflow_deals: one look for the run, no later than its earliest leg, through `team.deals`), and checks the deals with G14; the lane fails on any double deal. On the library the 54 assignments give one deal per ordering and run moment, 6 distinct, which the report counts; they are still played, since the mutant is judged on the same 54. Until the fix each leg passed its own snapshot through `team.next_for`, and fifteen of the 54 deals were doubled, which the mutant "each leg dealt from its own look" shows again. validation/prototype/probe_deal.py still shows that double deal at the first interleaving it tries, since it asks `team.next_for` for each worker from its own look, which the workflow no longer does.

Since F18's fix the lane also deals from one look at every state a person can label an issue into, and holds each deal to the spec's Pick and Planning as the oracle reads them (dealing.picking_lane, oracle.deal_problems), with each worker's own answer from the same look (team.next_for), which the spec has dealt by the same rule. The issue carries, in turn, every mix of labels domains.label_sets gives but those with in-review, which neither Pick nor Planning reads, 1,024 mixes, in three settings: alone, dealt to a and b in either order; beside a queued issue, dealt to a alone, which holds the order of Pick's places; and beside a task given to a alone, dealt to a and b in either order, which holds a task given alone before one given to several. It is held, in turn, by no claim, by a's claim or b's, each file naming its worker, by a's claim whose file has no Worker line or one naming nobody, where the issue's labels say whose it is, or by a's claim released a day and an hour after its last commit and not yet renamed, which is nobody's, and, since F8's review, by the same claim with its commit dated 72 hours ahead of its push, released a day and an hour after the push, and by a's released head pushed again as its branch an hour after the rename, not yet deleted, which the release that kept it releases (SPEC["pushed again"]): 40,960 deals in about 21 seconds (30,720 before that review, in 16 on the same machine and about 11 on the laptop), each with every worker's own answer from the same look and, in the first setting, each worker's run alone (team.deal_moves), which the team's deal binds. The oracle requires that each worker with a claim of its own, open and not labelled blocked, carry one of its own and nothing else; that one with none take only from the first of Pick's places that holds anything for it, the other workers' takes left out, a task given to it alone first, and take nothing only where Pick leaves it nothing or, its place the Queue, lets it wait for an open blocker; that a task given to several go to the first of them in the team's order with nothing else to do; and that no issue go to two. No claim in the lane has a pull request, so each has work for its holder, the one kind of claim that needs work the spec's words settle (oracle.carries); a regression trace's deal is judged the same way at a look where no claim has a pull request, F18's, and by G14's doubles alone elsewhere, F4's (dealing.pick_problems). On the clerk as built until F18's fix the lane found fifteen distinct problems, in 7,256 of the deals (twelve, in 4,888, before the review widened it): a claim carried by the worker the labels name rather than the one its file names, a claim that no label or two labels name carried by nobody, a released claim carried by its former holder, and a task the labels of two workers give dealt to neither.

### 9.2 The standard-library property lane, always on

The package takes no dependency, so its properties are plain functions in validation/properties.py (built in step 4), outside the package: the leading asserts are the preconditions and the later asserts are the property, the shape CrossHair's `--analysis_kind=asserts` reads, and plain unittest calls the same functions over enumerated inputs. Under unittest a failing precondition assert would look like a property failure, so the lane filters the inputs by the preconditions first, and fails when the filter leaves nothing to check.

validation/domains.py (built in step 4) builds the inputs:
- every string of up to 3 characters over an alphabet of ASCII digits, `0`, `/`, space, `+`, `#`, `-` and NUL, together with the classes Python's own `str` methods define: in Python 3.12.3 (Unicode 15.0.0) there are 128 code points for which `isdigit()` holds and `isdecimal()` does not, and 670 non-ASCII code points for which `isdecimal()` holds (`[c for c in map(chr, range(sys.maxunicode + 1)) if c.isdigit() and not c.isdecimal()]`, and the same with `c.isdecimal() and not c.isascii()`), enumerated whole rather than sampled;
- whole hours around the thresholds: 0, 1, 23, 24, 25, 48, 49;
- label sets made from the label names the spec gives (lines 13 to 19), in all three spellings of a worker's label, with a bare worker:, which names nobody (F15's fourth review);
- the worker names of a three-worker scope.

Enumerating those classes whole, with `0`, `1` and `+`, as one- and two-character names breaks each of the four properties CrossHair broke (section 9.3) in well under a second (validation/prototype/unicode_classes.py: 2,403 names tried in about 130 ms, after the classes take a fraction of a second to build). tests/test_validation_properties.py runs every property over this domain, deterministically, in about a second.

validation/unproven.json lists the functions no property may call because they are not pure, each with its reason: `adapters/`, `cli.py`, `guard.branches`, `guard.git_line` and `board.write_if_changed`. The lane prints the list on every run and refuses a stale entry, as beethoven-qa's gate does with its unproven table (beethoven-qa's src/beethoven_qa/gate.py, lines 29-40).

### 9.3 Which functions get properties

Line numbers are in the module the first column names.

| Function | Property | Measured |
| --- | --- | --- |
| claims.number_of (85-91) | A claim branch names one issue: the number is None, or `branch_of(number) == branch`. | CrossHair: `'00'`; `claude/00` names #0 but spells back as `claude/0`. |
| claims.by_number and issue_order (99-103, 211-214) | Never raise; numbers sort before names. | CrossHair: `'\u00b2'` raises ValueError. |
| board.render (660-683) | Never raises for any branch name; the page is ASCII. | Not confirmed at 20 s on 3-character names; at a 120 s bound on 2-character names, `'\u27800'`. |
| claims.release_due, was_released and is_idle (187-272) | Due exactly when idle and not released this period; never due twice; "more than" 24 hours. | Over whole hours, "an idle unreleased claim is due" was confirmed over all paths. "Never due twice" hit a TypeError inside CrossHair's datetime emulation, which replays cleanly in plain Python: a tool fault, so these time rules run in the enumerated lane. |
| claims.is_release_comment and starts_with_release_marker (217-241) | The marker counts only on the first line; an untrusted author never releases. | Not confirmed at 20 s. |
| claims.said_finished (244-252) | Only the author's latest comment decides. | not run yet |
| claims.released_branch (275-279) | Starts with `released/`; two heads with different short shas give two names. | not run yet |
| claims.claim_worker, claim_files, issue_files and claim_plan (106-169) | The worker line round-trips through the claim template; a claim file's list of files comes back sorted (the library keeps a path named twice, which the spec does not forbid), an issue's sorted and each once. | step 4: holds over the enumerated lane |
| labels.holder_names, reserved and worker_of (67-118) | Each name once, in order; any worker label reserves the issue. | holder_names: not confirmed at 20 s. |
| board.state_word and cell (293-302, 481-485) | Always one of the five words; a cell is one ASCII line with every pipe escaped. | state_word: confirmed over all paths, at once (it is a lookup). |
| board.overlaps and landing_key (597-646) | Each listed group has 2 or more pieces, in the spec's landing order written out (a blocker first, then work marked ready, then the lowest number), and every piece touches the file. | step 4: holds over 4,096 small worlds |
| board.released_keys and is_released (329-359) | A subset of the claim keys; released implies not claimed, not in-review, and no ready pull request of the claim's own, one from a fork being no claim's (board.claim_of, F17). | not run yet |
| board.comments_due (456-476) | Never addressed to a released claim's pull request. | not run yet |
| coordinator.release_text and labels_released (111-143) | The first line is the marker, so starts_with_release_marker accepts it; only claimed and worker labels come off. | not run yet |
| coordinator.told_numbers and overlap_comments_due (180-234) | Never tells again a subset of what was told. | the checker found F2 here |
| guard.refusal (141-193) | Refuses a `+` refspec, `-f`, `--force` and bundled short options that hold `f`, the default and board branches however spelled, wholesale pushes and their abbreviations, and a lease outside `claude/`. | CrossHair: `'\x00'`; `+\x00` passes, and so does `+claude/7`. |
| protocol_check.problems_for (48-74) | The four rules, exactly. | "A ready pull request that keeps its claim file is refused": not confirmed at 20 s. |
| team.deal (248-262) | Each issue to at most one worker; every dealt issue fits its worker's area; greedy-maximal. | Not confirmed at 20 s; no counterexample. |
| workers.pattern_regex, in_area and fits (205-243) | `fits` implies every file is in the area. | not run yet |
| gate.issue_number and main_moved_instruction (300-307, 597-609) | ASCII digits only, raising nothing but Refused; the instruction names `--force-with-lease` exactly when the policy rebases. | issue_number: not confirmed at 20 s. |

### 9.4 CrossHair, by hand

CrossHair is run by a person, from a scratch virtual environment `<venv>` outside the checkout, over the same functions; it is no dependency of this repository, needs no issue under the no-dependency rule, and no workflow runs it:

```
python3 -m venv <venv>
<venv>/bin/pip install crosshair-tool==0.0.111
<venv>/bin/crosshair check --analysis_kind=asserts --report_all --per_condition_timeout=20 validation/prototype/properties.py
```

Without `--report_all` CrossHair prints only counterexamples and errors: run on this branch, it printed four lines for the seven properties in validation/prototype/properties.py and left out the one confirmed over all paths and the two not confirmed, so a bound that proved nothing would pass in silence.

Every counterexample is replayed in plain Python before it is believed; a counterexample that does not fail on replay is a tool fault.

What the prototype's properties gave (validation/prototype/properties.py, properties_two.py and props_more.py):

| Result | How many | Which |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Confirmed over all paths | 2 | `state_word`, at once, a lookup; the idle rule over whole hours, which restates `is_idle` |
| Not confirmed at 20 s | 6 | `holder_names`, `is_release_comment`, `gate.issue_number`, `problems_for`, `deal`, `render` |
| Counterexample | 3 | `number_of` (`'00'`), `by_number` (`'\u00b2'`), the guard (`+\x00`); render's `'\u27800'` came only at a 120 s bound |
| Tool fault | 1 | the datetime release rule |

What that shows and does not:
- It finds real counterexamples in pure rules in seconds, but every one it found is a short string the enumerated Unicode classes find in milliseconds. Only finite functions, such as `landing_key` and `main_moved_instruction`, can be expected to confirm; anything that takes strings, lists or mappings comes back "not confirmed", which is a bounded search and proves nothing. beethoven-qa measured 107 of 117 conditions not confirmed at 20 s on its own package (beethoven-qa's CLAUDE.md, "Every verdict, every run").
- It says nothing about interleavings (the checker's job), git, GitHub, the wall clock or the I/O modules.
- Its datetime emulation cannot mix with the module's own `timedelta`, made at import time as IDLE_AFTER, so the time rules belong in the enumerated lane.

beethoven-qa's gate is not used as it stands: it always passes `--analysis_kind=icontract` and fails any public function that carries no icontract contracts (beethoven-qa's src/beethoven_qa/gate.py, lines 1-17 and 184-205), so pointed at this package it would fail on every function, and passing it would take icontract inside the package, against the no-dependency rule. What carries over is its discipline: every verdict printed and counted, a precondition no input can meet failing the run, the unproven list, and every counterexample replayed concretely before it is reported.

## 10. Rule coverage and the report

### 10.1 Rule coverage

validation/coverage.py (planned) records which lines of the package each lane executed, with `sys.monitoring`, which is in Python 3.12's standard library; validation/prototype/rule_coverage.py shows the method, and validation/prototype/run_coverage.py runs it over a 2-issue, depth-5 exploration with the board split (9,478 states). That reached 84% of claims, 84% of board, 91% of coordinator, 87% of labels and 63% of the adapter (the board's half of it; GitHubWorld and the clerk's calls are other lanes'), and named what the scope was missing:
- `board.pulls_behind_main` and `main_moved_text`: the scope moved main, but every board run in it got the event `schedule`, the default of `board_run` and `board_second_half` in validation/prototype/model.py, so no run had a push event to the default branch and the main-moved rule (board.py:469) never ran;
- `rename_refused_text` and `explain_refusal`: it had no rename fault;
- `may_write`'s inner `ask`: it had no untrusted author;
- the ordering in `claims.said_finished`: the lossy key folded every state after a "finished" comment into the state before it (section 6.3);
- `labels.holder_labels`, `holders`, `account_label` and `agent_label`: no label of any spelling could reach them, since no board path calls them. `holder_labels` and `account_label` are called only by tests/test_labels.py; `agent_label` is only re-exported by team.py, as `agent_of` (team.py:41), and called nowhere; and `holders` is the clerk's (team.py:123 and 242). The board reads labels through `worker_of`, `reserved`, `holder_names` and `HOLDER_PREFIXES` (board.py:522, 559 and 575; coordinator.py:85 and 143). So `holders` is the dealing lane's to enter (section 9.1), and the other three go into validation/unreached.json (planned) with that reason.

Coverage is how scopes get chosen, and it has a floor: when no lane enters some function of claims, board, coordinator, labels, gate, the board's half of the adapter, or the clerk's dealing and planning in team.py (next_for and deal; route, integration_due, owners_for and seed), the fast lane fails, unless validation/unreached.json (planned) names the function with a reason. A check that passes because it never looked is the failure to fear.

### 10.2 The report

validation/report.py (planned) writes the report as Markdown, from `python3 scripts/validate.py report validation-out/*.json --md validation-report.md` (planned). It is built last, once the lanes work, and it has these sections:
1. **Header:** the library's version and the last commit to the package, read the way scripts/vendor.py reads them (scripts/vendor.py:63-82); Python's and git's versions; the date; one line of verdicts.
2. **Guarantees:** G1 to G15, each with its status and the evidence behind it (scenarios passed, states checked, mutants caught, properties, canary checks). A contradicted guarantee names its finding.
3. **Scenarios by world:** a matrix of passed, known red (with the finding) and red.
4. **Each scenario:** its steps (time, who, verb, outcome, guarantees checked, violations), then its timeline, then the board after each run as a diff from the one before. The timeline has one lane per issue, with bars per holder and marks for release, rename, merge, overlap, "main moved", idle and finished:

   ```
   S02 idle-holder (memory)   0    6    12   18   24   30
   #1  a  claim==draft #100==================| released 26, released/1-3fc99e4
       c                                       claim 27 ====>
   ```

5. **Exhaustive search:** for each scope, states, transitions, depth, time, reductions, the violations with their shortest traces and replay commands, and the situations reached and not reached.
6. **Mutation lane:** each mutant, caught by what, or slipped.
7. **Properties:** inputs tried and failures for each property; the unproven list; and CrossHair's verdicts when a person ran it.
8. **Rule coverage:** for each module, lines reached against executable lines, and the functions no lane entered.
9. **Canary:** the checks from its last run.
10. **What it does not show:** Claude's own behaviour, since no model runs; GitHub beyond the canary's checks (the delay before mergeability is computed, a secondary rate limit, which no check trips, and outages); rulesets other than the one C7 makes; workers' own REST calls outside board runs, which are modelled as atomic verbs; forks; the template's skills and hooks other than the guard.

tests/test_validation_report.py (planned) pins the Markdown on a synthetic results file. An HTML page with the same content (inline SVG timelines, no script, no CDN, as coordbench's comparison page) is optional and comes after everything else.

## 11. The live canary

These are what no local world can show, and only these run live; facts GitHub's documentation settles are taken from it once:
- renaming a branch closes the open pull request whose head it is (github/docs renaming-a-branch.md:19); deleting one is left to C6, since the page that says the same of it speaks of the web UI's delete (managing-branches-within-your-repository.md:71) and the board makes the REST delete;
- a branch covered by a branch protection or a repository-level ruleset can be renamed only by an administrator (renaming-a-branch.md:31);
- at most one run is pending in a concurrency group, and a newer one replaces it, unless the group sets `queue: max` (github/docs data/reusables/actions/actions-group-concurrency.md), which github.com offers (github/docs data/features/actions-nga.yml);
- a secondary rate limit answers 403 or 429 with a message that says so, and a `retry-after` header when it sends one (github/docs content/rest/using-the-rest-api/rate-limits-for-the-rest-api.md:112). F11's fix is tested against the fake's injected answers of that shape that outlast the client's tries, `comment-limited-for-the-run` and `comment-limited-then-unread`, beside `comment-500`, and the client's waits against `comment-rate-limited`, `branches-429`, `branches-limited-for-the-hour`, `pushes-429-for-the-run`, `pushes-limited-too-long` and `rename-limited` (section 3.2); no check trips the limit on purpose: requests made while limited "may result in the banning of your integration" (the same page, line 114). Whether GitHub acts on a call it refuses for a rate limit the documentation does not say, and the client's making a refused write again assumes it does not (section 3.2, what the fake assumes); C4, below, is the check that would settle it. The labels a release puts back after such a refusal assume nothing of it since F23's review (section 13, F23).

**Where it runs.** The canary repository OpenSpineConsortium/symphony-protocol-canary, an internal repository the owner made from scratch for this purpose on 2026-10-13, in place of the old test repository sdlc-template-test the first design named, which is to be deleted. A new public repository made from the template would put the template's files in public, which the template being internal rules out. It starts empty and is brought to the current template by an adoption through the front door (OpenSpineConsortium/symphony-start: Symphony Setup installed on it alone, one press on the form, with "Do not share" ticked so its throwaway claims never enter the field metrics), which also gives it the settings the checks count on, a head branch deleted on merge among them. It then takes each release of the library through its own protocol.yml, so the canary after a release checks the release on the live service. Until it is adopted, the driver stops at once, saying so in the run's summary, and opens no issue.

**The driver.** `.github/workflows/canary.yml` and `scripts/canary.py` (built by step 7), in this repository, use an App token minted for that one repository with the sync's `SDLC_APP_ID` and `SDLC_APP_PRIVATE_KEY` (`actions/create-github-app-token@v1` with `repositories: symphony-protocol-canary`, as .github/workflows/ci.yml mints one for a consumer), with contents, issues and pull requests write, which SymphonyBoard's manifest grants (symphony-app's manifest.json), and checks read, which the App has for its own check run: C2 waits for the required check on the two pull requests it opens before it merges one, since the merge needs the check on the one merged, and the other must have passed it: GitHub reads a pull request whose required check failed or is pending as blocked, which hides how it stands to main, so when that check has not passed within the budget C2 records the miss as its own and does not judge the word. The draft C2 leaves behind main beside it is not waited for: since F9's fix a word that says a rule holds a pull request is read through the comparison. C2 and C6 also read the runs, which takes actions read (github/docs src/rest/data/fpt-2022-11-28/actions.json, "List workflow runs for a repository"), and that manifest grants no Actions permission: the workflow first asks for a token with it and, refused, asks again without, and the driver then records the runs as not read rather than failing, until the owner adds Actions read to the App. No check dispatches a workflow, which takes Actions write ("Create a workflow dispatch event", in the same file); each starts its board run with an event the token can make. A push made with that token starts the canary repository's own workflows, as a real worker's push does (README.md, "How a change reaches the other repositories", step 3). Git is given the token through a credential helper that reads it from the environment, so it is in no argument, no file and no message. The driver polls the branch board's STATE.md every 10 seconds, for at most 5 minutes per expectation; the required check on the pull requests C2 opens, which runs the template's whole suite, gets 15, and the run the push to main started gets 10 to finish, since the merge's runs wait their turn in the group; C2 and C6 ask for the runs created from a minute before the merge, the push or, for a claim an earlier run held, the label's removal, since GitHub filters them by its own clock to the second and the runner's may be a few seconds ahead. An installation token lives an hour from its mint, so the driver stops its checks 55 minutes after it starts (TOKEN_LIFE in scripts/canary.py), records the check it was in as failed and the rest as not run, and cleans up while the token still works; a 401 stops the checks that follow in the same way, since every later call would be refused too, and the job's timeout is 75 minutes. Each check pushes a scripted worker's claims on throwaway issues the run closes at its end, whose closing starts the template's tidy workflow, which deletes their branches, but for the one claim C6 or C7 holds in review for a later run (below). The checks read GitHub and git through the methods of one object, so tests/test_canary.py drives every check end to end against a fake with the same methods, and runs the git side for real against a bare repository, the race included.

| Check | What the driver does | What it settles |
| --- | --- | --- |
| C0, done on 2026-10-14 | one REST GET on an open draft pull request, read by hand on two live drafts of this repository | the `mergeable` and `mergeable_state` GitHub reports for a draft (F9): "clean" for a clean draft (#23 while it was one) and "dirty" for a conflicting one (#21), never "draft", though GraphQL marks `MergeStateStatus.DRAFT` only deprecated (github/docs src/graphql/data/fpt/schema.docs.graphql:26531-26536), so the REST answer was not known before; "blocked" is what a rule that holds the pull request gives, as a required check does while it is pending or red, which every copy of the template has on its default branch. F9 is fixed from it: the adapter reads either opaque word through mergeable and one compare (section 13) |
| C1 the race | two clones push `claude/N` from main at the same instant | that exactly one push is taken; the first design's check pushed one after the other, which a local bare repository already shows |
| C2 the concurrency group | opens two pull requests from throwaway claims and waits for the required check on both, then merges one, which fires pull_request closed, a push to main, issues closed and a branch deletion at once, beside a worker's push to a third throwaway claim that has no pull request, so the four events meet one skipped run and nothing but the merge touches the pull request left behind; counts the runs by event, the cancelled ones included | whether the pull request left behind hears "main moved" for main's new commit, judged by the state as the library tells it since F12's fix: the marker for that commit on the pull request within the budget, whichever run posts it, judged only once the check on that pull request passed and only where GitHub's word for it after the merge says the spec owes it, not where it is clean, unstable or has_hooks (F16); whether a draft left behind with it, whose check is not waited for, hears it too, judged the same way and read as known for F9, so GitHub's word for a draft that is behind, which C0 never read, is recorded; whether the push to main's run, read once it has finished, survives the concurrency group, which the copies' board.yml keeps with `queue: max`, F12's other half, recorded beside whether the canary's own board.yml has that line and never read as known, since that line is the template's and no release of the library changes it; how GitHub stands the pull request left behind to main after the merge; and, in a copy whose board.yml has no `queue: max`, which the record `board.yml queues its runs` marks, whether a skipped run took the pending slot, read off the counts (under `queue: max` nothing is replaced and the question is moot) |
| C3 a non-ASCII name | pushes `claude/\u00b2` and `claude/caf\u00e9` on a throwaway issue | whether GitHub takes the names, and what the board run does with them (F6, live) |
| C5 the activity API | after a lease push onto a throwaway claim, reads `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/activity` | whether it lists the `force_push` with its `actor`, `timestamp`, `before` and `after`, as GitHub's REST description says (the options for F7 and F8) |
| C6 a release end to end, monthly | on a throwaway issue N: pushes a claim, announces it, opens a draft and comments on it, then pushes one more commit whose committer date is 25 hours back (GIT_COMMITTER_DATE), whose pull_request synchronize event (board.yml:66) starts a board run, and opens a mark, a second throwaway issue labelled todo, whose run reads the repository after the push; a copy before F8's fix releases N at once and the release is followed in this run, while on a copy with the fix N is held in review for the next run (below), and the release followed is that of the claim the run before held, once at least 25 hours old, which this run takes in-review off; whichever claim is released, on its issue R, pushes `claude/R` again at the released head and opens a draft from it; and reads the newest entries of the repository's activity for `released/R-<sha7>` and `claude/R` once the rename is seen, and for `claude/R` after that second push and after the wait for its delete (below) | that the board does not release at once a claim whose commit is dated 25 hours back, since a claim is idle by when GitHub received its push, judged once the board lists the mark with N's claim still on it or N's release comment is seen, and read as known for F8 while the canary vendors 0.4.1; that the draft's updated_at moved with the comment and with N's push; that a claim held in review waited unreleased, as a finished claim waits however long it takes; then, of R's release, a release comment by `github-actions[bot]` with the marker first, and the author_association GitHub gives it (the fake gives NONE); the labels off; the rename to `released/R-<sha7>`; the draft closed; one "released:" comment; R back in the Queue; that the board's own pushes start no board run; and that the run the second push of `claude/R` starts deletes it, its rename answering 422, and that the delete closes the draft opened from it. When the rename does not come, the note says whether the board said GitHub refused it, and the draft's closing, the "released:" comment and the second push, which depend on the rename, are not asked; and, recorded and never judged, what GitHub's activity lists after a rename and after a push of the name it freed, which the harness's model assumes (below) |
| C7 rulesets, by hand | adds a repository ruleset on `refs/heads/claude/**` with only non_fast_forward, then pushes a throwaway claim, a lease push onto it, and C6's backdated push and mark; the claim released under the ruleset is that one on a copy before F8's fix, and on a copy with it the claim an earlier C7 run held, once at least 25 hours old, the new one held in its place | a lease push onto a claim is refused; the backdated push is not released at once, as in C6 (F8); the board's rename of a released claim is refused, 403 or 422; one "rename refused" comment names the ruleset; after the ruleset is removed the next run renames (F7's trade-off) |

C6's backdated commit comes last on purpose: the claim's own push fires `create` (board.yml:63), whose run would release a claim already 25 hours idle before the draft it is to close was open.

**What C6 records of the activity.** The board dates a claim's push by the newest entry of its branch's activity (F8) and the Overlaps date work by its branch's making (F13), and the harness's model assumes three things of GitHub's activity there that no other check shows: that a rename carries the branch's entries, its making and its pushes, over to the new name, lists no entry of its own, and leaves the freed name none (model.rename_ref), so a claim renamed back, as a person does to undo a release, is dated by its last push and ordered by its first, where an entry dated by the rename would hold it a day from the rename; that a push making a name a rename freed is listed as that name's making alone, with no entry of the rename before it (model.pushed_now); and that the REST delete of a claim branch is listed as a branch_deletion whose before names the head it took away (model.delete_ref, as the fake lists it once a push makes the branch again). So C6 reads, in one call each (`GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/activity?ref=<ref>&direction=desc&per_page=6`, every kind), the newest six entries of `released/R-<sha7>` and of `claude/R` once the rename is seen, after the Queue's wait and before the second push; of `claude/R` again after that push, once its draft is open, which is before the run that deletes it where the timing allows; and of `claude/R` once more after the wait for that delete, whether the delete came or not. It keeps of each entry its activity_type, timestamp, before, after, ref and the actor's login, with when it read them, as the values `activity after the rename`, `activity after the second push` and `activity after the wait for the delete`, and a note says which assumption each settles (canary.ACTIVITY_SETTLES). These are observations, not expectations: GitHub's behaviour is what is being learned, so nothing GitHub answers fails the check, a refused read is a note and its words stand in the record in place of the entries, and only a 401 stops the check, as it stops every wait, since the token itself is refused. They cost four reads a run and no wait. C6 makes the forward rename, which the board does; a rename back is GitHub's same rename with the names swapped, so what the forward rename lists under the name it makes is read as what a rename back would list under the claim's name. A record that shows GitHub otherwise is the model's to follow, its rename, push or delete and the fake's activity with it, and F8's and F13's traces then say what the change moves.

**A claim held from one run to the next.** Since F8's fix a claim is idle by when GitHub received its push, so a backdated commit no longer has its claim released at once, and no run can wait out the spec's day, since its token lives an hour (TOKEN_LIFE). The backdated push stays, as F8's own live check, and the release by idleness is followed across two runs of the same check. The judgment waits for a board run that read the repository after the push: the mark, an issue labelled todo opened after the push, is its event; the board reads the open issues before the branches (adapters/github.py, collect, as 0.4.1's does), so a page that lists the mark read the claim's branch after the push, and since the coordinator releases before the board is rendered, a page that lists the mark in the Queue with the claim still on it says that run released nothing; the release comment says the opposite. A pull request's edit would start no board run, since board.yml listens for a pull request opened, synchronized, reopened or closed only. A run that sees the claim not released labels its issue in-review, which the coordinator never releases (spec/PROTOCOL.md, Release: an issue labelled in-review waits for review however long it takes), and leaves that issue and its draft out of its clean-up; the sweep at the start of every run leaves a canary issue labelled in-review standing too, with the pull requests from its branch (canary.is_held). The next run of the same check at least 25 hours after that issue was made (HELD_FOR: the spec's day, the 55 minutes of the run in which the push came, and five minutes for the clocks' skew) expects the held claim to have waited unreleased, its branch standing, its draft open, the label claimed on and no release comment; takes in-review off, whose board run is to release it; follows that release as C6 or C7 always did; closes it with the run; and holds its own fresh claim in its place. A run sooner than that leaves the held claim standing and holds no other, and one found released while it was held fails the check and is closed with the run. Nothing that can fail stands between holding the fresh claim and taking in-review off the held one: C6 makes the clone that pushes the held claim's head again before it holds the fresh claim, so a clone that fails leaves the held claim for the next run and closes the fresh one with this run, and C7 makes no call between the two. A run stopped in that moment all the same, as a cancelled job is, leaves two held, and the next run of the check takes the oldest as its held claim and closes the others with the run, issue and draft (canary.close_extras), where following the oldest alone would hold one more in its place and keep two for good. So at most one claim per check is held once a run of it ends, and a run stopped between the two leaves one more only until the next. On a copy before the fix, as the canary's 0.4.1 is, the fresh claim is released at once, F8's expectation misses as known, the release is followed in the same run and nothing is held, so the checks see what they saw before, with F8's miss beside it marking C6 and C7 known rather than passed; the first run on a release that carries the fix holds a claim and follows none, and each later one follows the claim the one before held. The monthly schedule stays, since a month is more than 25 hours, and by hand two runs at least 25 hours apart do the same. GitHub's own permission data (github/docs, src/github-apps/data/fpt-2022-11-28/server-to-server-permissions.json, entry list-repository-activities: contents read, server-to-server true) says an installation token such as GITHUB_TOKEN with contents read may read the repository's activity, so the board's own token reads the push times C5 measured with the App's token.

C4 is not used. A burst of comments that trips the limit would learn one thing the documentation does not say, whether GitHub acts on a call it refuses for a rate limit, which the client's making a refused write again assumes it does not (section 3.2), and of which, since F23's review, the labels a release puts back assume nothing: the comments posted one after another on a throwaway issue until GitHub refuses one for a secondary rate limit, the driver stopping there, and the issue's comments then read for the refused one. But it would trip the limit on purpose, which the documentation warns against, would spend the installation token of SymphonyBoard, on which the sync and the front door depend, and nothing shows it would test the board run, which posts with the test repository's own GITHUB_TOKEN (symphony-template's board.yml:127-128), not with that token. The other numbers stay as the findings cite them.

C7 needs Administration on the canary repository, so it runs by hand, from a clone, with the owner's own sign-in (`CANARY_ADMIN_TOKEN="$(gh auth token)" CANARY_TOKEN=... python3 scripts/canary.py run --checks C7`), or with a token of Symphony Setup installed on that repository alone; never with SymphonyBoard, which by decision holds Administration on no repository. The driver makes the ruleset, named `canary C7`, and deletes it when the check ends, whatever the check met; a run killed in between leaves it, and then the next by-hand run with an administrator's token sweeps it, or a person deletes it under the canary repository's Settings, Rules, Rulesets. Until then the board's rename of every claim C6 releases is refused, which C6's note names. A run takes the canary repository alone, by hand too, unless `--any-repository` says otherwise on purpose, since the checks merge into the default branch and close the issues and pull requests titled "canary ".

**Output.** The driver writes `canary.json`, with each check, its expectations and whether each held, the values it saw and the library version the canary repository vendors (its tools/VENDORED), and a job summary. An expectation that fails for a finding validation/known.json still lists as built, or lists in `fixed_after` as fixed on main after the version the canary repository vendors (C2's "main moved", F12, while the canary vendors 0.4.1), is recorded as known, not failed, so the run goes red on the day something new breaks and not every month until the owner's decision lands or the release carrying the fix reaches the canary. The canary runs that release and never main, so a fix counts for it only once a release carries it: a fix adds its finding to `fixed_after` with the version main stated just before the fix merged, and takes it out of `as_built` when it was there; the records mark each missed expectation known or not and name the findings the run read as known, the summary says why each is known (as built, or fixed after a version), a check that missed a known expectation and a new one is failed, with the known line marked as such, and the canary holds the expectation from the first release after that version. Decided 2026-10-14, in place of running C2 only after the release: every check runs from the first run, so nothing of C2's other expectations waits and no one has to remember to turn it on, and the word is judged by the state, as the fixed library tells it. On a failure the workflow opens one issue in this repository, "Canary failed: \<checks\>", and updates that issue on later failures rather than opening another.

**How often.** Monthly (C1, C2, C3, C5 and C6) and by hand, with the checks named; C0 was read by hand once, on 2026-10-14, and is read again only when the owner asks. The first design ran C6 once too; it runs monthly, since it is the release end to end, the one check that shows live that an idle claim is never left held and no work is orphaned, which the owner put first on 2026-10-13, and a throwaway issue a month costs nothing. On a copy with F8's fix each monthly C6 follows the release of the claim the month before held, and C7, by hand, takes two runs at least 25 hours apart, the first to hold its claim and the second to release it under the ruleset.

## 12. Where it lives, how long it runs, and how it guards every change

The files the harness adds; those the steps built so far (section 14 says which) are on main, the rest planned:

```
symphony-protocol/
  sdlc_protocol/          unchanged, but for the board run's two halves in cli.py (section 3.3), built by step 3
  validation/             never vendored: scripts/vendor.py copies only sdlc_protocol/ (scripts/vendor.py:151-156)
    README.md  DESIGN.md  prototype/            on this branch
    GUARANTEES.md  known.json  unproven.json  unreached.json  equivalent.json  guard_git.json  traces/
    __init__.py  model.py  fake_github.py  git_store.py  cycle.py  verbs.py  policies.py  page.py
    oracle.py  invariants.py  situations.py  scenarios.py  run.py  replay.py  known.py  clerk.py
    explore.py  shrink.py  mutants.py  guard_grammar.py  dealing.py
    deep.py  soak.py  gate_search.py  lanes.py            the deep lane (step 6)
    runs/                                                  the deep lane's kept runs, each with a README
    properties.py  domains.py  coverage.py  report.py
  scripts/validate.py     fast | deep | mutants | scenarios | traces | explore | guard | deal | properties | replay | report
  scripts/canary.py
  tests/test_validation_<module>.py, tests/test_validate.py, tests/test_canary.py
  .github/workflows/validate.yml, canary.yml
  validation-out/         what the lanes write (traces, results, the report): uploaded as an artifact, added to .gitignore
```

Every file is ASCII with LF endings and a final newline; a hostile character appears only as a `\u` escape, because scripts/check.sh rejects anything else (scripts/check.sh:13-35). ECOSYSTEM.md is unaffected, because the ecosystem scripts read only the package (scripts/ecosystem.py:25).

| Lane | Command | Where | When | Budget |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| fast | `python3 -m unittest discover -s tests`, which picks up tests/test_validation_*.py | the job named "check" (.github/workflows/ci.yml:40-50) | every pull request and every push to main | at most 60 s added to the suite, which ran 661 tests in 35 s when this was designed, and took 35 to 44 s in three measurements on 2026-10-13 |
| mutation | `python3 -m validation.mutants` | validate.yml | weekly, by hand, and on every pull request that changes `validation/`, the library or the harness's tests | about 100 seconds |
| deep | `python3 scripts/validate.py deep --out validation-out` | validate.yml | weekly, by hand, and on a pull request labelled `deep validation` | hours: no budget by hand, where the searches run to the end (section 14, step 6, has the measured run), and five hours a search in validate.yml under the job's six |
| CrossHair | `crosshair check --analysis_kind=asserts --report_all ...` (section 9.4) | a person's own scratch environment | by hand, after a rule changes | 1 to 5 minutes |
| canary | `python3 scripts/canary.py run` | canary.yml | monthly, and by hand | 15 to 40 minutes, most of it waiting for board runs and the canary's own check |
| report | `python3 scripts/validate.py report ...` | each lane above, uploaded with `actions/upload-artifact@v4` | with each lane | seconds |

How it guards every future change:
1. **The fast lane is part of the required check.** A pull request to the library runs it inside "check", so a rule change that breaks a guarantee goes red with the shortest trace in the log.
2. **Exceptions stay visible.** A fix removes its entry from known.json in the same pull request. A spec edit that drops or rewords a guarantee breaks tests/test_validation_guarantees.py until the oracle and validation/GUARANTEES.md follow.
3. **Coverage has a floor** (section 10.1), and **the mutation lane keeps the harness honest**: a change to the harness that stops it catching a mutant fails that lane.
4. **The harness grows without sync noise.** A commit that changes only `validation/` changes no consumer's record, since scripts/vendor.py records the last commit that changed `sdlc_protocol/` (scripts/vendor.py:71-82), so no sync pull request follows.

No step is added to the downstream jobs. A partial copy is already covered by scripts/ecosystem.py's `missing_imports` and `copy_problems`, by `vendoring.py verify`, and by each consumer's own check, which those jobs run.

## 13. Findings

Every reproduction runs from the repository's root, on the library as the checkout holds it; the outputs quoted are from cf3bdb0.

**F1. A release whose label removal failed is never completed.** Status: fixed in 0.4.1 (#11).
- **What happens.** github.release_one posts the comment first (adapters/github.py:494), then takes the labels off (497-502). When the removal raises, it returns a line and never calls after_release (503).
- **Why it never recovers.** On every later run coordinator.releases_due skips the claim as already released, since claims.release_due counts a trusted comment that came more than a day after the last commit as a release (claims.py:194-208, 262-272). Meanwhile board.is_released refuses the claim, because claimed is still on (board.py:355).
- **Evidence.** `python3 validation/prototype/probe_coordinator.py` prints `releases_due = [] | released_keys = set()` on the next run and again thirty days on. The checker's shortest trace (`python3 validation/prototype/explore.py --depth 5 --all`) is claim a 1, announce a 1, wait, board with the label DELETE answering 500, board.
- **Effect.** The issue says the claim was released, while the board shows it held and the Queue leaves it out. Only a person taking the label off, or the holder committing and going idle for another day, moves it. GitHub's issue timeline would tell a failed removal from an undo (an undo shows a person's "labeled claimed" event after the comment), but the board does not read the timeline.

**F2. Overlapping work is told again when a claim opens its pull request.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every item, the choice recorded on 2026-10-14 as the issue staying the work's identity for the whole claim, in symphony-template's docs/checkpoints/2026-10-14/coordinator-checkpoint.md): a claim and the pull requests from its branch are one piece, named by its issue in the overlap markers and told on that issue, a marker on the claim's own open pull request counting as one on the issue, so opening a pull request tells nothing again; an issue's number names each claim on it in turn, so a marker counts for a claim only from the push that made its branch; a pull request that is no claim's is a piece of its own, named by its number and told on itself.
- **What happened.** tell_overlaps read what had been told from the comments on the work's own number (adapters/github.py:513-517 at cf3bdb0). A claim's work was numbered by its issue until it opened a pull request, and by the pull request after that (board.open_work, board.py:624-635 at cf3bdb0), and the pull request had no markers yet. The marker also named the other piece by the number it had at the time (coordinator.py:229-233 at cf3bdb0).
- **Evidence.** `python3 validation/prototype/probe_coordinator.py`: run 1 tells #7 and #12 of each other; once #7's claim opens #13, run 2 tells #12 and #13 of each other, the same two pieces of work. The checker's shortest trace (`python3 validation/prototype/explore.py --issues 2 --depth 6 --all`) is claim a 1, claim a 2, board, draft a 1, board.
- **Against the spec.** Line 73 says the coordinator "tells it again only when work it has not been told of starts to meet it". This is noise, not a safety problem.
- **The fix.** board.Work carries the issue a claim's work is for (`issue`, None for a pull request that is no claim's), and board.identity names each piece by it: the issue for a claim and the pull request later opened from its branch, which are one piece, and the pull request's own number for one from a fork, from a stranger or from a branch that is no claim. coordinator.meeting keys the work by identity, coordinator.overlap_comments_due names the work met by identity in the marker and owes the comment to the identity, and github.tell_overlaps reads the markers there and posts there: a claim's work is told on its issue for the whole claim, so opening the pull request tells nothing again (since the review, a marker on the claim's own open pull request counts too, and a marker counts for a claim only from its push on: The review, below), and a pull request that is no claim's is told on itself, as before. The numbers a marker names are read as the identities of the pieces shown by them now (coordinator.identities), so a marker on an issue written before the fix, which named a claim's pull request, names the claim and tells nothing again at the upgrade, unless that pull request is closed by then. The comment's first line says whose work it is, "this claim", "this claim's pull request #100" (since the review, "this claim's pull requests #100 and #101" for two) or "this pull request", a rendered string changed on purpose; the list of the work met and the landing order name the numbers the board shows, as before. The spec's Overlaps paragraph says so, the oracle reads it with no switch (SPEC["one piece"]: oracle.Piece.identity, oracle.told_of reading the identity's comments, and since the review the claim's own open pull requests' from the claim's push on, oracle.overlap_effects owing the comment to the identity), and the switch F2 is gone: known.json's as_built is empty, its four red entries went with the fix, and fixed_after names F2 at 0.4.1.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F2.json: a claims #1 and b #2 on one file; a board run tells each of the other on its issue; a opens its draft #100; the next run, which shows the work as #2 and #100, tells nothing. Green since the fix, and red for G7 at step 4 under the mutant "a piece of work named by the number it is shown by" (board.identity giving the number), which names the work by #100 and tells #2 of it and it of #2, as the library did (validation/mutants.py, 93 in all since the second round; the lane credits it to the regression traces). S05a, S05b and S05q, whose hour-1 board run showed F2, are green with no step that shows a finding, their comments on the issues now, #2's overlap comment standing through the merge while #101 hears "main moved" alone. tests/test_coordinator.py `Overlaps`: the meeting by identity, the markers and the comments on the identity, a marker naming a claim's pull request read as naming the claim, a pull request that is no claim's told on itself, and the text's first line; tests/test_github.py: the comments read and posted on the issue, issue 13's marker from before the fix naming #40 read as claim 12, a stranger's marker and a refused read there; tests/test_board.py: the meeting's keys; tests/test_validation_invariants.py: F2's steps green and red under the mutant, and a run in two halves owing the overlaps a fork's pull request opened between them brings, where a draft the holder opens between them owes none now; tests/test_validation_traces.py: F2 among the fixed findings, undone by its mutant at step 4, and nothing read as built; tests/test_canary.py: F2 fixed after 0.4.1 and a canary vendoring 0.5.0 knowing no finding. F17's trace expects the claim told on its issue and #101 on itself.
- **What it costs.** tell_overlaps reads one comment list per piece that meets work, as before, on the identity in place of the number shown, and, since the review, one more for each of a claim's own open pull requests, only where the issue's markers leave the claim something to tell, that is where a comment would otherwise go out: so the upgrade repeats nothing, a claim told on its pull request under the old reading counting as told (coordinator.told_places). Such a claim is never told on its issue, so that read recurs on every run until its overlap ends (the second round, below); a claim told since the fix costs nothing more. A marker on the issue that names the pull request counts (coordinator.identities), and only one naming a pull request closed since does not; a claim whose pull request told under the old reading was closed and another opened is told once more, since a closed pull request's comments are not read.
- **What it leaves.** The comment moves: a claim's work is told on its issue, where its plan and a release's comment are, and no longer on its pull request, where "main moved" and "idle" stay; a reader watching the pull request alone sees the Overlaps on the board. A claim whose pull request is closed and another opened is one piece still, told once, where the old reading told the new pull request again. The scripted workers and the scopes are unchanged, since the verbs tell nothing themselves. A claim on a released issue whose push GitHub will not date takes every marker on the issue, so it is told nothing the claim before it was told, as every claim was before the review; its draft dates its place in the order (board.pull_began) but not its push, which only the activity dates, so where the token may never read the activity the rule is off for every claim, as the spec says (the second round, below). The time rule assumes, as F13's order does, that GitHub keeps a renamed branch's creation under its new name; were a rename back dated as a new creation, or a branch deleted and restored by hand, the claim would be told once more, and its partners of it, once for each such creation; the fake lists only branches that stand and carries a rename's creation over, so no lane sees it; canary C6 now records the activity after a rename, which the owner's next run of it settles (section 11, What C6 records of the activity). In the model, minutes, a marker the minute a claim's branch is made counts for it, which a release and a claim anew in the minute of the telling could make wrong, and GitHub's seconds make unlikely. Two pull requests from one branch, which GitHub allows to two bases, are reached by the unit tests alone, since the model opens one pull request between two branches and every one to main. The markers on a claim's own pull request are judged by F2's trace alone, at step 15, since the library posts only on identities now and the scopes give the hand marker to sam alone, on issues. A stranger's pull request from a claim's branch that is the claim's own in one run, GitHub refusing to say whether its author may write, and a piece of its own in the next, is told twice, once under each identity (F19's, outside this fix). The template's docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the spec's sentences at the sync.
- **The review.** Two independent passes at b55b083. The library's: (1) the fix read a claim's markers on its issue alone, where the coordinator had told it on its pull request since it had one, so every open claim with a pull request that met work was told once more at the upgrade, which the fix's own words and the commit message said did not happen; by the owner's rule of correctness over cost, a marker on the claim's own open pull request counts as one on the issue (the spec says so), read only where the issue's markers leave something to tell (coordinator.told_places, github.markers_on), and the oracle reads the same places with no condition (oracle.told_of). (2) Two open pull requests from one claim branch, which GitHub allows to two bases, are one piece: coordinator.meeting named a shared file once per pull request and the marker the piece twice, and the comment spoke of one of the two and left the other out of the order; a file is named once, the marker names each piece once, and the comment speaks of both pull requests and lands both (coordinator.this_work), as the oracle does by identity. (3) The issue's number named a claim "for as long as the claim lives", but its markers outlived the claim: a claim released and claimed anew took the markers that told of the claim before it as its own, so it was never told of the work it met, nor that work of it; an issue's number names each claim on it in turn, so a marker counts for a claim's work, on it or naming it, only when it came no earlier than the push that made the claim's branch, by GitHub's time for that push from the activity, as F13's order dates a branch, never by the draft's opening (coordinator.claims_made, coordinator.heard_of, oracle.claims_made, SPEC["in turn"]). Each has a test that fails on b55b083 (tests/test_coordinator.py and tests/test_github.py), (1) and (3) steps of F2's trace and a mutant each, "the markers on a claim's own pull request never read" caught at step 15 and "a marker counted whenever it came" at step 8, both for G7; (2) none, since no lane opens a second pull request from one branch. The documents' pass found wording: the F2 entry's "What happens" in the present tense, F13's entry calling as_built F2, the checkpoint named without its repository, an example in 5.1 that read as current, a bare class name, one sentence too broad about the upgrade (answered by (1) now), and the oracle's unused `known` said in its docstrings as the other rules say it; each is answered.
- **The second round.** Two independent passes at dbe8f87 found no loop, no telling missed where b55b083 told, and no disagreement between the oracle and the library in any fault-free run, and these. The oracle never read the spec's clause on a claim GitHub does not date, and a run an activity fault touched had none of its writes judged, though since the review such a fault decides which work is told: a library that dated such a claim as the newest of all, taking no marker, told every claim on every such run and no lane saw it. The oracle now leaves the branches the run could not date out of its dates (oracle.claims_made, expected_effects' unsaid), and a run whose only faults were on the activity has its writes judged (invariants.injected, WRITES_KEPT), though not its quiet second run, which dates them; F2's trace gains a board run with every activity read refused that tells nothing, and the mutant "an undated claim taking no marker" is caught there at step 16 for G7 (93 in all). The Overlaps listed a file the two pull requests of one claim alone change, against the spec's "more than one piece"; board.overlaps and oracle.overlaps count pieces by identity now (a test in tests/test_coordinator.py). The spec's "the pull requests from its branch" took in a stranger's, which the same sentence makes a piece of its own; it says "its own pull requests". And documents: "The fix" named the oracle reading the identity alone and the singular first line only; "What it leaves" said a claim's draft dates it, where the draft dates its place in the order and only the activity dates its push; the extra read recurs on every run for a claim told on its pull request before the fix; a branch restored by hand, and the rename F13 assumes, would each tell a claim once more; the hand marker on a pull request is judged by the trace alone; and a stranger's pull request whose standing changes between runs is told twice (F19's). A marker the minute a claim anew is made counts for it, reachable by the verbs a scope already enables and seen by no lane, since the oracle reads the minute as the library does; that stays as "What it leaves" records it.

**F3. The push guard passes forced pushes and pushes to main in plain shapes.** Status: fixed in 0.4.1 (#11).
- **What happens.** guard.refusal takes a leading `+` off a refspec (guard.py:171) but does not count it as a force; it counts only `-f` and `--force`, matched exactly (150-151), matches `--all`, `--branches` and `--mirror` exactly (154), and skips every other word that starts with a dash (158-159), so bundled short options (`-fu`) and the abbreviations git accepts (`--al`, `--mirr`) pass. It takes only `refs/heads/` off a destination (174), so `heads/main` passes. push_arguments reads a command as a push only when its first word is git (125), so `FOO=1 git push origin main`, and the same behind `env`, `command`, `nice` and `time`, pass.
- **Evidence.** `python3 validation/prototype/probe_guard.py` lists the shapes the guard passes. `python3 validation/prototype/guard_grammar.py` judged 10,800 commands with a hand-written oracle and found 7,734 of the 8,550 forbidden ones let through, and no false refusal; by cause, 132 are a plain `+` refspec, 1,596 another worker's claim, and the rest the same causes behind a prefix or inside `bash -c`. `python3 validation/prototype/gitflags.py` shows git 2.43 performing `-fu` as a forced update, `--al` as `--all`, `--mirr` as `--mirror`, and `heads/main` as a push of `main`, each passed by the guard. `python3 validation/prototype/guard_git_oracle.py` judges the same shapes by what git offers. CrossHair found `+\x00` on its own (validation/prototype/properties.py, `a_forced_refspec_is_refused`).
- **Which can be fixed.** Every cause the command shows (section 8.3's first four rows) can; a command inside a string another program runs, and whose a claim branch is, cannot be read from the command alone (G11b).

**F4. The clerk can deal one issue to two workers of the same run.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14): one job deals the whole run. `team.deals` answers for every worker of a run from one Snapshot, the same rule `next_for` gives one worker; `team deal --registry DIR [--workers JSON]` prints the run's moves as one JSON object by worker, making the label of each take first, for the workers' workflow's first job to hand to its legs. The dealing lane deals as the workflow does (clerk.workflow_deals) and fails on any double deal, F4's trace is green and its known.json entry gone, and the mutant "each leg dealt from its own look" puts the old rule back and is caught on the trace. The template's workers.yml takes the deal by a pull request of its own, reading `next` until its copy carries `deal`.
- **What happened.** Each worker in symphony-template's workers.yml asked at its own job's start (the template's workers.yml:117 until its own pull request) and got its answer from its own snapshot (team.py:265-282 at cf3bdb0), from which its idle peers were worked out (team.py:280 at cf3bdb0).
- **Evidence.** `python3 validation/prototype/probe_deal.py`, which asks `team.next_for` for each worker from its own look as the workflow did: b asks while a's ready pull request is clean and is told `take 6`; c asks after main has moved, so a's claim needs work again, and is also told `take 6`.
- **Effect.** The lock still held, so one push of `claude/6` won and the other run was wasted. Spec line 77 says the work is "dealt ... so no two race for one claim".
- **The critique's view.** It reproduced F4 and proposed no change of its own. The choice stayed the first design's: one job deals the whole run and passes the deals to the matrix, or the occasional wasted run is accepted, since the lock holds; the owner chose the first (section 14).

**F5. Two branch names can claim one issue.** Status: fixed in 0.4.1 (#11).
- **What happens.** claims.number_of accepts any decimal digits after `claude/` (claims.py:85-91), so `claude/03` and `claude/\u0663` (Arabic-Indic three) both name #3, while GitHub's first-push lock works per name.
- **Evidence.** `python3 validation/prototype/probe_names.py`: the board lists two rows, `#3 alice` and `#03 bob`, and the protocol check passes both drafts. The checker's careless scope (`python3 validation/prototype/explore.py --depth 5 --all --careless`) finds it in two steps, and also a release posted for `claude/01` that the oracle never counted due. CrossHair found `'00'`.
- **Contrast.** The gate already takes issue numbers in ASCII digits only (gate.py:74-79).

**F6. A single hand-made branch name stops the board.** Status: fixed in 0.4.1 (#11).
- **In the render.** claims.by_number tests with `isdigit` and then calls `int` (claims.py:103), which is exactly what its neighbour warns against: "a superscript passes isdigit() but not int(), and a branch name must never raise" (claims.py:86-89). board.claim_rows sorts by it (board.py:531), so render raises ValueError for `claude/\u00b2` (validation/prototype/probe_names.py) or `claude/\u27800` (CrossHair on validation/prototype/properties_two.py). board.claimed_numbers and open_work make the same mistake (board.py:326, 632-635).
- **In a live run, earlier and wider.** http.client refuses any non-ASCII request path before it connects, with UnicodeEncodeError, which is a ValueError, and GitHub.request turns it into the GitHubError "the answer was not JSON" (adapters/github.py:150-151), so cli.run_board exits 1 before STATE.md is written (cli.py:121-127). claim_text builds `/contents/docs/agents/claims/{key}.md` with the raw key, and collect calls it for every `claude/` branch (adapters/github.py:269-272, 418), so a branch such as `claude/caf\u00e9`, with no digit at all, stops every live run although the render handles it; claim_issues puts a key that passes `isdigit` into `/issues/{key}` (adapters/github.py:301-303). The prototype's fake parsed and unquoted the URL, so it could never show this; the harness's fake encodes the request line as http.client does (section 3.2).
- **Effect.** Until that branch is deleted, every board run in the repository fails, the coordinator's releases included.

**F7. A worker that has fetched can take someone else's claim.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1, the first of the owner's decisions of 2026-10-13 (section 14): before a lease onto `claude/N` the guard reads the claim file on the branch the lease holds against (the commit a `--force-with-lease=claude/N:<commit>` names, else, or when git cannot show it, as a commit written as a shell variable is, the remote-tracking ref of the remote pushed to) and the same file on the branch pushed, each at its tip or, once the branch has deleted it, where its history last added it (the last, so that an issue claimed again after a merge is judged by its new claim, not by the add main's history keeps), and refuses the lease when they name different workers or the branch pushed carries none (guard.other_holder, guard.claim_file_reader); the hook pays a few git calls on a lease push and nothing on any other command, and a claim file git cannot show, as on a branch whose history never had one, leaves the lease unjudged, as the guard fails open. The review of the fix measured the guard over git 2.43 with a bare remote: the bare lease, the lease naming the ref, the lease naming the commit and the lease written with a shell variable are all judged. The fence judges whose claim a branch carries, not who pushes, since the session's own name is nowhere in the command: a branch built on the holder's own claim commit passes, and such a push keeps the holder's work and name. The spec says so (line 55); the template's hook passes the reader (symphony-template, tools/guard_push.py).
- **What happened.** With git 2.43, a lease push after a fetch overwrites another worker's claim (`python3 validation/prototype/gitprobe.py`: the remote's claim file then says `- Worker: b`), and the guard passed it (guard.py of 0.4.1, lines 188-192).
- **Why GitHub does not stop it.** A ruleset covering `claude/**` would refuse the push, but under a repository-level branch ruleset only an administrator may rename a branch (renaming-a-branch.md:31), and the board renames every released claim. That is why the spec tells rulesets to leave `claude/**` out (spec line 69). So under the setup the spec recommends, nothing on GitHub enforces "never push to another worker's branch". Canary C7 measures the trade-off.
- **The critique's view.** A ruleset blocking forced pushes on `claude/**`, the first design's proposal, defeats itself: it refuses every `--force-with-lease`, the session's prescribed way to bring main in (spec line 55; ACCOUNT_POLICY, gate.py:121), and makes every rename an administrator's. Two better options: the guard, before a lease push, reads the claim file on `origin/claude/N` locally and refuses when its Worker line is not this worker; or, on GitHub's side, `GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/activity` (GitHub's REST description) lists each `force_push` with its `actor`, `timestamp`, `before` and `after`, which the board could read (canary C5).

**F8. Idleness is the pusher's clock.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every item), built once canary C5 passed on 2026-10-15: a claim's last commit is when GitHub received the push that set its head. github.head_since reads the branch's newest entry in the repository's activity (`GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/activity?ref=refs/heads/<branch>&direction=desc&per_page=1`, github.newest_entry) and takes its `timestamp` (github.pushed_by) when that entry set the head the board read (`after`), a push, a forced push, the branch's creation or a merge into it, or, whatever heads it names, when it came no earlier than five minutes before the run began (github.came_since, PUSH_SINCE_SLACK, for a runner's clock ahead of GitHub's, by which a push GitHub received after the run began can bear a time up to that much before the run's now), a push after the run read the branch, which says the claim had a commit then: one that moved the branch off the head, one of two or more pushes since the run read the branch, which names that head on neither side, or a delete (since F8's fix check; as the second review built it only an entry that moved the branch off the head dated it this way). An older entry that names another head is stale, a later push the activity does not list yet having set the head the run read, and dates nothing, since read as the claim's last commit it released a claim in the run its holder's push started (F8's second review; the run takes its now before it reads the branches, github.collect, and the clerk before it reads the head); nor does a time GitHub never writes (github.entry_time). A first read that dates no push, its newest entry a stale one, as a list that does not show the latest push yet gives, or no entry of the branch at all, or that GitHub gives no answer that holds (a 5xx or none at all; a rate limit's refusal, which the client has waited out as long as GitHub asks and a run waits, is a refusal like any other; github.may_answer_later), is made once more after a pause (github.read_push, PUSH_RETRY_SECONDS), since the push a run was started by is the one the activity is likeliest not to list yet, and the committer date in its place would let a commit dated back have its claim released in the run its own push started (F8's review). The second read is judged by the same now as the first, the moment the run began. In the run that a push to the branch started, which the workflow's event names (cli.pushed_branch: a push or create event's GITHUB_REF, or a pull_request synchronize whose head is in this repository, since the template's board.yml runs on no push to a claim branch itself), a head that no read dates is dated at the run's start, and so, since F8's follow-up's second review, is one a read dates by an entry that set it more than five minutes before the run began, an older push of the same head that the activity lists while it does not list the run's own yet, as a branch pushed back to a head it had before gives, or the run's own, where the run began more than five minutes after it, queued behind other runs, the log saying the newest push the activity lists of the head came before the run began (github.OLDER_PUSH_LISTED, worded so by the review of that change, since the first wording, that the activity listed only an older push, was false of a queued run's own), for that run alone, since that push came just before the run, whatever the two reads list, so no claim is released in the run its own push started, however far the activity lags and however far back its commit is dated, and the commit is not read then; the next run reads the activity again (since F8's third review; github.head_since's started_by, which github.snapshot and github.collect pass down). Where the second read dates none either, or GitHub refuses the read for good, the head's committer date stands in, the rule before the fix, so a token that may not read the activity still sees claims released and no claim is held for good; but, since F8's fix check, never earlier than the newest entry either read listed for the branch, the floor (PushRead.floor, the later of the two reads' floors by github.latest), since the push that set the head came no sooner than that. The claim is dated by the floor where it is strictly later than the committer date, the same instant in another spelling being no later and a commit GitHub does not show earlier than any floor, and by the committer date otherwise.
