In the harness the scope's forks has sam, a stranger who may not write, open a pull request from a fork whose branch is named claude/<n> there, as a draft or ready, and say a draft finished (verbs.fork_pull, fork_verbs); the model keeps the fork's head commit with the pull request (PullRequest.fork and sha; model.pull_head, pull_standing, pull_files), a rename leaves such a pull request open and the owner's reopen skips it, and GitHub runs board.yml for it while the job skips such a run, whose token is read-only (model.FORK_RUN); the fake names the fork in the pull request's head, in its detail and in the list (fake_github.head_repo); and the oracle reads the spec's sentence with no switch (SPEC["fork"], oracle.claim_of), as the protocol check's invariant does, and G9 now holds the page's open pull requests to the oracle's (oracle.pulls_spoken_to) and each claim's row to the claim's own pull request. That check met the model first: its delete left open the pull request of a branch the board deletes, one pushed again at the head a release kept, which GitHub closes (section 3.2), so the fast lane's search from S04 found the board leaving off a pull request the oracle, reading the model, still saw open; the model's delete now closes it, as its rename does.
- **What happened.** Every reading of which pull request is a claim's went by the branch's name: `p.branch == branch_of(key)` in coordinator.is_candidate and board.is_released, `p.branch not in released` in board.comments_due and pull_rows, a dict by branch in claim_rows and open_work, and the ready branches and the drafts by head ref in github.releases. A pull request from a fork whose branch is named claude/<n> there, which anyone who may open a pull request can make, was taken for claim n's own. Marked ready, it kept claim n from ever being released, since a ready pull request finishes its claim; as a draft its author said is finished, it held the claim as a finished draft does; and the board named it on the claim's row, folded it into the claim's work in the Overlaps, so the two were never told of each other, and once the claim was released left it off the page and told it nothing, as if the rename had closed it. The protocol check already failed a claim's pull request from a fork; the board and the coordinator did not read that rule.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F17.json: a claims #1, announces it and opens its draft (#100); sam opens a pull request marked ready from a fork's claude/1 (#101), changing the file a's claim lists; a board run lists the claim with its own draft and #101 as a pull request of its own, which meets the draft on src/1.py and, marked ready, lands first; at 25 hours the next run releases the claim and renames the branch, which closes #100 and leaves #101 open, and #101 hears it is idle; main then moves past sam's branch, and the run that follows tells #101 so. It is green as built and red at step 7 under five mutants: "a fork's ready pull request finishes a claim" (coordinator.is_candidate by the branch's name), "a fork's ready pull request skipped as a claim's" (github.ready_branches) and "a fork's ready pull request keeps a release from counting" (board.is_released) for G3, "a fork's pull request silenced with a released claim" (board.comments_due) for G10, and "a fork's pull request left off with a released claim" (board.pull_rows) for G9; and red at step 9 under a sixth, "a fork's pull request never told main moved" (board.pulls_behind_main leaving out a pull request from a fork), for G10. The search "a stranger's pull request from a fork on a due claim" (section 7), where the owner may move main, finds nothing on the library and catches all nine, three of them first: "a fork's finished draft holds a claim" (github.releases) for G3, "a fork's pull request named on a claim's row" (board.claim_rows) for G9, and "a fork's pull request taken for a claim's work" (board.open_work) for G7; tests/test_validation_mutants.py holds the search to all nine (validation/mutants.py, 68 in all). Each mutant changes one place: those of is_candidate and is_released read board.of_claim by the name while the function runs, those of comments_due and pull_rows board.closed_by_release, those of claim_rows, open_work, ready_branches and releases are their code before the fix, and that of pulls_behind_main leaves a fork's pull request out of what it returns. coordinator.release_text, which names the claim's own pull request in the release comment, and github.pull_detail have no mutant, since no guarantee reads the comment's words or the clerk's standing from a same-organization fork; unit tests hold them, below. tests/test_board.py, `ForkPulls`: claim_of, of_claim and closed_by_release; a ready pull request from a fork named like a released claim keeps no release from counting and stays listed; one hears what any other does; a claim's row names its own pull request, never a fork's; and a fork's pull request is work of its own that meets the claim named like its branch. tests/test_coordinator.py: such a pull request keeps no claim from its release, while the claim's own ready one does, and the release names the claim's own pull request. tests/test_github.py: ready_branches leaves a fork's out, and a fork's ready pull request or finished draft neither finishes nor holds the claim, at no call for its comments, whether the claim is released already or still held, where the claim's own draft, listed before the fork's, decides; and pull_detail asks for the repository's own pull request, never one from a fork the head filter matches, and answers None when only a fork's is open.
- **The choice made.** Read a pull request from a fork as no claim's everywhere, as the protocol check already did, rather than by its branch's name: a claim branch lives in the repository, where GitHub refuses a second push of its name, and a fork's branch of the same name is another branch, which anyone may make. So a stranger can no longer hold a claim, or hide work from the board, by naming a branch, and it costs no call, since the API's head and base already say where a branch stands (board.from_fork, from F13's review).
- **The review.** The fix's review, at cfdf22b, found nothing blocking, and these, answered here. Nothing held a pull request from a fork to "main moved": F17's trace now moves main and runs the board once more (steps 8 and 9), the ninth mutant above leaves a fork's pull request out of the word, and the fork search lets the owner move main, so it catches all nine; the lane's other searches keep the mutants they were added for (68 of 68 caught). github.pull_detail read the first pull request the head filter listed, which can be a same-organization fork's; it now takes the repository's own, with a test. The releases test had a fork's finished draft only beside a released claim; it now has one beside a claim still held, listed after the claim's own draft, whose place it took until the fix. Two readings were left as they are. The page lists a fork's pull request by its branch's name, claude/1 in the trace, as GitHub gives it, and says nothing of the fork; the claim's row names only its own pull request, so a reader can tell them apart. The model's tidy deletes a closed issue's claim branch even while a fork's pull request from a branch of that name is open, as the spec reads: such a pull request uses the fork's branch, not this repository's. And one thing the review found is a finding of its own, F19, fixed in #33 (its entry below): anyone who may read a repository may open a pull request between its branches (github/docs, content/pull-requests/how-tos/create-pull-requests/creating-a-pull-request.md:4: "Anyone with read access to a repository can create a pull request."), so a stranger may open one from the claim's own branch, claude/<n> in this repository, which every reading takes for the claim's.
- **For the template.** At the sync of the release after this, its docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the new bullet into its Rules that hold everywhere. One thing in the template reads a pull request by its branch's name the same way: .github/workflows/tidy.yml deletes `claude/<issue>` of a closed issue once no open pull request uses it, asking `gh pr list --head "$branch"`, which matches a pull request from a fork whose branch bears that name too, so such a pull request keeps the claim branch of a closed issue from being tidied; it counts only the open pull requests whose head stands in the repository itself. Two more read a branch's name the same way, as the review found: .github/workflows/add-worker.yml asks `gh pr list --head "$branch"` for the pull request a worker's file rides in, so a fork's pull request from a branch of that name is taken for it and none is opened, and tools/metrics.py counts a pull request as a claim's by its head's name alone (claim_number), so a fork's pull request from claude/<n> is counted as claim n's and keeps that claim's branch out of the claims with no pull request; each should read only a head that stands in the repository itself.

**F18. The clerk reads whose a claim is, and whose a task is, from the issue's labels alone.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the fifth review of F15's fix. The spec's Planning now says whose a claim is, as a release already named whose it was: the worker its claim file names, whatever the issue's labels say, or, where no claim file names a worker, the one its issue's label names, a worker:<name> label before the older spellings and the first by name among labels of one spelling; and its Labels section says an issue the labels of several workers name is given to each of them, and the first of them to claim it holds it, which Planning deals to the first of them in the team's order with nothing else to do, after each worker's own claims and any task given to it alone. The clerk reads it so. team.Snapshot.named reads a claim's file once a look, through the Client's new claim_text (github.GitHub.claim_text, the board's own read of the contents API; clerk.ModelClient.claim_text in the harness), and where the file names nobody, or GitHub gave no text of it, takes labels.worker_of, the first by name among the labels of one spelling, which it always was, since labels.label_names sorts them, so the answer never hung on the order GitHub lists the labels in; Snapshot.carry carries the claims the member holds (Snapshot.holds: the claim names the member and does not count as released) and that are not labelled blocked; Snapshot.own_move, the move no peer's can change, which next_for answers from the worker's own look, is that carry, else a task given to the member alone (team.is_mine); and team.moves_from deals the tasks several workers' labels give (team.given_to) before the shared pool, each member left with nothing, in the team's order, taking the first given to it that no member before it took. The review of the fix settled three more things. The team is every worker the deal is for, in the workers' workflow every registered worker in Actions whether or not the run starts it (team.deal_moves), so a worker outside the run keeps its share and two runs that look at one state deal no issue to two workers; the spec's Planning says so, where the fix first said "the run's order". A claim that counts as released, by the board's rule (Snapshot.released: its issue labelled neither claimed nor in-review, its own pull request, if any, still a draft, and a release comment that counts after a day without a commit, through the Client's released, github.claim_released), is nobody's from the release on, though its branch stands until the board renames it, so the clerk sends nobody back to a claim a release freed, as the board shows its issue unclaimed again; the oracle, which let anyone take such an issue, now has Pick read its branch as another's until the rename (oracle.takeable). And a claim file whose Worker line names nobody, "- Worker: " or "- Worker: (to be filled)", names nobody (claims.claim_worker), where until the review it named "", which the board showed as the worker, a release named as whose the claim was and the clerk took for the holder, so nobody carried the claim; the spec's Claim section says so.
- **What happened.** The clerk read both from the labels alone: a member's own work was an issue whose labels named it and no other worker (team.is_mine, `holders(issue) == {agent}`), a claim among them carried and a free one taken, and the pool left out any issue a worker's label names. So a worker whose label an issue still carried, as when the coordinator gave it the task and another worker claimed it, was told to carry a claim it does not hold, its session working on another worker's branch, which the rules forbid it to push to, while the claim's holder was told nothing; a claim whose issue no worker's label named, pushed and not yet announced, or that named two, since Announce takes off only a label an earlier claim left and not one the coordinator gave, was carried by nobody, so a worker in Actions whose session stopped before its Announce, or that claimed a task given to it and another, was never told to carry it, and the claim lay idle until its release; and a task given to two workers was neither one's own nor in the pool, so it was dealt to nobody, though the board shows it under Given to workers for each and Pick has each take it, 112 of the 2,048 label mixes on one issue by the review's count.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F18.json: #1 is given to a and b, #2 to a alone, and #3 is queued; a board run lists #1 under Given to workers for both; a deal to a, b and c gives a #2, b #1 and c #3; b claims #1, and its session stops before its Announce, as a worker in Actions' can, so the issue still carries a's label and b's and no claimed; c claims #3 and labels it blocked; a second deal has b carry #1, a take #2 and c, whose claim waits, nothing; then b announces, which takes a's label off (The choice made, below), so the labels name the holder alone. It is green as built; on the clerk as built until the fix it is red for G14 at both deals, #1 dealt to nobody and b dealt #3 at step 1, and b's claim carried by nobody at step 5. Four mutants undo the fix on it: at step 1, "a shared task dealt to none" (team.given_to read as is_mine) and "a shared task before one given alone" (own_move with no task given alone), and at step 5, "the clerk carrying by labels alone" (Snapshot.named as labels.worker_of), which tells a to carry b's claim, and "a blocked claim carried" (Snapshot.carry that does not leave a claim labelled blocked to wait). The lane of section 9.1, the clerk's deal against Pick, finds nothing on the library and fifteen distinct problems on the clerk as built, in 7,256 of its 30,720 deals, and catches the two mutants of the clerk F15's fifth review found no catcher of, "the clerk dealing blockers in number order" and "the clerk dealing an issue labelled blocked" (Snapshot.pool and Snapshot.free); the third, "the Overlaps landing a blocker after other work" (board.landing_key without the blocker first), is caught on the new search two claims on one file, the later a blocker (section 7). validation/mutants.py has 78 mutants: the review's answers added three, each caught by the lane of section 9.1, a released claim carried by its holder (Snapshot.released reading no release), a Worker line naming nobody taken for a name (claims.claim_worker as it was before the review) and a run dealt as though it were the whole team (deal_moves dealing over the run alone). tests/test_team.py: a claim is carried by the worker its file names whatever the labels say, and by the label's worker where the file names nobody, has no Worker line or was not read; a task two workers' labels give goes to the first of them in the team's order, and to the next where the first carries a claim; a task given alone comes before one given to several, in either order, next_for agreeing; and a released claim is carried by nobody and its issue dealt to nobody until the rename, the Client asked whether it is released only where its labels and its pull request leave a release possible; and, from the second review, with a client that counts its reads, an unannounced claim's pull request is read once a look, a peer's claim costs a worker's own look its claim file alone, and a worker outside the team is dealt after it, as the command answers a session or a routine the registry names. tests/test_labels.py: worker_of reads the first by name among one spelling. tests/test_github.py, `TeamClient`: the clerk reads the claim file over REST, once for the look, and a read GitHub refuses stops the look where a 404 is no file; github.claim_released asks the branch's head and its commit, and only once that commit is more than a day old the issue's comments and a permission per author no association vouches for, a branch GitHub does not know being no released claim. tests/test_board.py: a claim file whose Worker line names nobody is shown as unnamed, as a file with no Worker line is; tests/test_coordinator.py: a release names the label's worker for such a file. tests/test_validation_dealing.py: the lane's cases, and each mutant on the case that shows it. tests/test_validation_invariants.py: G1 owes the board's cell unnamed for such a file, and the picking lane's three file forms go through a board run green; tests/test_validation_policies.py: the harness's Announce takes every other worker's label off in any spelling.
- **The choice made.** Read whose a claim is from its claim file, as the board's claim rows and a release already do, rather than from the labels: the file is what the first push wrote, and GitHub's refusal of a second push makes it the one record of who holds the claim, while labels are put on by hand, by the coordinator and by Announce, and one given before the claim can stay. Deal a task given to several to the first of them with nothing else to do, rather than to none, which leaves work the board shows given undone, or to each, which deals one issue twice (G14). The review settled what a claim does to the other workers' labels of a task given to several: Announce takes them off, one an earlier claim left there or one that gave the issue to several workers, as the spec's Announce now says and as the harness's workers do (verbs.worker_verbs offers every worker's label; policies.announce took off worker: labels alone until the second review, which has it take off every spelling, as the spec's Labels reads them), so that from the claim on the labels name the holder alone wherever no claim file is read: the board's claim row once the finishing pull request has deleted the file, a release's "whose", and the clerk's carry of a finished claim that still needs work, each of which falls back to labels.worker_of, the first by name, and would otherwise name a peer, which for the clerk is the finding over again. The claim file still decides whose a claim is where a label disagrees, as a claim pushed and not yet announced shows. Reading the file where the branch's history last added it, as the push guard does, was set aside: it costs two calls per finished claim at every look and leaves every other reader misnaming the holder.
- **What it costs.** One read of a claim file for each open claim not labelled blocked, once a look and only when a member's carry first asks, through the contents API as the board reads claim files: up to one call per open claim for a run's deal or one worker's next. The template's workers.yml takes 1 + 2W looks a run, W the workers in Actions it starts (the deal, each leg's fresh look before its session and each leg's look for another round after it), so up to (1 + 2W) times K contents calls a run, K the open claims not labelled blocked. Whether a claim is released is asked only of a claim that names the member (Snapshot.holds reads the name first), and only where its issue is labelled neither claimed nor in-review and its own pull request, if any, is a draft (Snapshot.released), at the cost github.claim_released names: the branch's head and its commit, and once that commit is more than a day old the issue's comments and a permission per author no association vouches for; that pull request is read once a look and shared with whether the claim needs work (Snapshot.open_pull), where until the second review a look read an unannounced claim's pull request twice, and asked whether every claim was released, a peer's or an outsider's included, before the member's own move. So a peer's claim costs a member's look one contents call and nothing more, and a worker's own look (next_for) reads no peer's pull request, which tests/test_team.py holds with a client that counts its reads. Until the fix the clerk read no claim file. A read GitHub refuses stops the look, as every other read of the look does (the issues, the branches, each claim's pull request and checks; tests/test_github.py holds it for the claim file, where a 404 is no file and the label names the holder) and as the template's workers.yml has it: the deal's look that fails stops the run for the schedule to pick up, and a leg's fresh look that fails leaves the dealt move standing on its own checks, since that move came from a look that succeeded; neither guesses whose a claim is and sends a worker to another's.
- **What it leaves.** The clerk reads a claim by its branch, never by a claim file on the default branch, which Pick also counts as another's (SPEC["another's"]); such a file stands only once a claim's pull request merged with it, which the protocol check refuses, and the model never puts one there for an open issue. The oracle counts every claim its holder carries as one with work, where the clerk carries only one that needs work, which the spec does not define further; the lane makes no other kind, and a trace's deal is judged by Pick only at a look where no claim has a pull request. The lane writes a claim file's Worker line in three exact forms, naming its worker, left out, or naming nobody in parentheses, never the other spellings claims.claim_worker reads, which tests/test_claims.py holds; it gives one issue the labels domains.label_sets gives, so it never sets an account: label against an agent: label of another worker, whose order labels.worker_of settles by spelling; its workers have no areas; it puts no claim file on the default branch; and no claim in it has a pull request, so a claim that needs no work, and a finished claim whose file the pull request deleted, are judged by tests/test_team.py alone. No verb of the searches writes a claim file whose Worker line names nobody, so the board's cell for one, unnamed, is judged by tests/test_board.py and, through G1 over the picking lane's hand-written files, by tests/test_validation_invariants.py alone.
- **For the template.** The template's docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md is prose edited by hand, which no sync carries: symphony-template#87 already holds the Labels line's new sentence, the Claim sentence on a Worker line that names nobody and the Planning sentence's words, each behind "once the vendored library carries it", and the template's own vendoring pull request drops those hedges. Its .github/workflows/workers.yml looks again before each leg's session and keeps a dealt take only while the issue is free or, by its standing(), given to the worker alone, so a leg refuses a take the run dealt of a task several workers' labels give ("given to a, b") and takes nothing of the queue that run; standing() should accept a dealt take whose labels give the issue to the worker among others, and keep a fresh look's take to an issue given to it alone; its worker prompt's step 1 should read whose a claim is from the claim file and not from a label alone, since a task given to several keeps every one's label until the claim's Announce, falling back to the labels only where the file names nobody, as the clerk and a release do; and its claim skill's and prompts' Announce should take every other worker's label off. symphony-template#87 does these ahead of the release, and the vendoring pull request brings the library; until it merges such a task is taken by a person's session from the board, as before the fix, when the clerk dealt it to nobody.

**F19. A pull request someone who may not write opened from a claim's own branch is taken for the claim's.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the review of F17's fix. The spec's Rules that hold everywhere now say whose a claim's pull request is: the repository's owner's, a member of its organization's, a collaborator's or anyone else's who may write to the repository, or a GitHub App's, which opens one only where it is installed, as the workers in Actions open theirs; a pull request anyone else opens from a claim's branch, as GitHub's documentation says anyone who may read the repository can, is no claim's either: the board lists it as a pull request of its own and tells it what it tells any other, it finishes no claim, marked ready or said finished, and a release's rename closes it with the claim's own, since GitHub closes each open pull request whose branch it renames; and Work in the open says GitHub opens one pull request at a time between two branches, so a holder that finds a stranger's open from its branch closes it, as anyone who may write can, and opens its own. The library reads it so. claims.opened_by_writer says whether someone who may write opened a pull request, as the API gives it: by author_association (RELEASERS, the words a release comment is trusted by), as an App (APP_LOGIN_SUFFIX), or, for an author neither vouches for, by the adapter's answer from the collaborator permission API (github.permissions, asked once per login and client; github.may_open reads its answers so that a call GitHub refuses counts as yes). github.mark_strangers writes the board's own key stranger on each pull request from a claim branch here that such an author opened, as collect reads the open pull requests, and board.from_stranger reads the key back, so board.claim_of, which F17 made the one place that says whose a pull request is, answers None for it as for a fork's, and every reading through it follows: coordinator.is_candidate and board.is_released, which ask whether a ready pull request finishes the claim, coordinator.release_text, which names the claim's own, and board.claim_rows and board.open_work, which take only a claim's own for the claim's. github.is_claims leaves it out of the ready branches and the drafts github.releases reads, so neither a stranger's ready pull request nor its finished draft keeps a claim from being read for its release, and its comments cost no call; github.pull_detail, the clerk's read of a claim's pull request, takes the repository's own over a stranger's the head filter lists, from a claim branch alone, so the clerk (team.Snapshot.released, F18) agrees with the board, and the gate's world, which reads a branch's pull request through it too (GitHubWorld.pull_of, merge), gives a stranger's from a claim's branch no standing and never merges it; and board.closed_by_release says a release's rename closes every open pull request from the released branch here, a stranger's with the claim's own and never a fork's, so board.pull_rows leaves it off the page and board.comments_due tells it nothing more. In the harness the scope's strangers has sam, who may not write, open a pull request from the claim's own branch while none from it is open, as a draft or ready, and say a draft finished (verbs.stranger_pull, stranger_verbs); the holder may close it (verbs.close_pull), and open_draft is refused while it stands, as GitHub refuses a second pull request between two branches; the model's PullRequest carries the author's association (verbs.ASSOCIATIONS, WORKER_ASSOCIATION), which the state key holds, and a rename or a delete closes a stranger's pull request as it closes the claim's own; the fake names each pull request's author and association in the list and the detail and answers the permission API admin for the owner, write for the model's other writers, read for anyone else and 403, on every call, for a login the model's unanswered names, as a token without the right meets (fake_github; model.unanswered is part of the state key, and a search seeds it through Search.unanswered); the scripted workers close a stranger's pull request before opening their own, as the spec says (policies.announce); and the oracle reads the spec's sentence with no switch (SPEC["stranger"]; oracle.opened_by_writer, own_pull, claim_of, closed_by_release, pulls_spoken_to), and its sentence on an answer GitHub will not give (SPEC["unanswered"]: such a login's pull request stays the claim's and its release comments and overlap markers count for nothing; oracle.opened_by_writer, trusted), G9 holding each claim's row to the claim's own pull request through own_pull.
- **What happened.** Every reading F17 routed through board.claim_of took any pull request from `claude/<n>` in this repository for claim n's own, whoever opened it. GitHub's documentation disagrees with itself on who can open one: github/docs, content/pull-requests/how-tos/create-pull-requests/creating-a-pull-request.md:4 says "Anyone with read access to a repository can create a pull request.", content/pull-requests/reference/branches.md:36 says "You must have write access to a repository to create a branch, open a pull request, or delete and restore branches in a pull request.", and the REST reference for creating a pull request (github/rest-api-description, POST /repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls) says "To open or update a pull request in a public repository, you must have write access to the head or the source branch. For organization-owned repositories, you must be a member of the organization that owns the repository to open or update a pull request." The library now holds whichever is true of a repository: where only writers can open one, the marking marks nothing and costs one permission call per author no association vouches for; where a reader can, a stranger's ready pull request from the claim's branch kept the claim from ever being released, its finished draft held the claim, and the board named it on the claim's row and folded it into the claim's work in the Overlaps, exactly as a fork's did until F17. The protocol check, which reads the event alone, does not tell it from the claim's own.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F19.json: a claims #1, announces it and commits; sam, who may not write, opens a pull request marked ready from claude/1 itself (#100) before a has opened its draft; a board run lists the claim with no pull request of its own and #100 as a pull request of its own, which meets the claim on src/1.py and, marked ready, lands first; at 25 hours the next run releases the claim and renames the branch, which closes #100, so it hears nothing more. It is green as built and red under eight of F19's nine mutants: at step 6, "a stranger's pull request named on a claim's row" (board.claim_rows) for G9 and "a stranger's pull request taken for a claim's work" (board.open_work) for G7; at step 7, "no pull request taken for a stranger's" (github.mark_strangers marking nothing), "a stranger's ready pull request finishes a claim" (coordinator.is_candidate), "a stranger's ready pull request skipped as a claim's" (github.ready_branches) and "a stranger's ready pull request keeps a release from counting" (board.is_released) for G3, "a stranger's pull request listed after its release" (board.pull_rows) for G9 and "a stranger's pull request spoken to after its release" (board.comments_due) for G10. The ninth, "a stranger's finished draft holds a claim" (github.releases reading a stranger's draft), the trace does not reach, since sam marks its pull request ready; the search "a stranger's pull request from a due claim's own branch" (section 7) catches it, finds nothing on the library and catches all nine, which tests/test_validation_mutants.py holds it to. Two more came with the fix's review, each reading a permission answer GitHub refused the other way: "a refused permission answer taken as no for a pull request" (github.may_open), under which a stranger's ready pull request whose author GitHub will not answer for is marked a stranger's and the finished claim released, red for G6, the release it should not make, with G3, G7, G9 and G10 beside it, and "a refused permission answer taken as yes for a release comment" (github.may_write), under which a release marker by such an author counts and the coordinator releases nothing, red for G3, the release it owes, with G10 beside it; each Mutant names the guarantee it should break, G6 and G3, and the lane's record credits both to G10, the smallest name among the violations of the shortest trace it meets, which it reports and does not require (the second review); both on the search "a stranger's pull request from a due claim's own branch, GitHub refusing to say who may write", the one search that seeds logins GitHub answers 403 for (sam and dana), which finds nothing on the library, and each on a trace of its own in tests/test_validation_mutants.py, whose entries now carry the logins to seed; the first stranger search, in which GitHub answers, cannot tell either from the library, which the same test holds (validation/mutants.py, 89 in all). Each mutant changes one place: those of is_candidate and is_released read board.of_claim as F17's fix left it (of_claim_but_strangers) while the function runs, by the swap F17's mutants use; those of claim_rows and open_work are the library's two functions with their pull requests read so and their releases the library's (claim_rows_but_strangers, open_work_but_strangers), since the swap for the whole call they first used reached board.is_released through released_keys too, so the trace was red for one more guarantee than the mutant names (the review); those of pull_rows and comments_due read board.closed_by_release as F17 left it, closing nothing but the claim's own; that of ready_branches and that of releases are their code before the fix; and that of mark_strangers marks nothing. tests/test_claims.py, `OpenedByWriter`: an association that vouches needs no asking, anyone else is asked and counts only when they may write, an App's pull request is a claim's where the App is installed, and one that names no author counts as a writer's. tests/test_github.py: in `Releases`, a stranger's pull request from the claim's branch, to another base beside the claim's own, neither finishes nor holds the claim, marked ready or said finished, at no call for its comments and one for the permission, and the rename takes it off the page, while a claim still held is read by its own draft; a permission call GitHub refuses keeps a pull request the claim's, where a 404 makes it a stranger's, and the marking and the release rule share one answer about an author who both opened a draft from the claim's branch and posted the release comment; `MarkStrangers`: what is marked, what costs no call, only a pull request from a claim branch here, a refused answer marking nothing, and the ready branches and drafts leaving a marked one out; `Permissions`: which permission and role count, a login GitHub does not know, a refusal as no answer, one call per login and the client's one cache; `TeamClient`: open_pull leaves a stranger's out, keeps one a refused call could not tell, and takes whoever's pull request from a branch that is no claim's at no call; `ClaimReleased`: one client asks about an author once however many claims it reads; `Coordinate`: one client asks about an author once through a lost release comment and the overlaps too; `World`: a stranger's pull request from a claim's branch has no standing and is never merged, and beside the claim's own, the own one is; `WhenWorkBegan`: the log's line for a branch GitHub refused to date says a stranger's pull request from it is dated by its opening and the claim put last. tests/test_coordinator.py: such a pull request keeps no claim from its release, while the claim's own ready one does, and the release names the claim's own. tests/test_board.py, `StrangerPulls`: claim_of, of_claim and closed_by_release; a ready one keeps no release from counting and the rename takes it off the page; one from a released claim's branch hears nothing more; a claim's row names its own; one is open work of its own that meets the claim; and the key is the board's own, with the work dated by the branch's push. tests/test_validation_model.py: a rename and a delete close a stranger's pull request, and the state key holds the association and whom GitHub will not answer for; tests/test_validation_oracle.py: whose pull request is the claim's own, down to the rename closing a stranger's, and a login GitHub will not answer for keeps its pull request and loses its comments; tests/test_validation_policies.py: a holder closes a stranger's pull request before opening its own; tests/test_validation_verbs.py, `TheStrangerVerbs`: what sam and the holder are offered, and open_draft refused while a stranger's pull request stands; tests/test_validation_fake_github.py: the author, the association and the permission answered from the writers, admin for the owner and 403 for a login GitHub will not answer for.
- **The choice made.** Read a claim's pull request as one a writer opened, where GitHub's author_association (OWNER, MEMBER, COLLABORATOR, the spec's "owner, a member of its organization or a collaborator", the words a release comment is trusted by) or an App's `[bot]` login vouches for the author at no cost, and the collaborator permission API otherwise, once per login and client, the same call the release rule already makes for an author it cannot vouch for; a refused call keeps the pull request the claim's (github.may_open), since taking a claim's own for a stranger's would free a finished claim, while taking a stranger's for the claim's costs the runs in which GitHub refuses the call, each releasing no claim such a pull request holds, as every run did before the fix, since nothing remembers a refusal and one a token earns recurs every run; a 404, no such login, makes it a stranger's. The spec's sentence on an answer GitHub will not give says so, and, since the review, that a release comment or an overlap marker by such an author counts for nothing, as the library's may_write has read a refused call since the release rule was built: each direction is the one that frees nothing and silences nothing. The adapter writes the board's key stranger on the pull request (github.mark_strangers, board.from_stranger), so the rule stays in board.claim_of and no other reader asks GitHub. A release's rename closes a stranger's pull request with the claim's own (board.closed_by_release), since GitHub closes each open pull request whose head it renames (github/docs, content/repositories/configuring-branches-and-merges-in-your-repository/managing-branches-in-your-repository/renaming-a-branch.md:19), where a fork's stays open; so the board lists it while the claim is held and leaves it off once released, where it keeps listing a fork's. The spec's Work in the open says GitHub opens one pull request at a time between two branches, so a holder closes a stranger's and opens its own.
- **What it costs.** One permission call per login that opened a pull request from a claim branch here and that no association or App login vouches for (a private member of the organization shown as a contributor, say), once a client: github.permissions is the client's one cache (GitHub.asked), which every reader shares, collect's marking, the release rule (github.releases, github.claim_released, github.landed_release), the overlap markers (github.tell_overlaps) and the clerk's read of a claim's pull request (github.pull_detail), since may_write takes the client's cache when its caller passes none, so a board run and a clerk's look each ask about an author once, however many pull requests, comments or claims name them; until this change claim_released built a cache per call, so the clerk could ask once per claim it read, and the fix's first build left landed_release and tell_overlaps building one each of their own (the review). A pull request an association or an App's login vouches for costs nothing, and a repository where only writers can open a pull request costs nothing but those calls.
- **What it leaves.** COLLABORATOR and MEMBER are taken at GitHub's word, though a collaborator or a member may hold read access only, exactly as the release rule takes them (claims.RELEASERS): closing that would cost a permission call for every author, the board's and the clerk's alike. The protocol check does not fail a stranger's pull request from a claim's branch, since it reads the event alone, where a private member reads as a contributor, and such a pull request is merged by nobody who may not write. The model gives every pull request the base main, so a stranger's to another base beside the claim's own, which GitHub allows, is held by tests/test_github.py (the base board) and not searched; no deep space or soak scope has strangers (deep.py's comment), and adding one would need the scripted workers (policies.py) a close-pull step, since open_draft is refused while a stranger's pull request is open; the canary has no check for it, since the canary's token is a writer's. The marking goes by claims.is_claim_branch, so a misnamed claude/03 is marked too, which only takes it out of readings that count it as no claim's anyway; and pull_detail filters a claim branch alone, so on any other branch the first open pull request is the branch's whoever opened it, as before, which the gate's world then reads and merges as before, while on a claim branch the gate now gives a stranger's no standing and refuses to merge it, a change in the gate's answers said here as CLAUDE.md asks. Who may write is read at the run, not at the opening: a holder whose write access is gone, or whose account is, whose pull requests GitHub then shows as the user ghost's with no association, has its ready pull request read as a stranger's and its finished claim released once the branch is idle, the work kept under released/ as any released claim's; the spec's present tense says so, and a rule that took ghost's word would keep a stranger's pull request whose author is gone the claim's for ever instead. The model gives every pull request one author at a time between two branches and the oracle reads the open one as the claim's own where a writer opened it (oracle.own_pull), which rests on the one base; the scripted workers close a stranger's pull request before opening their own, though no scenario, soak or deep space has strangers yet, so only tests/test_validation_policies.py exercises the step; and under unanswered the oracle reads sam's pull request as the claim's own, as the spec says, so a scripted holder would take it for its own (policies.view fills Mine.pull with it) and could mark it ready, which no space reaches, since the one search that seeds unanswered runs no scripted worker. A refusal GitHub gives once in a run holds for the run, since every reader shares the client's cache (What it costs), where a cache per reader asked again: a lost answer during collect keeps a pull request the claim's and silences its author's comments for landed_release and tell_overlaps too, the reading the spec gives a refusal, now given once.
- **The review.** The fix's review, at bf51d42, found nothing blocking, and these, answered here. Two readers of who may write, landed_release and tell_overlaps, built a permission cache each of their own, so a board run asked GitHub twice about a login collect had asked about, against the cost this entry claimed: may_write now takes the client's cache when its caller passes none, and a test holds one client to one call through a lost release comment and the overlaps. pull_detail applied the stranger filter, and a permission call, to every branch, where mark_strangers and the spec speak of a claim's branch alone, and the gate's world reads through it: it filters claim branches alone now, with a World test for a stranger's pull request on a claim branch and a TeamClient test for another branch. The spec's sentence on an answer GitHub will not give had no state in the harness, no fault, no oracle reading and no search, so a fault added later would have turned the lane red where the library is right: the model now has unanswered, the logins GitHub answers 403 for on every call, the fake answers so, the oracle reads the sentence, a search seeds sam and dana and two mutants read a refused answer the other way, and the spec's sentence says what may_write has always done with a refusal, that such an author's release comment or overlap marker counts for nothing. The mutants of claim_rows and open_work swapped board.claim_of for the whole call, which reached is_released through released_keys, so the trace was red for G5 beside the G9 named: each is now a copy of the function with its own pull requests read as before the fix and its releases the library's. The scripted workers had no step for the spec's new instruction and would have asked for a refused draft for ever beside a stranger's pull request: announce closes it first, with a test. The log's line for a branch GitHub refused to date called a stranger's pull request the branch's own and said nothing of the claim, which lands last: it says both now. may_open's "at most a run" understated a refusal a token earns every run, said above as it is; the fake answers admin for the owner, as GitHub does; the mutants of is_candidate and is_released read of_claim as F17's fix left it, not by the name, said so above; G5 stays in F17's and F19's traces' breaks, where this round, finding it red under the claim_rows swap alone, had dropped it: the mutants of is_released, a fork's or a stranger's ready pull request keeping a release from counting, turn both traces red for it at step 7, the claim kept on the page and off the Queue, which the second round replayed; GUARANTEES.md's G10 row said the board spoke to a stranger's pull request after a release until the fix, which only the mutant does, where the library took such a pull request for the claim's own, so the row says that; and who may write being read at the run, the one base and the scripted workers' coverage are named under What it leaves. A second round, at 5081d4e, found nothing blocking, and these, answered here: G5, above; the Evidence bullet read as if the lane credited the two refusal mutants to G6 and G3, where its record credits G10, the smallest name among the violations it meets, said so now; a test's docstring kept may_open's retired "at most a run"; the Releases test on the marking and the release rule sharing one answer was credited to this round where collect handed one cache to both readers before it, so it is F19's; and the scripted workers under unanswered, and a refusal holding for the run, are named under What it leaves.
- **For the template.** At the sync of the release after this, its docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the spec's new sentence in Work in the open and the new bullet in Rules that hold everywhere, with its sentence on an answer GitHub will not give; its worker prompt's and claim skill's step that opens the draft should not take a stranger's pull request from the branch for its own, but close it, as the spec says, and open its own; and the three readers by head name F17's entry lists (tidy.yml, add-worker.yml, tools/metrics.py) read a non-writer's pull request as the claim's the same way.

**F20. A lost reply and a refused re-read hold a claim for good.** Status: fixed after 0.4.1 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding; the handoffs call it the stuck claim); found by the review of build step 2 (symphony-protocol#16), which noted it outside its change: when GitHub answers the release comment with an error and then refuses the read of the issue's comments that would say whether it wrote the comment anyway, the run cannot tell whether the release is whole, and the spec said nothing of that case. The spec's Release now says it: where the run cannot read the issue's comments to tell whether the comment is there after all, it leaves the labels as the release left them and counts no release of its own, so its page lists the claim as held and the branch keeps its name, since labels put back beside a comment that stands would hold the claim for good, the comment telling every later coordinator the claim is released and the label claimed telling the board it is held, and the next run, reading the comments, finds the release whole where GitHub wrote the comment, or releases the claim again where it wrote none, unless its holder pushed since, which keeps the claim, or a person put the label claimed back beside a comment GitHub wrote, which undoes that release, though a branch at a head a release already kept is released in that same run by its latest release comment, where one that counts stands, which counts for such a branch whenever it came. The library reads it so: github.landed_release raises GitHubError on a read GitHub refuses, where it took the refusal for a read that listed no comment, and github.release_one answers it with github.unsettled, which brings the snapshot's labels up to date (coordinator.after_labels) with no release of its own counted, so the page lists the claim as held and the board renames nothing that run, and says so in a line (on a kept head the earlier release counts once the labels are off, What it leaves, below); the next run reads the comments and counts the release, or, finding none, releases the claim again, its labels already off counting as taken off (github.take_label_off). In the harness the faults comment-lost-then-unread and comment-500-then-unread (validation/verbs.py) are the two faults in one run that the review of step 2 said no search reached: the comment's POST answered 502 having written it, or 500 having written nothing, then the next read of an issue's comments refused with 500, one Fault chain each (Fault.then, since F8's third review firing its links in order); the oracle quotes the spec's three clauses (SPEC["unsettled"], SPEC["settled next"], SPEC["unsettled kept"]); invariants.unread_releases names the issues whose release a run could not settle, by the first read of an issue's comments after its failed post, labels_put_back holds such a run to putting nothing back, unsettled_left_alone holds it to renaming, deleting and saying nothing of the branch but on a kept head with a release comment that counts in the state the run started from, a condition no verb can make false on a kept head, since every kept head the verbs make has its release comment, seen takes the comment the run could not read back out of the oracle's view of the page, from a copy, and reads_failed no longer excuses a run that fails over that read, since the run does without it (unsettling_reads, G15).
- **What happened.** Until the fix landed_release caught the refused read and answered "", as for a read that listed no comment, so release_one put the labels back and took off the todo it put on. Where GitHub had written the comment, the claim was held for good: every later coordinator found the release comment, more than 24 hours after the branch's last commit, and skipped the claim as one it had released, while the board, seeing the label claimed, counted no release and never renamed the branch; only a person's hand freed it. Where GitHub had written nothing, putting the labels back did no harm, but the run cannot tell the two apart.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F20.json: a claims #1, and b claims #2 an hour later; at 25 hours a board run under comment-lost-then-unread leaves #1 labelled todo alone, with the release comment GitHub wrote, the branch claude/1 kept and the claim listed as held, and the next run counts the release and renames the branch; at 26 hours a run under comment-500-then-unread leaves #2 so with no comment, and the next run releases the claim again. It is green as built and was red at steps 8 and 10 under F20's three mutants, the first only until F23's fix, since which it is red at step 21 (below): "an unsettled release put back" (landed_release as before the fix) and "an unsettled release counted" (release_one counting the release at the run's start) for G3L, and "an unsettled release failing the run" (unsettled letting the refused read through) for G15, which is red at step 15 too; none of the three targets the kept head, since under the first the trace has no released branch of #1 to push at step 12 and so never makes the kept head, and the second counts on a kept head what the library counts there, so steps 12 to 16 are held by the trace's expectations and by tests/test_github.py rather than by a mutant of the lane; the every-fault searches reach both faults too, since verbs.FAULTS holds them (validation/mutants.py, 117 in all). The trace also caught a hole in the fix's first build of the harness: seen took the comment out of the model itself, which oracle.dated gives back when nothing differs, so the comment GitHub wrote vanished from the state and the next run posted a second one. The mutants' two copies of release_one (comment_then_labels, relabelled_before_the_comment) and the copy of landed_release F8's review left (landed_release_whenever_it_came) take the refused read as the library does, so each still changes one rule. tests/test_github.py: in `Coordinate`, the unsettled line, its calls and the snapshot with the labels off, no release counted and the claim held on the page, and the test of labels GitHub will not put back now reading the comments and finding none; in `ReleaseAcrossRuns`, over two runs, a comment GitHub wrote and the run could not read back is counted by the next run, and one it never wrote is made by the next run (UnreadAfterTheComment); tests/test_coordinator.py: after_labels alone counts no release and lists the claim as held; tests/test_validation_invariants.py, `AReleaseThatCannotReadItsComment`: both faults green, each mutant caught under each (the first, since F23's fix, under the refusal alone, below), and which reads the run does without. The adversarial review of the fix (symphony-protocol#37) found the kept head: F20.json's steps 12 to 16 push a's released head again as claude/1 and put the label claimed back, and at 52 hours, under comment-500-then-unread, the run counts no release of its own while the earlier release comment frees the claim and the board deletes claude/1, with no comment of this release; tests/test_github.py has the same in `ReleaseAcrossRuns` (test_an_unsettled_release_of_a_kept_head_is_the_earlier_release), and AReleaseThatCannotReadItsComment a holder who pushes between the two runs, under both faults, and a re-read GitHub answered followed by a refused read, which is no unsettled release. The review also found unsettling_reads taking any refused read of the issue's comments after the failed post, where only the re-read that follows it says nothing, and the line foretelling the next run, which a kept head, a holder's push or a person putting the label claimed back each makes wrong. Its second review found the spec's clause on the next run naming two outcomes where a holder's push and the label claimed put back give others, the kept-head clause wanting its condition, a release comment that counts, the clerk misread in What it leaves, and the residual below of a comment GitHub lists only after the re-read. The review of F23's fix (symphony-protocol#41) found "an unsettled release put back" doing what the library does under both of F20's faults once that fix left the labels off where a read that lists none follows a server error, as both faults' posts are answered, and putting the labels back only where GitHub refuses the comment with a 4xx and then the read after it, a case no fault made, so no check told the mutant from the library: the fault comment-422-then-unread makes it, and F20.json's steps 17 to 22 have b claim #2 again and, at 77 hours, a run under that fault leave the labels off with no comment, the next run releasing the claim again, so the mutant is red at step 21 for G3L, and the other two there as well; tests/test_github.py's `Coordinate` has the same refusal and refused read, and AReleaseThatCannotReadItsComment catches the first mutant under that fault alone and the other two under all three.
- **The choice made.** Leave the labels as the release left them and count nothing, rather than put them back or count the release: putting them back is safe only where no comment stands, and counting the release is right only where one does, while leaving them is safe either way, since the next run's read settles it, and until then the page lists the claim as held, as it did before the run. The line says what was done and nothing of what comes next: `#<n>: release unsettled, the comment failed: <why>; its issue's comments could not be read to see whether a release comment stands: <why>; the labels stay as the release left them`, a rendered string that is new, said here as CLAUDE.md asks; until the review of F23's fix it read "to see whether GitHub wrote it anyway", which after a refusal is no reason, GitHub having written nothing, so that review changed it on purpose to the reason that holds whatever GitHub answered: a release comment may stand that an earlier run's post or someone else wrote. With this fix, the last of the owner's decisions of 2026-10-13, the version is 0.5.0.
- **What it costs.** Nothing on a run GitHub answers; on an unsettled one, the read GitHub refused and a run more before the claim is released, the next run reading the comments as collect does for every candidate.
- **What it leaves.** Between the unsettled run and the next, the issue carries todo alone, or the labels a release leaves on an epic or a blocker, while the board lists the claim as held: a worker reading the page sees the claim and not the Queue. Where GitHub wrote no comment the clerk reads whose the claim is from its claim file (F18) and deals it to its holder to carry; where it wrote the comment, the claim counts as released by the clerk's own reading (github.claim_released), the comment standing and the labels off, so the clerk carries it for nobody and deals its holder other work while the page lists it as held, until the next run counts the release. A holder who pushes in that window keeps the claim, its labels off and todo on, since nothing makes a holder announce again, until the claim finishes or goes a day without a commit and is released again; a person who puts the label claimed back undoes the release, as at any time. On a head a release already kept, the run's release is the earlier one: once the labels are off, its comment counts for the kept head whenever it came, so the same run lists the claim as released and deletes the branch, whose work the released branch keeps, and where GitHub wrote no comment the issue hears no word of this release, as when a person takes the label claimed off a kept head; putting the labels back instead would hold such a claim for good where GitHub wrote the comment, as on any head. Where GitHub refuses the next run's read of the comments too, that run fails before it writes anything, as any run does whose read of a candidate's comments GitHub refuses (github.releases), and the claim waits for a run GitHub answers. The canary does not reach the case, which needs GitHub to fail twice in a row on one issue. The residual the second review found, an inference from how GitHub may answer and not a reading of it, GitHub answering the post with a 502 or no answer at all and listing the comment it wrote only after the re-read, so the re-read lists none, the labels go back, and the comment then stands beside the label claimed, which holds the claim for good where its holder never pushes, is F23, fixed after 0.5.0 (below): a re-read that lists none after a server error, no answer or, since its review, a rate limit's refusal is taken for one that cannot tell, and the labels go back only after a refusal that says GitHub wrote nothing, a 4xx other than a rate limit's.
- **For the template.** Its docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the spec's new clause in Release at its vendoring of main, which brings the library's unsettled release with it.

**F21. The push guard judges every push in the session's own clone.** Status: fixed after 0.5.0 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the review behind the taxonomy of 2026-10-15, whose check of the claim lock (symphony-template docs/checkpoints/2026-10-15b/taxonomy/research/verify/02-claim-lock.txt) was passed on at 21:25 UTC that day, and reproduced on main at ec49498. The spec's Work in the open has the guard read the claim file on the branch the lease holds against and on the branch pushed, which are the pushing clone's; the hook ran git in the session's working directory alone (guard.run and where_to_ask), wherever the push ran. The guard now judges each push in each clone it may run in (refused_push, push_places): placed_commands follows the shell through the command, cd, pushd and popd moving it for what follows in the same shell, bare or behind command or time, a subshell's moves ending at its ), and each part of a pipeline and each list run with & taking a shell of its own; push_place joins on the directory env names last with -C or --chdir, then each -C git is given, in turn; and refused_push asks each clone for its current and default branch and, for a lease, its claim files, each place once, and refuses the push when any judgement refuses it. Where it cannot tell the directory, after `cd -` or `cd -P`, through a path that holds what the shell expands, absolute or relative, or, since the fourth review (below), one that starts with bash's `~+`, `~-` or `~N`, or with the repository named by `--git-dir`, `--bare` or `GIT_DIR`, or since that review GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND, set before the command or exported earlier, the push is judged by what its words name, with no current branch and no claim files and the session's default branch, and, since the third review (below), in the session's own clone too. The review of the fix found two shapes the guard read as no push at all, whatever the clone: `--config-env`, whose value it read as git's verb, and an empty word, `''` or `""`, which it read as an operator ending the command, so `git -C '' push origin main` passed. GIT_VALUE_OPTIONS now holds the options of git 2.43 that take their value as the next word (git.c, handle_options), `--attr-source` and, since the third review, `--shallow-file` among them, and no longer `--exec-path`, which alone prints a path and runs nothing, as `--html-path`, `--man-path`, `--info-path` and `--list-cmds` do (GIT_QUERIES); and the empty word is a word. Reviewing the draft found three more things. A cd that fails, as into a directory that is not there, left the push after it judged in that directory, which shows nothing, though the shell runs what follows a ; or a newline from where it stood, so a lease from the clone it never left went unjudged where 0.5.0's guard, judging every push there, refused it; a cd or pushd to what is not a directory as the hook runs now leaves the shell where it stood (guard.reached, os.path.isdir by default). git's and env's -C were joined as cd's logical path is, normalised, where git and env change directory as the system resolves the path, so a .. after a symbolic link climbs from where the link leads; they are now joined as written, for the system to resolve, and cd's path is still normalised, as the shell keeps it. And a NUL byte in a directory, or in the ref a lease names, made subprocess raise ValueError, which the hook did not catch; git_line now takes it as no answer, so the hook judges such a push by its words. The third review, of the draft at 77b8a39, over real git and bash, found the guard more lenient than 0.5.0's wherever it read a move the shell does not make, and a crash. A cd in a here-document's body, which the guard reads as commands (the module's docstring says why), moved the push after the body, so a lease from the session's own clone onto another worker's claim, which 0.5.0's guard refused there, was judged in a directory that showed nothing and passed; so did one after a cd behind a test that fails (`[ -n "" ] && cd ..`), in a function never called, in a group inside a pipeline, behind a `case` pattern's `)`, one bash refuses for too many operands or for a `..` after a directory that is not there, after a quoted `~`, and after a second popd that fails; and a push whose clone the guard cannot tell, as `cd "$PWD" && git push` or `git -C "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)" push`, lost the judgement 0.5.0's guard gave it in the session's own clone. The guard cannot tell which moves the shell makes, so it now judges a push after a move also where the command starts, as if no move ran, and a push whose clone it cannot tell also in the session's own clone, as every push was judged before the fix (push_places); a refusal from either reading ends with what it adds (UNMOVED, UNTOLD), which says how to have the push judged in its clone alone. That review took git's and env's -C for exact, since the shell never skips them; the fourth found that what a relative one is relative to can be misread (below). The review also found `git --shallow-file x push origin main` read as no push, `--shallow-file` taking its value as the next word in git 2.43 as `--config-env` does, now in GIT_VALUE_OPTIONS and the grammar's global options; an absolute path after a place the guard could not tell left the push untold, where joined now takes an absolute path, or one a ~ leads to, wherever the shell stood; env's repeated -C joined on in turn, where GNU env changes directory once, to the last it is given, from where it was run (env_directory); `time cd`, which bash runs in the shell itself, `time` being a keyword, not followed (IN_THE_SHELL); and a ~ before a NUL byte or a lone surrogate, which JSON's `\ud800` carries, made os.path.expanduser raise ValueError, which the hook did not catch, so guard.run, the CLI's and the plugin's, failed with a traceback where 0.5.0's passed; joined now takes such a path as a place it cannot tell. The fourth review, of the revision at 29cf9d5, over real git and bash, found two shapes the guard still judged more leniently than 0.5.0's, and a misread tilde. A -C names its clone exactly only from where the shell really stands, and the guard can misread that: a cd into a directory the command makes first, as in `mkdir -p build && cd build && git -C .. push origin HEAD`, reads as failed, and one behind a test that fails, or in a here-document's body, as made, so such a push was judged at the -C from where the guard put the shell and from where the command starts, and never in the session's own clone, where it ran; now a push by a -C relative to where the shell stands, after any cd, pushd or popd, made or failed, is judged in the session's own clone too (after_a_move, Shell.moved), and one by an absolute -C, which names its clone wherever the shell stands, is not. And an exported GIT_DIR was read as a directory the guard cannot tell, which a later absolute -C made one it could, so `export GIT_DIR="$PWD/.git"; git -C /elsewhere push origin HEAD`, which pushes the session's own branch, was judged in /elsewhere alone; now a variable that names the repository, set alone or exported, holds whatever cd or -C comes after, as it does in the shell, until a subshell's `)` (followed_and_unmoved, Shell.named_repository), and the push is judged by its words and in the session's own clone, as one with GIT_DIR on it is. GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND, which git reads since 2.54 and which keeps the refs where its `files://<path>` says, counts with GIT_DIR (git 2.55 took a lease by `git -C` into a clone that had not fetched the claim, by the refs of one that had), and GIT_COMMON_DIR, which git 2.43 and 2.55 both showed moves no ref, does not. bash's `~+`, `~-` and `~N`, the shell's own directory, the one it stood in before and an entry of its directory stack, were read as directories named so, since os.path.expanduser leaves them as written; joined now reads them as places it cannot tell (SHELL_TILDE). Checking that fix over bash found one more: the guard read a `~` from the hook's own HOME, and took a -C from one for the clone the push runs in, alone, though a HOME the command sets before it sends the shell elsewhere and quotes, which the guard does not keep, leave a `~` as written; so `HOME=<the clones>; git -C ~/b-own push --force-with-lease origin HEAD:claude/7` from b-own, which had fetched a's claim, was judged in the hook's own `~/b-own` alone and passed, where 0.5.0's guard refused it. Now a push by a -C from a `~` is judged where the guard reads it and in the session's own clone too, with UNTOLD's advice (from_a_tilde), and a cd to a `~` is judged as after any move, where the command starts too. The refusals were settled with them: a refusal reads as 0.5.0's, and adds UNMOVED's or UNTOLD's advice only where the reading that refused it is one the command's words do not name, so an exported GIT_DIR with no cd before the push now ends with UNTOLD's advice and not UNMOVED's, and both now name an absolute path that holds nothing the shell expands, which has a push judged where it names alone after any move, UNTOLD's adding that nothing may name the repository. So every push is judged in the session's own clone, as by 0.5.0's guard, save one whose clone git's or env's -C names by an absolute path, or a relative one with no cd, pushd or popd before it in the same shell, holding nothing the shell expands, and whose repository nothing names, which is judged where the -C names alone. What it leaves, below, says where that is not where the push runs.
- **What happened.** Until the fix, with b's session in a clone on main that never fetched claude/1, `git -C ../b-other push --force-with-lease origin claude/1`, where b-other had fetched a's claim and b had committed a claim naming itself, was judged in the session's clone: its reader showed no claim file on `origin/claude/1`, the fence could not judge, and the guard passed a lease git takes in b-other, as F7's lease was taken before its fix; `cd ../b-other && git push --force-with-lease origin claude/1` the same. A plain push was misjudged the same way: `git -C ../other push origin HEAD` from a session on its claim branch, with ../other on main, pushes main and was judged a push of the claim branch.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F21.json: a claims #1; b's three leases from its session's clone, which never fetched a's claim, by `git -C ../b-other`, by `cd ../b-other &&` and by `cd ../b-other; cd ../b-nowhere;`, a cd that is made and one into a directory that is not there, and its five from its own clone, which has fetched a's claim this time, after a here-document whose body holds `cd ..`, by `git -C "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)"`, by `cd ..; false && cd b-other; git -C b`, whose skipped cd the guard reads as made, with `export GIT_DIR=<the clones>/b/.git` before `git -C <the clones>`, and by `HOME=<the clones>; git -C ~/b`, whose `~` the guard reads from the hook's own HOME, are refused, and a still holds the claim at the board; green since the fix, in memory and over git (validation/git_store.py, whose lease-elsewhere runs git in the clone the push runs in and the guard as the hook runs it from b's own clone with the library's own readers), and red for G11a at step 1 under "the guard ignores git's and env's -C" (push_place answering the shell's directory), at step 2 under "the guard does not follow the shell's moves" (moved_to answering where the shell was), at step 3 under "a cd to what is not a directory taken as made" (reached taking every target as a directory), at step 4 under "a push judged only where the shell's moves take it" (push_places giving that reading alone), at step 5 under "a push whose clone cannot be told judged by its words alone" (push_places leaving the session's own clone out), at step 6 under "a relative -C after a move judged where it names alone" (after_a_move answering no) and at step 7 under "GIT_DIR forgotten once an absolute path names a place" (followed_and_unmoved reading the places as if no variable named the repository) and at step 8 under "a -C by ~ judged where it names alone" (from_a_tilde answering no), validation/mutants.py, 125 in all. In the harness the verbs push through guard.refused_push from the worker's clone, the memory world naming each clone by its directory (verbs.clone_path, clone_named), holding a writer's clone and each clone a verb fetched into or pushed from as the directories a cd reaches (clone_exists), and keeping each clone's remote-tracking refs, b-other's apart from b's. tests/test_guard.py: `PlaceTest` (env's last -C, as env itself shows, and git's each in turn, joined as written, an absolute path or one a ~ leads to a place wherever the shell stood, the moves of cd, pushd and popd for what follows in the same shell, bare or behind command or time and not behind nice or env, a cd or pushd to what is not a directory leaving the shell where it stood, a subshell, a pipeline and a background list keeping theirs, every place it cannot tell, `cd -P` among them, and a variable that names the repository holding through an absolute cd or -C until a subshell's `)`, GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND with GIT_DIR and GIT_COMMON_DIR not), `CloneTest` (a push of another clone's current branch, a lease judged by the claim files of the clone it runs in, each place asked once, a push whose clone it cannot tell judged by its words and in the session's own clone, and a push after a cd in a here-document's body, behind a test that fails, in an `if` never taken, behind a `case` pattern's `)`, in a function never called, after `||`, in a group inside a pipeline, after a popd that fails or one bash refuses, judged where the command starts too, each refusal ending with what its reading adds, and what it costs: a push after a cd the shell makes refused for what the session's own clone shows, where one by git -C is judged where it runs; and, since the fourth review, a push by a relative -C after a cd into a directory the command makes, after one behind a test that fails and after a here-document's body judged in the session's own clone too, where one by an absolute -C after a cd is judged where it names alone and one by a relative -C after a cd the shell makes is refused for what the session's own clone shows, what that costs; a push after GIT_DIR or GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND, set alone or exported, judged by its words and in the session's own clone through an absolute -C or cd, with UNTOLD's advice and never UNMOVED's; bash's `~+`, `~-` and `~N` read as places the guard cannot tell, in joined, a cd, a pushd and git's and env's -C; and a push by a -C from a `~`, after HOME is assigned or exported in the command or with the `~` quoted, judged in the session's own clone too, with UNTOLD's advice, where an absolute -C after the `~` names the clone alone and a cd to a `~` is judged where the command starts too, and what that costs, `git -C ~/x push origin HEAD` from a session on main refused, as by 0.5.0's guard) and `OtherCloneTest`, over git with clones of one bare remote (a push of the other clone's main refused; the lease from the clone that fetched another's claim refused; the lease after a cd that is made and one that fails judged where the shell stood; the lease after a cd in a here-document's body, which bash shows it does not run, by a -C the shell expands, after `time cd` and by an absolute -C after a place the guard cannot tell, each refused; git -C through a symbolic link and .. climbing from where the link leads, while cd does not, bash and git each showing it; and, since the fourth review, the push of main and the leases by a relative -C after a cd into a directory the command makes, after one behind a test that fails and after a here-document's body, each refused where a dry run shows git would push main or take a's claim; the push of main and the lease after an exported GIT_DIR and an absolute -C refused, a dry run showing git takes the lease by the exported repository's refs; the lease after GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND refused, git 2.54 and later taking it by the refs it names, while GIT_COMMON_DIR moves no ref, git and the guard both judging that lease by the clone the -C names; and the push of main and the leases by `git -C ~/other` after HOME is assigned or exported, and by `git -C "~"` into a directory named `~`, refused where a dry run shows git would push main or take a's claim), with the query, the empty word, the NUL byte, and a ~ before a NUL byte or a lone surrogate in a cd, a pushd, git's -C and env's. The guard grammar holds the shapes of the first review and the third (section 8.3): 1,398 argument lists recorded on git 2.43.0 in 85 s, every answer of the 1,165 recorded before unchanged and each with `--shallow-file x` the same as without it, 41,940 commands judged in 6.1 s, 33,030 agreed, the 5,670 let through all inside `bash -c` (G11b) and 3,240 harmless refusals listed, where the guard of 0.5.0 lets 11,304 through outside it.
- **What it costs.** On a command with no push, nothing, as before; on a push, the same git calls in the clone it runs in, one more set for each other place it may run in, where the command starts after a move and the session's own clone where the clone cannot be told, each place asked once a command, and one look at the file system for each cd or pushd before it. A push after a move the shell does make is refused for what the session's own clone shows, as 0.5.0's guard refused it: `cd ../other && git push origin HEAD` from a session on main is refused as a push to main, though the shell pushes other's branch, and the refusal says to name the clone by git -C, whose push is judged where it runs alone. Since the guard reads a here-document's body as commands, a cd there can refuse a push the shell makes from where it stands, as a body's `cd ../onmain` before `git push origin HEAD` from a clone on its own claim: rare, and answered by a rephrasing, as a body line that starts a push is. Since the fourth review a push by a relative -C after any cd, pushd or popd is judged in the session's own clone too, so `cd ../a && git -C sub push origin HEAD` from a session on main is refused as a push to main, where the same push by `git -C ../a/sub` with no cd before it, or by the -C's absolute path, is judged where it names alone; a push after GIT_DIR or GIT_REFERENCE_BACKEND is set, alone or exported, is judged by its words and in the session's own clone whatever -C comes after, as one with the variable on it was; and a push by a -C from a `~` is judged in the session's own clone too, so `git -C ~/x push origin HEAD` from a session on main is refused as a push to main, as 0.5.0's guard refused it, where the -C's absolute path has it judged where it names alone.
- **What it leaves.** A push whose clone the guard cannot tell is judged by its words and in the session's own clone: it refuses a forced push, a push to the default or the board branch and a misnamed claim as anywhere, and what the session's own clone shows, and passes the rest, a lease onto another worker's claim that only the clone it runs in shows among them, to the second fence, as it passes a command inside `bash -c`.
