The default branch such a push is judged by is the session's, the one the hook can read. The guard reads a command as if each part of it runs, and judges a push after a move also as if none ran: a cd after `||`, in a here-document's body, behind a test, in a function or one bash refuses counts as made, and a cd into a directory the command itself makes first, as after `mkdir` or `git clone`, counts as failed, the push then judged where the shell stood, and, by a relative -C, in the session's own clone too (the fourth review). A push after moves of which the shell makes some and not others is not judged where it runs: after `cd ../a; false && cd ../b`, which bash leaves in ../a, the guard judges a push with no -C in ../b and where the command starts, where 0.5.0's guard judged it alone, and one by a relative -C at that -C from both and in the session's own clone. Where the guard judges a push by git's or env's -C alone, by one that is absolute, or relative with no cd, pushd or popd before it, holding nothing the shell expands, with nothing naming the repository, it judges it where it runs, save where the shell does what the guard does not read. It does not trust a `~` to name a clone: it reads one from the hook's own HOME, while a HOME the command sets before it sends the shell elsewhere, and quotes, which it does not keep, leave a `~` as written, so a push by a -C from a `~` is judged in the session's own clone too (the fourth review). And it does not follow a move or an export in what a command hands to eval or trap or in a file run by source or ., nor what a function or alias the session's shell has before the command does, as one named git that adds a -C of its own or one named cd may, nor a variable that names the repository given its value by read, printf -v or a for loop: it judges the push as if none of them ran. Every other push it judges in the session's own clone too, as 0.5.0's guard did, whatever such a move does; CDPATH, which can send a cd elsewhere, it does not read, and after any cd it judges the session's own clone anyway, save by an absolute -C. `git push --help`, `git push -h`, `git --help push` and `git --version push origin main`, from a clone on main, are refused as pushes to main, as by 0.5.0's guard, though git pushes nothing; GIT_QUERIES could take them, in a change of their own. The fence still judges whose claim a branch carries, not who pushes, as F7's fix says. And an absolute -C names the clone a push runs in only as the path stands when the hook reads it, before the command runs: a command that first points a symbolic link on the path elsewhere (`ln -sfn`), makes a repository there (`git init`, `git worktree add`) or writes a `.git` file there sends the push where the guard did not judge it, which any hook that runs before the command leaves to the second fence (the fifth review).
- **For the template.** Its tools/guard_push.py writes out the hook's reading itself, from simple_commands, refusal and branches in the payload's cwd, so the fix reaches its hook only when the template vendors main and that shim calls guard.refused_push; the plugin marketplace runs guard.py's own run, which takes the fix with the release.

**F22. A reopened issue keeps the labels its ended claim left.** Status: fixed after 0.5.0 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the review behind the taxonomy of 2026-10-15, whose check of the claim lock (symphony-template docs/checkpoints/2026-10-15b/taxonomy/research/verify/02-claim-lock.txt, Claim 3) was passed on at 21:25 UTC that day with F21, and reproduced on main at 4c5e3f2, whose library differs from main's head after F21's merge, ff75657, in guard.py alone, under this fix's harness. A claim's labels, claimed, in-review and its worker's, stay on its issue when the merge closes it, since nothing takes them off at a merge or a close, and the spec's Finish ended there. Reopened, the issue kept them: the board's Queue and Given to workers leave out an issue labelled claimed, the clerk deals it to nobody (team.Snapshot.free, whose docstring named this very case), and the coordinator, whose candidates are the open issues with a branch `claude/<issue>`, had no claim to release where the repository deleted the merged branch, as the template's setup has it do, so the issue was listed in no section and taken by no worker until a person took the labels off; where the repository kept the merged branch, that branch stood as its old holder's claim, which in-review, the label of a finished claim, kept the coordinator from ever releasing. The spec's Finish now says what a run does, and Release and Labels say the same: the coordinator, in the board's run, frees an open issue GitHub says was reopened that carries claimed or in-review, unless an open pull request marked ready from its claim branch finishes it; where neither `claude/<issue>` nor a branch `released/<issue>-<short sha>` stands, it puts todo on as a release does, then takes off every worker's label, in-review and, last, claimed; where one stands, in-review alone, since a claim branch is a claim whatever the labels say, which is then released once its branch has gone a day without a commit, in the same run where it has, and a released branch beside the label claimed is a release being undone; and it reads the issue again first, freeing none closed by then, and, where it read no branch for the issue, the claim branch again, freeing nothing once one stands. The library reads it so: board.Issue keeps whether GitHub's state_reason says the issue was reopened (board.REOPENED), as the adapter's claim_issues does for a claim's issue; coordinator.reopened_due names, in number order, the open issues reopened whose labels a freeing would take one off, and coordinator.freeing says what it does (coordinator.Freeing: the labels put on, the labels taken off and whether a branch stands, by claim_stands over the claim branches and the released branches, which the snapshot now lists by name, board.Snapshot.kept_names from github.kept_names); github.coordinate frees each before it reads which claims are due, through github.free_reopened, which reads the issue again and leaves one closed since, reads the claim branch again where the board read none for the issue and leaves the issue to the next run once one stands, puts todo on, takes the labels off in freeing's order, a label already gone counting as taken off (take_label_off), and brings the snapshot up to date (coordinator.after_labels, which now takes an issue no claim branch stands for), so the page the run renders shows it; and github.may_be_due counts the claim of a reopened issue as one that may be due though its issue is labelled in-review, so a claim whose in-review the freeing took off is dated with the others and released in the same run once idle. Nothing is put back: every step brings the issue nearer to free, and a run that fails partway leaves it carrying claimed or in-review, which the next run frees. In the harness the model's issue carries reopened (model.Issue.reopened), which the fake gives as GitHub's state_reason, reopened while the issue stays open and completed once it is closed (fake_github.issue_json); the owner reopens a closed issue (reopen-issue) and merges in a repository that keeps head branches (merge-keep, in memory and over git), where the scope's reopens is on (verbs.Scope.reopens); the oracle quotes the spec's five clauses (SPEC["reopened"], SPEC["reopened free"], SPEC["reopened stands"], SPEC["read again"], SPEC["same run"]) and owes the freeing before the releases (oracle.freeing, branch_stands, freed_numbers, expected_effects); and invariants.freeing_order holds a run to the freeing's order, under faults too, freeing_guarantee files a difference in a reopened issue's labels under G8, and as_freed judges the run's releases, and the labels a failed release puts back, from the state its freeing left.
- **What happened.** Until the fix nothing read whether an issue was reopened. Where the repository deletes merged branches, a reopened issue carried claimed, in-review and its worker's label with no branch: the board listed it in no section, the clerk dealt it to nobody and the coordinator had no claim to release, so no worker took it, however long it waited, until a person took the labels off. Where merged branches are kept, the reopened issue read as its old holder's claim, which in-review kept the coordinator from releasing, however long it stood; a new claim from the default branch was then a fast-forward of the old branch after a merge commit, which git takes as a plain update, so the claim lock made no new branch and the board named the new worker on the old holder's claim, or, after a squash merge, a push git refuses, the issue held until a person deleted the branch or took in-review off, as the review's Cases B and C showed.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F22.json, played on main at 4c5e3f2 under this fix's harness (main's library with the fix's model, fake, verbs, git world, oracle and invariants), is red at step 10 for G8, the first run owing todo on #1 and worker:a, in-review and claimed off and making none, so the Queue that should list #1 is empty; at step 24 for G8, in-review owed off #1 beside b's kept claude/1; at step 25 for G3, G8 and G10, b's claim, a day idle at 26 hours, never released, its in-review read as finished; from step 26 to the end for G1, a's claim at 27 hours taken as a fast-forward of b's merged branch, which the board then names a on while its holder is b; and at steps 36 and 40 for G3, G8 and G10, that branch still never released; 18 mismatches, the same in memory and over git. With the fix it is green in both, and red under each of the seven mutants that undo a part of it (validation/mutants.py, 141 in all, the rate limits' nine landing on main before it), in memory and over git alike: for G8 at step 10 under "a reopened issue left as it is" (reopened_due naming none) and "todo put on last" (free_reopened taking the labels off before it puts todo on: "#1: a label went on after one came off"), at step 21 under "a ready pull request's in-review taken off" (freeing reading no pull request), at step 24 under "a claim branch not read for a reopened issue" (claim_stands reading the released branches alone, so the coordinator plans to free #1 wholly and, reading claude/1 again, frees nothing, its line `#1: not freed, claimed since the board read the branches`) and "in-review left on where a branch stands" (freeing taking nothing off there), and at step 40 under "a released branch not read for a reopened issue" (github.kept_names listing none, so claimed comes off beside the release being undone); and for G3 at step 36 under "the releases due read before the freeing", the steps as the trace then ran, 49 and 47 since its review extended it (coordinate reading which claims are due first, so a's claim, a day idle, waits a run). No search reopened an issue then, so the lane credits all seven, and the review's eighth, to the regression traces, its first catcher (tests/test_validation_mutants.py). tests/test_coordinator.py's `ReopenedIssues` holds the rule (no branch: todo first where it goes, every label of the claim off, claimed last; a blocker or an epic gets no todo; either label alone is enough; a branch standing: in-review alone; a ready pull request: nothing; an issue not reopened: nothing; the Queue lists the issue once freed), tests/test_github.py's `FreeReopened` the calls (the issue and the claim branch read again, an issue closed since or a claim made since left alone, a refused step ending the freeing with nothing put back and the page showing what was done, a refused read freeing nothing, the freeing before the releases, a claim whose in-review came off released in the same run once due, the released branches read from the branch list), and tests/test_board.py the snapshot's new fields, which a snapshot without them reads and writes back as before. The review's first finding, played the same way on the fix's own head at 022836c under the review's harness, is red at step 36 for G8, #1 owed todo and worker:a, in-review and claimed off and given in-review alone, the Queue empty; the search a reopened issue after a release and a later claim meets it there too, three G8 differences, and both are green since. The mutant that reads a release before the last close as an undo (github.ended_releases naming none) is the eighth, 142 in all, caught at step 36; the two searches that reopen an issue each catch every one of the eight, which tests/test_validation_mutants.py holds (test_the_searches_that_reopen_an_issue_catch_every_mutant_of_f22_s_fix). tests/test_github.py's `EndedReleases` holds the read (a release before or at the close ended, one after it or a refusal or no close not, only a candidate costing calls), `FreeReopened` the pull request read again and the branch read before claimed comes off, and tests/test_coordinator.py the rule. Its second review found two more. First, a release undone after a close and reopen by hand: a's claim on #1 released, #1 closed with no claim between and reopened, and the owner, following the release's note, putting claimed back; the release came before the close, so 5073ea3's rule counted it ended and the freeing took the person's claimed off, where before that rule nothing came off, while the spec's Release says putting claimed back undoes a release before the rename or after it. A release before the close now counts as ended only where the label claimed came through the close: the issue's events, already read, list claimed put on after the last close only for a person's undo, since a claim made since would show its claim branch (github.claimed_since_close, by the order GitHub lists the events in, so a close and a label in the same second read in the order they came); the oracle reads the same from the model's label history, which the close marks (Issue.closed_after), and the fake serves that history as the issue's events. F22.json gains it at its end, the owner taking claimed off, closing and reopening #1 and putting claimed back: red at step 54 for G8 and G3L under 5073ea3's library with this harness, green now; the mutant that undoes it (github.claimed_since_close always no) is the ninth, 143 in all, caught there, and the search a release undone on a reopened issue now starts from a release before a close and reopen by hand, so it catches that mutant and the one that reads no released branch, and the two searches still catch all nine together. The first review's stuck case, b's claimed put on before the close that ended b's claim, still frees, which tests/test_github.py's `EndedReleases` holds beside the undo. Second, ended_releases read a release comment's author through may_write, which counts a refused permission read as no, so a writer's release by hand after the close, shown to the token as a contributor, was dropped and the labels came off on a guess; it now reads the run's raw answers, and a release comment after the close counts unless GitHub says its author may not write (github.may_open's reading), so a refused answer leaves the issue out that run as a refused read of the events or the comments does, which `EndedReleases` holds too, a release before the close costing no permission read. Its third review found one more: a claim made after the close read as an undo once its branch was gone. a's claim released, #1 closed by hand and reopened, b claiming it then, its pull request marked ready and in-review on, and the owner closing that pull request unmerged and deleting claude/1, the issue left open: b's claimed went on after the close, so 1f32eb6's rule read it as a person's undo of a's release, the freeing took in-review alone off, and claimed and worker:b stayed for good on an issue with no claim branch and no release comment, listed nowhere, a regression from 5073ea3. Claimed after the close now counts as an undo only where the repository's activity, which the library already reads to date a claim's push, lists no push that made `claude/<issue>` at or after the close (github.claim_made_since, one read, the newest branch creation of that name, a making in the close's own second read as after it, so the issue is freed): a claim made since put its own claimed on, and an undo brings no such push. Where GitHub refuses the activity, keeps none of the repository (a 404) or answers with no list or no time, the issue is left out that run, as for a refused read of the events. The oracle reads the same from the model, which now keeps the minute a deleted claim branch was made (RepoModel.gone_made, beside deleted, part of the key), and the fake lists a deleted claim branch's making and delete in the activity as GitHub does (FakeGitHub.gone_entries). F22.json gains the case at its end, with a new verb of the owner's, delete-claim-branch, closing the claim's pull request unmerged and deleting its branch while the issue stays open: red at step 67 for G8 under 9c7913e's library with this harness (claimed, worker:b and todo owed and not made), green now; the mutant that undoes it (github.claim_made_since always no) is the tenth, 147 in all, caught there; and a third search that reopens an issue, a claim since a reopen ended by deleting its branch, the one with the scope's new deletes on, starts from a's release, a close and reopen by hand and b's claim ready for review, finds nothing on the library and catches that mutant, the three reopening searches together catching all ten of F22's (tests/test_validation_mutants.py). tests/test_github.py's `EndedReleases` holds the read: a making after the close or in its second freeing, none listed or one before the close an undo, b's own release after the close still an undo, and a refused, missing, odd or undated answer leaving the issue out with no comments read. The review of that fix found four more points, settled by one refined rule: claimed after the close is an undo only where it went on later than the newest entry the activity lists for `claude/<issue>`, of any kind (a making, a push or a delete), at or after the close; such an entry no earlier than the label means the label was a claim's (github.claimed_by_a_claim, reading the newest entry as github.newest_entry does, one read, an entry in the label's own second reading as the claim's). First, an empty list read as an undo even where a claim made after the close went unlisted, its entries aged out or taken by a rename; a claim that ended now leaves a delete newer than its making, which is likelier kept, and an empty list still reads as an undo, since it is what the ordinary undo shows, so reading it as a claim's would take every such person's claimed off, while an unlisted claim read as an undo only leaves claimed on, in-review off, for a person to take off. Second, an entry with no time read as no claim; it now leaves the issue out that run, as does a label claimed with no time, which costs no activity read. Third, F22.json's note on its undo at step 54 said a new claim would show its claim branch; it now says no push made claude/1 since the close. Fourth, a person's undo after a claim made since has ended, the claim's delete before the label put back, reads as an undo under the rule; F22.json gains it at its end, the owner putting claimed back an hour after b's delete, green now and red at step 69 for G8 and G3L under the mutant that reads any entry since the close as the claim's whenever the label went on (an undo after an ended claim read as the claim's), the eleventh, 148 in all; a fourth reopening search, an undo after a claim since a reopen ended, starts from b's claim ended and #1 freed, finds nothing on the library and catches it. The oracle reads the rule from the model, the newest entry of a deleted claim branch being its delete (RepoModel.deleted) and the label's minute the label history's (LabelEvent.at); invariants.push_reads leaves this read of a branch that did not stand when the run began out of G2's reads of a claim head's push, since it dates no claim. tests/test_github.py's `EndedReleases` holds each case. Since 5073ea3 the trace had not played over git: a's claim at hour 29 added a claim file identical to the one a's merged claim at hour 27 left on main, so git had nothing to commit; a's claim at hour 27 now deletes its claim file, as Finish asks, in the place of its plan comment, so every step keeps its number, and tests/test_validation_git_store.py plays the trace over git green and holds it red there, under each of F22's mutants, at the steps and for the guarantees it is red in memory.
- **The choice made.** Read GitHub's state_reason rather than guess a reopen from the labels: an issue labelled claimed with no claim branch is also one whose release a person is undoing, and the reason says which issues were closed and opened again, so an issue never closed keeps whatever labels people gave it. Free only an issue carrying claimed or in-review, the labels that say a claim holds it or has finished, claimed keeping it out of the Queue, Given to workers and the clerk's deal, and in-review keeping a claim branch from release; take its workers' labels off with them, since the claim they named has ended; and leave an issue a worker's label alone names given to that worker. Put todo on first and take claimed off last, and put nothing back, where a release puts its labels back when its comment fails: a freeing has no comment for its labels to match (G3L), and every state it can stop in still carries claimed or in-review for the next run to free, where claimed off before todo went on is the finding itself. Where a branch stands, take in-review alone off: a claim branch is a claim whatever the labels say, and its holder keeps it, or the coordinator releases it once idle as any claim, which puts todo on and takes the rest off; and a released branch beside claimed is a release being undone, which a freeing must not reverse. Read the issue and, where no branch was read, the claim branch again before writing, since a merge closes its issue a moment after the board may have read it open, and a worker may claim the issue after the board read the branches. Its lines, one per freeing and each new, a rendered string said here as CLAUDE.md asks: `#<n>: reopened with its claim ended, freed`, `#<n>: reopened with a branch standing for it`, each followed by `; <labels> put on` and `; <labels> taken off` where any went (github.done_words), `#<n>: reopened, nothing left to free`, `#<n>: reopened, not wholly freed, the next run goes on: <why>` with what was done, `#<n>: not freed, closed since the board read it`, `#<n>: not freed, claimed since the board read the branches` and `#<n>: not freed, reading it again failed: <why>`, and since its review `#<n>: not freed, its claim's pull request was marked ready since the board read it` and `#<n>: reopened, not wholly freed, claimed while it was freed, so claimed stays` with what was done; and the snapshot's JSON gains `"state_reason": "reopened"` on a reopened issue, among the open issues and the claims' issues, and `kept_names`, the released branches, and since its review `ended_releases`, the reopened issues whose released branches an earlier claim's release left before the last close, each written only where there is one.
- **What it costs.** Nothing for an issue GitHub does not say was reopened, or a reopened one carrying neither claimed nor in-review, or one whose claim's own pull request marked ready finishes it: the list of open issues every run reads carries the state_reason, and the branch list the released branches, so reopened_due costs no call. Freeing a reopened issue with no branch standing costs, once, 2 reads and a write for each label it puts on or takes off: on the fake, F22's trace at step 10, 6 calls for #1 with claimed, in-review and one worker's label, 4 of them writes, in a run of 10. With a branch standing it costs a read and the write that takes in-review off, beside the read of the activity that dates the claim, which in-review spared it before the fix (step 24, 8 calls in all), and a claim whose in-review came off is released as any claim once idle, in the same run where it is (step 36, 18 calls with the release and the rename). Its review's reads of the claim's open pull request, and of the claim branch before claimed comes off, add 2 where no branch stands (12 calls at step 10) and 1 where one does (9 at step 24, 19 at step 47 as the trace now runs), and the reads of the events and comments 2 more for a released branch beside an ended claim (14 at step 36). A worker's in-review on a later claim of a reopened issue, put on before its pull request is marked ready, comes off in the next run, as an ended claim's would, where on an issue never reopened it would keep the claim as finished; Finish has the label go on with the pull request marked ready, which then finishes the claim and keeps it.
- **What it leaves.** Since its review a released branch counts as a release being undone only where the issue's latest release comment that counts came after its last close, as the issue's events date it, or, since its second review, where its events list the label claimed put on after that close (github.ended_releases): one an earlier claim's release left before the close, a later claim having ended with it, no longer keeps claimed and the later worker's label on an issue listed nowhere. A release and a close in the same second read as the release first, so the issue is freed; a person who undoes a release, closes the issue and reopens it before any board run sees the label claimed come off, the close having ended what the undo kept, and puts it back again to keep it. Claimed put on after the close later than every entry the activity lists for the claim branch since is read as an undo whoever put it on and why, since nothing else on GitHub tells a person's undo from it, and so is the claimed of a claim made since that the activity does not list, its entries aged out or taken by a rename, which then stays on, in-review off, for a person to take off, the side chosen because an empty list is what the ordinary undo shows; a label in the same second as the newest entry reads as the claim's; the issue then stays out of the Queue, as the label claimed holds any issue, until a person takes the label off or renames the branch back. Where GitHub refuses the events, the activity or the comments, or will not say whether the author of a release comment after the close may write, or the events record no close, the released branch counts as before, in-review alone comes off, and a later run reads them again. The read costs, for each reopened issue with claimed or in-review, a released branch and no claim branch, every page of its events in every run while it stays so, one read of the activity for the newest entry of its claim branch where claimed went on after the close, every page of its comments where the events and the activity do not settle it, and a permission read, once a run, for each author of a release comment after the close whom GitHub's word does not vouch for: once where the issue is freed, since it then carries neither label, and in every run for a release being undone, which the release's note asks a person to finish by renaming the branch back and which nothing finishes for them, so this cost has no end while the person leaves it, two calls a run for each such issue where the undo came after a close (the events and the activity settle it) and one more where the release came after the close (the comments too), the events and the comments each one more per further hundred entries. No clean bound was found: a cap or a read only on a change would need a record kept from one run to the next, which the board's run has none of beyond the branch board, and a cap would leave the issues beyond it counting their released branches as undone, so an ended claim's labels stay on them for as long as undone releases fill the cap, so the cost is left as said, small beside a run's four lists. GitHub's state_reason stays reopened while the issue is open, so a later live claim on it meets the freeing too: its draft carrying in-review, put on before the pull request is marked ready, loses that label, as Finish has it earned only by the ready pull request, while a draft its author said is finished still waits for review, the release never taking one. A kept merged branch is its old holder's claim until it is released, a day after its last commit: until then the clerk deals that holder its claim to carry, a reopen read as more work for the claim it reopened, and a claim pushed from the default branch onto it meanwhile is a fast-forward after a merge commit, as the review's Case B showed, which Pick keeps a worker reading the board from. The re-reads narrow, and do not close, the races they answer: a claim pushed and announced between the read of its branch and the writes can lose the label claimed and gain todo, its branch still holding it, as F20's What it leaves says of a holder who pushes while the labels are off, and an issue a merge closes in that moment gains todo and loses the claim's labels, which a closed issue does not show. No deep space reaches a reopened issue (section 6.8), so the rules meet F22's trace, the tests and two of the mutation lane's searches; and no canary has read state_reason on a live issue: the fake gives it as GitHub's REST description has it (github/rest-api-description, the issue schema's state_reason, whose reasons are completed, reopened, not_planned and duplicate, read on 2026-10-16), which a check that closes and reopens an issue on symphony-protocol-canary would show; nor has one read the activity of a deleted claim branch: the fake lists its making and its delete as it lists a standing branch's, as GitHub's REST description of the activity list says it lists a repository's pushes and branch changes, deletions among them, which a check that deletes a branch on the canary and reads its activity would show, and where GitHub listed no entry of a deleted branch the read answers none, so claimed after the close reads as an undo and in-review alone comes off, as before F22's third review.
- **For the template.** Its docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the spec's new clauses in Finish, Release and Labels at its vendoring of the release after 0.5.0, which brings the freeing with it; its board.yml already runs on an issue's reopened event, so the run the reopen starts frees the issue.
**F23. A comment GitHub lists late stands beside the labels put back.** Status: fixed after 0.5.0 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the second review of F20's fix, which left it in F20's What it leaves, an inference from how GitHub may answer and not a reading of it: GitHub answers the post of the release comment with a 502, or with no answer at all, having written the comment, and lists it only after the run's read of the issue's comments, a write that lands late or a read served before the write reaches it. The re-read then lists no comment, and the run, taking that for a comment GitHub never wrote, put back the labels it took off, so the comment stood beside the label claimed and held the claim for good, as F20 did. The spec's Release now says that a run whose comment fails reads the issue's comments, the comment there making the release whole; that where they list none and GitHub refused the comment, with an answer from 400 to 499 other than a rate limit's, which GitHub gives in place of writing anything, the run puts the labels back; and that where they list none after any other answer, a rate limit's refusal, a server error, a redirect, an answer the run could not read or no answer at all, none of which says GitHub wrote nothing, the run cannot tell whether a release comment stands, as where it cannot read them, whatever GitHub answered (F20), so it leaves the labels as the release left them, counts no release of its own, and the next run settles it. The fix's first build put these sentences in place of the one for a run that fails at the labels, taking one off or putting todo on, which puts back what it took off and takes off the todo it put on; its review (symphony-protocol#41) put that sentence back, before them, and had the spec name every other answer, the reason that holds after a refusal, and what a holder who pushes while the labels are off keeps. The library reads it so: github.wrote_nothing says a failed write surely changed nothing only for a 4xx, a rate limit's excepted since the review and one that ends a call the client made again after a rate limit's since its second (below), and github.release_one, after a post whose re-read lists none, puts the labels back only then, and otherwise answers with github.not_listed, which, as github.unsettled does for a refused re-read, brings the snapshot's labels up to date with no release of its own counted (both through github.labels_left). In the harness a Fault may have no status, which the fake answers with the timeout http.client raises, so the adapter reads the call as one with no answer, and a read of an issue's comments may be answered without the comments GitHub wrote in the run and lost its answer to (LISTED_LATE, FakeGitHub.lost); verbs.FAULTS gains comment-late (a 502, the comment written, then a re-read listing none of it), comment-unanswered-late (no answer, the comment written, then the same) and comment-422 (a refusal, nothing written), the last since comment-rate-limited, the one 4xx the faults had, becomes a whole release once the client retries a rate limit (symphony-protocol#40), and, since the review, comment-422-then-unread (a refusal, then the re-read refused); the oracle quotes the spec's new clauses (SPEC["whole"], SPEC["refused"], SPEC["listed late"], SPEC["unread"], SPEC["unsettled"]), and SPEC["put back"] quotes the label step's sentence again since the review, as GUARANTEES.md's G3L does; invariants.unread_releases takes in invariants.unlisted_releases, the issues whose release comment GitHub answered with a server error or no answer and whose re-read, answered, listed no comment the post wrote, so labels_put_back holds such a run to putting nothing back, unsettled_left_alone to renaming nothing, and seen takes the comment out of the oracle's view of the page; and a call's status may be None, which the checks read as a failure (invariants.failed). The review's third finding was the 4xx rule resting on C4 unsaid (sections 3.2 and 11): a rate limit's refusal, a 429 or a 403 that says it is one (github.rate_limited), is a 4xx, yet GitHub's documentation never says GitHub gives it in place of acting on the call, and C4, the check that would say whether GitHub acts on such a call, is not run, so the rule put the labels back on an assumption. github.wrote_nothing now reads a rate limit's refusal as one that says nothing of whether GitHub wrote the comment, as a server error does, and the spec's Release takes it out of the 4xx that puts the labels back and names it first among the other answers (SPEC["refused"], SPEC["listed late"]). The harness reads a rate limit from the fault and never from the library's rule (invariants.may_have_written, by invariants.rate_limited), so comment-limited-for-the-run and comment-limited-then-unread, a rate limit's refusal on each of the client's tries, alone and with the re-read refused too, leave the labels as the release left them, as the library now does, and verbs.FAULTS gains comment-limited-late: a release comment GitHub writes though it refuses it for a secondary rate limit, as it would were C4 to find it acting on a call it refuses so, with a retry-after of 150 seconds, longer than a run waits, so the client makes one try and writes nothing twice (invariants.written_once), and a re-read that lists none of it. The second review found the assumption still under the rule where a rate limit's refusal is not the call's last answer: the client raises the last try's refusal alone (GitHub.request), so a comment refused for a rate limit, the wait made, and its second try refused with a 422 or a locked issue's 403 read as a 4xx that wrote nothing, and the labels went back beside a comment the first try may have written. GitHub.request now marks the refusal it raises when an earlier try of the call was refused for a rate limit (GitHubError.after_rate_limit), and github.wrote_nothing reads such a refusal as one that may have written; the spec's Release says the refusal that puts the labels back is one "on a try that followed none GitHub refused for a rate limit", and that a rate limit's refusal leaves them "on the last try or on one the run made the comment again after" (SPEC["refused"], SPEC["listed late"]). The harness judges what a post may have written by every try of it, not the last alone (invariants.tried, may_have_written, lists_its_comment), and verbs.FAULTS gains comment-limited-then-422: a release comment GitHub writes though it refuses the first try for a secondary rate limit with a retry-after of 60 seconds, which the client waits out, and whose second try it refuses with a 422, writing nothing more, so nothing is written twice, and a re-read that lists none of it.
- **What happened.** Until the fix release_one put the labels back after any failed post whose re-read listed none, so a comment GitHub wrote and listed late stood beside the label claimed: every later coordinator found it, more than 24 hours after the branch's last commit, and skipped the claim as released, while the board, seeing claimed, counted no release and never renamed the branch; only a person's hand, or a push of the holder's, moved it.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F23.json was red on main before the fix at step 16 for G3L ("#1: the release comment stands and its labels were put back"), where GitHub writes a's comment, answers 502 and lists none of it at the re-read, the claim then held at every later run (claude/1 kept and listed as a claim to the end of the trace, hour 28), at step 18 the same for b's comment, whose post had no answer at all, and at step 20 under the new rule, where GitHub answered c's comment 500 having written nothing and the run put the labels back. With the fix it is green: the runs at 25, 26 and 27 hours leave the labels as the release left them and count no release, the next run counting a's and b's comments, now listed, and releasing c's claim again, and the run at 28 hours, whose comment GitHub refuses with a 422, puts d's labels back, the next releasing it whole. It is red under F23's five mutants, which the lane credits to the regression traces (validation/mutants.py, 169 in all): "a late comment's labels put back" (github.wrote_nothing true for every failure, the rule as built until the fix) at step 16 for G3L, and at steps 18, 20 and 28; "no answer taken for a refusal" (no answer read as a 4xx) at step 18; "a refusal taken for a late comment" (wrote_nothing false for every failure) at step 22, where d's labels stay off; and, from the review, "a rate limit's refusal taken for one that wrote nothing" (every 4xx a refusal that wrote nothing, the rule until the review) at step 28; and, from the second review, "a refusal after a rate limit read by its last try alone" (wrote_nothing reading the refusal as if no earlier try had been refused for a rate limit, the rule until the second review) at step 34. The trace's steps 24 to 29 are the review's: d claims #4 again at 28 hours, and at 53 hours, under comment-limited-late, GitHub writes the release comment, refuses it for a secondary rate limit and lists it only after the re-read. Under the rule before the review the trace was red there, at step 28 alone, for G3L ("#4: the release comment stands and its labels were put back"), the claim then held to the trace's end, claude/4 kept and listed as a claim with the label claimed beside the comment; with the review's fix it is green, the run leaving the labels as the release left them and the next counting the comment and renaming the branch. The trace's steps 30 to 35 are the second review's: d claims #4 a third time at 53 hours, and at 78 hours, under comment-limited-then-422, GitHub writes the release comment, refuses it for a secondary rate limit, refuses the client's second try with a 422 and lists the comment only after the re-read. Under the rule before the second review the trace was red there, at step 34 alone, for G3L, the claim then held to the trace's end; with the fix it is green. F20's "an unsettled release put back" (landed_release taking a refused read for one that lists none) does what the library does after F20's two faults once this fix leaves the labels off where a read that lists none follows a server error, but not after a refusal whose read GitHub refuses too, where it puts the labels back and the library leaves them off; the fix's first build took it for equivalent and remade it as a wrapper around release_one patching both of its old rules, red at F20's steps 8 and 10 but two rules where a mutant changes one, which hid that difference until the review found it: the mutant is the one rule it was again, and the fault comment-422-then-unread catches it, at F20's step 21 (F20, Evidence). The mutants' two copies of release_one (comment_then_labels, relabelled_before_the_comment) follow the fix, so each still changes one rule, and the second differs from the library only after a refusal now: its shortest trace in tests/test_validation_mutants.py, and those of "the labels not put back" and "a kept head's old release taken for the lost comment", refuse the comment with comment-422, as F15's trace at step 6 and F8's at step 29 now do, since after a 500 the run leaves the labels off, which on F8's kept head would free the claim by its earlier release. tests/test_github.py: in `Coordinate`, each answer to the comment, a 502, a 500 and no answer each followed by a re-read that lists none leaving the labels off with the new line, no release counted and the claim held on the page, a 403, a 422 and a 410 putting them back, a 502 and no answer followed by a re-read that lists the comment making the release whole, and wrote_nothing true from 400 to 499 alone; in `ReleaseAcrossRuns`, a comment GitHub wrote and listed only after the re-read, after a 502 and after no answer, counted by the next run (ListedLate), a release whose comment GitHub refused with a 422, or answered 502 having written nothing, finished by the next run, a holder who comes back after a 502 keeping the claim with its labels off, which the clerk still sends the holder to carry, a kept head whose re-read after a 502 or no answer lists only the earlier release comment freed by that earlier release, as after F20's refused re-read, and the tests whose labels go back refusing with a 4xx; tests/test_validation_invariants.py, `AReleaseThatCannotTellWhetherItsCommentStands`: comment-late, comment-unanswered-late and comment-500 leave the labels off and the next run settles each, a holder who pushes in between keeps the claim, each of F23's mutants is caught under its fault for G3L alone, and unlisted_releases is read from calls; in `TheFixesOf041Hold`, a 422 puts the labels back and a comment written and listed makes the release whole; tests/test_validation_fake_github.py: a call with no answer, the write made all the same, and a read listed late; tests/test_canary.py: F23 known for a copy at 0.5.0 and nothing for one past it. From the review: in tests/test_github.py's `Coordinate`, a 422 whose re-read GitHub refuses leaving the labels off with the unsettled line; in tests/test_validation_invariants.py, AReleaseThatCannotReadItsComment under comment-422-then-unread, and a first read of the activity with no answer at all owing a second, where invariants.may_tell_more compared a status None with a number and raised. From the review's third finding: in tests/test_github.py's `Coordinate`, a comment refused for a secondary rate limit on each of the client's tries, at once for a retry-after of 150 seconds, or as a 429 on each try, whose re-read lists none, leaving the labels off with not_listed's line, and wrote_nothing false for a 429 and for a 403 that names a rate limit or a wait, true for one that names neither; in `ReleaseAcrossRuns`, such a refusal that wrote nothing finished by the next run, and one that wrote the comment and listed it late counted by it (ListedLate); in tests/test_validation_invariants.py, `AReleaseThatCannotTellWhetherItsCommentStands` under comment-limited-late and comment-limited-for-the-run, the review's mutant caught under both, F20's mutant caught under comment-422-then-unread alone, and unlisted_releases reading a rate limit from the fault, and in `TheClientsWaitsForARateLimit` comment-limited-for-the-run's labels left off; and in tests/test_validation_fake_github.py, comment-limited-late's one try, the comment written and the re-read listing none of it. From the second review: in tests/test_github.py's `Coordinate`, a comment refused for a rate limit and then, on the client's second try, with a 422 or a locked issue's 403, whose re-read lists none, leaving the labels off with not_listed's line, the same refusal on a first try still putting them back, and the refusal GitHub.request raises marked as after a rate limit only where an earlier try was refused for one; in tests/test_validation_invariants.py, `AReleaseThatCannotTellWhetherItsCommentStands` under comment-limited-then-422 with both rate limits' mutants caught, unlisted_releases and may_have_written judging every try, and invariants.tried; and in tests/test_validation_fake_github.py, comment-limited-then-422's two tries. Every one of them is red under the rule before the review, or before the second review for its own, the library's or, for the harness's own tests, the harness's, and green with it.
- **The choice made.** Leave the labels off after a server error or no answer whose re-read lists none, rather than put them back: GitHub may have written the comment, and labels put back beside a comment that stands hold the claim for good, while leaving them is safe either way, since the next run's read settles it; after a refusal, a 4xx other than a rate limit's (below), GitHub wrote nothing, so putting them back is right. The re-read after a 4xx is kept, so a refused re-read still leaves the release unsettled and a release comment someone else wrote meanwhile still makes it whole, as before; the review gave the reason in the spec and the line, since after a refusal GitHub wrote nothing of this run's: a release comment may stand that an earlier run's post or someone else wrote, and labels put back beside it would hold the claim for good, so the labels go back after a refusal only where the comments, read after it, list none, as README.md and the coordinator's docstring now say too, where the fix's first build had them go back after any refusal. No status takes in two more failures, which GitHubError gives none either: an answer that was not JSON, which comes once GitHub has done what it was asked, so it is rightly no refusal, and a call never sent (github.unsent), which a run meets only for a token or a path http.client will not send, at its first call and not at the comment. A redirect of the post, a 307 or a 308, which urllib does not follow for a POST, is no refusal either, and since the review the spec names it, with an answer the run could not read, beside a server error and no answer. The run's line for it is new, a rendered string said here as CLAUDE.md asks: `#<n>: release unsettled, the comment failed: <why>; its issue's comments list none yet, though GitHub may have written it before that failure and list it only later; the labels stay as the release left them`; and the line `#<n>: not released, the comment failed: <why>; <labels> put back` now follows only a 4xx, and since the review only one other than a rate limit's. The review changed F20's line on purpose, a rendered string: its issue's comments "could not be read to see whether a release comment stands", where they could not be read "to see whether GitHub wrote it anyway", no reason after a refusal (F20, The choice made); the template's tests pin neither line. The review's third finding is answered the same way: a rate limit's refusal leaves the labels as a server error does, so the rule after a failed comment rests on no assumption of how GitHub refuses, where it rested on C4, unsaid and unmeasured. The client's making a write again after a rate limit's refusal still rests on C4, a choice of its own with a check of its own (section 3.2, invariants.written_once), and comment-limited-late holds the client to one try, so the fault does not put that assumption to the test: a retry-after the client waits out would have GitHub write the comment twice, the double write section 3.2 names. comment-limited-then-422 does have the client make the call again after a try GitHub acted on, the case the second review found, and refuses that second try with a 422 that writes nothing, so it tests the labels' rule there with no double write. The run's line after such a refusal is not_listed's above, unchanged, since its words fit, GitHub having perhaps acted on the call before it refused it; it now follows a rate limit's refusal whose re-read lists none, where the put-back line followed it before. The version stays 0.5.0, which waits for the owner's approval without this fix; the release after it carries the fix.
- **What it costs.** Nothing on a run GitHub answers, or one whose comment it refuses with a 4xx other than a rate limit's; where GitHub answers the comment with a server error, no answer or, since the review, a rate limit's refusal the client gave up on, or, since the second, any refusal after one it waited out, and the re-read lists none, a run more before the release counts, the next run counting the comment GitHub wrote or releasing the claim again where it wrote none.
- **What it leaves.** Between the run that cannot tell and the next, the issue carries todo alone, or the labels a release leaves on an epic or a blocker, while the board lists the claim as held, but on a head a release already kept, whose earlier release, where its comment counts, frees the claim in the same run once the labels are off (F20, What it leaves); the clerk deals it as F20's What it leaves says, and a holder who pushes in that window keeps the claim with its labels off, naming no holder against Announce, until it finishes, a person puts them back or it is released again, as F20 leaves it and the spec's Release says since the review; F23 widens that window from F20's two failures in a row to any one server error, lost answer or, since the review, rate limit's refusal the client gave up on, or, since the second, refusal after one it waited out, on the comment. A comment GitHub lists only after the next run's read too is taken for none by that run, which releases the claim again, so the issue carries two release comments, which does no harm. The canary does not reach the case, which needs GitHub to answer a write with an error and list it late, nor C4, which would say whether GitHub writes a call it refuses for a rate limit and is not run (section 11), and which the labels' rule no longer needs.
- **For the template.** Its docs/agents/PROTOCOL.md takes the spec's new clauses in Release at its vendoring of the release after 0.5.0.

**F25. The push guard reads a command whole or not at all, and a here-document's body as commands.** Status: fixed after 0.5.0 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found in the reviews after F21's fix and reproduced on main at ff75657. bash runs a command text one complete command at a time, so it runs each command before a line it cannot read, and it reads a here-document's body as text, whose quotes it does not read. The guard made the text plain for shlex (plain_shell) and read it whole: where shlex raised on a quote that never closes, shell_parts returned nothing and the hook passed the command, and an apostrophe in a body opened a quote that one in a later body closed, so the push between them was read as part of a quoted word. Three shapes reproduced it, each a push bash runs that the guard passed: `cat <<'EOF' > /dev/null`, It's done, `EOF`, then the push; the push, then `echo "unclosed`; and `git commit -F - <<'EOF'`, Don't stop, `EOF`, the push, `cat <<'EOF'`, It's done, `EOF`, the usual shape of a session's commit message and pull request body around a push. The property the fix holds: every complete command bash would run is read by the guard as a command, whatever a here-document's body or a later line holds; where the guard cannot tell how bash reads a stretch of text it reads it more than one way and judges every push any reading finds, so a misreading refuses too much and never passes a push. The hook now reads each command two ways (guard.readings) and judges every push either finds, in each shell the reading places it in (guard.placed_commands): whole, as before, with a quote that never closes read as a character of its word (guard.plain_text, lenient), so the commands on either side of it are read, which reads too much of a line bash refuses whole and never too little; and, where the command holds here-documents the guard tells (guard.here_document_bodies), with their bodies cut out but for the command substitutions of a body whose word nothing quotes, which bash runs when it expands the body (guard.substitutions_in). A body is told as bash 5.2 tells it: after << or <<- outside quotes and in a substitution, double-quoted or not (guard.takes_here_document), from the first newline at its operator's level to past the line that is its word, the leading tabs off after <<-, a line of a body the shell expands joined to the next where it ends in an odd run of backslashes (guard.continues, guard.body_line), at a line that starts with the word and holds a ) inside a substitution, where bash 5.2 ends the body with a warning (guard.closes_substitution), or at the end of the text; a <<< here-string, a comment, a quoted span and arithmetic open none, a (( being arithmetic only where the ) that closes its second ( is followed at once by another, as bash reads it, and a subshell in a subshell else (guard.arithmetic_end). Since the fix's second review four more rules hold, each a shape bash ran the push of and the guard passed: a here-document whose substitution or double quotes close on its operator's own line, as `x=$(cat <<'EOF')` does, has its body read from the next newline outside them, where bash reads it with a warning (guard.leave); a case pattern's ) inside a substitution closes nothing, as bash reads a case there (guard.case_step); the parentheses of a (( are counted past quotes and backslash pairs, as bash's parse_matched_pair counts them (guard.matching_parenthesis); and what the shell runs of a body it expands is read as bash reads it, each substitution scanned to its own ) with the bodies of its own here-documents cut out in turn (guard.substitutions_in, guard.scan), since their quotes are text too, down to 16 levels (guard.MAX_NESTING), past which the command is refused unread. The scan takes a time in proportion to the text, each context opened and closed in the same time however deep and each ( matched once, so no long command runs the hook out of time, which would pass it; and since shlex builds each word a character at a time, in a time in the square of the word's length (a word of 800,000 characters took 9 seconds on main's guard as on the fix's), a command longer than 262,144 characters is refused unread (guard.MAX_COMMAND), where one at the bound reads in about a second. simple_commands, which the template's shim reads, still reads a command whole and strictly, so plain_shell gives what it gave before the fix. Since the fix's third review the scan reads a parameter expansion, ${ }, to the } that closes it, a ( or ) inside it text and a << inside it no operator, and a backquoted span to the first ` no backslash escapes, as bash's parse_matched_pair reads both, so a ) inside ${y:-)} or inside `case a in a) :;; esac` closes no substitution around it, and a backquoted span's text is scanned as a command of its own, whose here-documents' bodies lie inside it (guard.expansion_step, guard.braced_step, guard.backquoted); the count that tells arithmetic from a subshell reads them the same way (guard.matching_parenthesis), and counts the parentheses after a #, as bash does there, which reads ((echo a #(, a newline and ))) as arithmetic. Three times that grew with the square of the command are linear: every scan of one text shares its matches, where each substitution of an expanded body matched every ( after it again (guard.scan's memo; `cat <<EOF`, 8,192 of `$( (( #((((`, a newline and `)))`, then the push, took 86 seconds at 128 KB, and at 262,144 characters takes about a second); a cd joins no path longer than the system takes in one call, 4,096 bytes, which it cannot tell (guard.PATH_MAX; 43,000 of `cd a;` took 16 seconds); and a pushd stacks one object on the stack below it, one per stack (guard.stacked; 29,000 of `pushd a;` took 68 seconds), each now under a second at the bound. And since Claude Code reads a hook that runs out of time as no objection, the guard has a deadline of its own, 10 seconds of the monotonic clock checked at the head of each loop of the scan, the lexing and the placing, past which the command is refused (guard.DEADLINE_SECONDS, guard.in_time), never passed. Since its fourth review $$, the shell's process id, is read whole, so the { or [ after it opens nothing (`echo $${ ; cat <<EOF ...` hid the push), old-style arithmetic, $[ ], is read to its ] as bash reads it, quotes, $( and backquotes nested, a [ counted and a ${ not read (guard.bracketed_step), a backquoted span is read in the lenient readings as one opaque part of its word and its text as the commands of a subshell, in double quotes and in a body the shell expands too (guard.backquoted_word, backquoted_in, substitutions_in), so a quote inside one pairs with nothing outside (`` x=`echo "`; git push ... # " `` passed, as on main) and a push inside one is judged, and the deadline holds the whole run, the judging and git's answers included: it is checked before each clone a push may run in is asked about, and git gets what is left of it, a call it cuts short refusing the command (11,000 pushes, each by `git -C` into a clone of its own, took 42 seconds; guard.DEADLINE_SECONDS, guard.first_refusal, guard.git_line). The count that tells arithmetic from a subshell keeps the match of every ${ and $[ as of every (, so the delimiter word `X${` of `<<X${`, which the scan reads as a word and the count as a ${ that never closes, no longer takes the count from each later (( to the end (4,900 of `((<<X${<<'X';` ran the guard of 629440f to its deadline, and take 0.2 seconds now; guard.opener, guard.CLOSERS). Since its fifth review a backquoted span in a $( ) in double quotes is that substitution's text, which the readings leave as one part of its word as 0.5.0 does, since there it may stand in single quotes or in a quoted here-document's body, which bash never runs (`` git commit -m "$(cat <<'EOF' `` and a body that names `` `git push --force` `` in Markdown was refused; guard.backquoted_in), the whole reading runs a span's text only where the command holds no body, the reading with the bodies cut out running every span bash runs (guard.readings), and every paragraph and file of this repository written as such a message passes; and a count that ran out of text keeps, for each ${ and $[ it met with only parentheses open, how far it fell below them, so a later count that meets one holding more parentheses stops there (`}<<'X'${y:-((` repeated to 32 KB took 4.1 seconds and to 64 KB ran out of time, and takes under a second at the full length now; guard.never_closed). Three rendered strings are new, the refusals of a command nested past that depth, of one past that length and of one the guard cannot read and judge before the deadline or for which git did not answer in time (guard.TOO_DEEP, guard.TOO_LONG, guard.TOO_SLOW); the other refusals, their advice and the board read as before, and what changes is which commands reach a refusal.
- **What happened.** Until the fix each of the three shapes above, and five more, passed the guard while bash ran the push, by -c and on stdin alike (a git that records a push and runs nothing; the template's docs/research/protocol-fixes-after-0-5-0/scripts/f25-bash): the same push between two `"$(cat <<'EOF' ...)"` bodies that each hold a double quote, as a session gives `git commit -m` and `gh pr create --body`; one inside `$( )` in a body whose word is unquoted, between two apostrophes of the body's text; one after a body in a double-quoted substitution that a line `EOF)"` ends; one after a body that a line E and a backslash, then one OF, ends in a body the shell expands; and one between the bodies of two `((cd .); ...)` subshells. On random commands of quotes, here-documents, substitutions, parentheses and a push, run through bash with that git, main's guard passed 144 of the pushes bash ran in 4,000 draws, and the guard of the fix none in 10,000 (backquotes and $'...' left out, F24's and what it leaves). The fix's second review, measured the same way, found four more shapes, each the push between two bodies: two `x=$(cat <<'EOF')` whose substitutions close on their operators' lines; a body whose word is unquoted holding `$(cat <<'X' ... )` whose own body holds an apostrophe, before a body It's; two `x="$(case a in a) cat <<'EOF' ... ;; esac)"`; and `((echo "))"); cat <<'EOF' ... )` before a body It's; and that the reading took time in the square of the text on a (( or $(( that never closes, 55 seconds for `cat <<A`, `A` and 16,000 `(( `, where a hook that runs out of time passes the command. Since its fix each shape is refused, and that text takes 0.2 seconds, 16 seconds' `$((` 0.14; on 40,000 random commands of quotes, here-documents, substitutions, cases and parentheses, the review's generator over eight seeds and the first one's over one, the guard passes no forbidden push bash runs but in the shapes what it leaves names. The fix's third review, measured the same way, found the push after `x="$(echo ${y:-)} ; cat <<EOF`, a body `say "hi`, `EOF` and `)"` passed, a ) inside ${ } or backquotes closing the substitution, as on main; and three readings in the square of the text, 86 seconds for the comment shape above at 128 KB, 68 for 29,000 of `pushd a;` and 16 for 43,000 of `cd a;`. Since its fix that shape and its variants are refused, the slowest of 36 adversarial shapes and 120 random ones at 262,144 characters reads in 2.5 seconds, and on 20,000 random commands, the review's generator over two seeds and one that adds ${ }, backquotes, cd and pushd over two, the guard passes no forbidden push bash runs but in the shapes what it leaves names.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F25.json: a claims #1; b's leases from its own clone, which never fetched a's claim, in the seventeen shapes above (verbs.ELSEWHERE's apostrophe, unclosed, paired, quoted-substitution, body-substitution, closing-line, joined and subshells, since the second review closed-substitution, nested-body, case-pattern and quoted-arithmetic, since the third braced and backquoted, and since the fourth pid-brace, old-arithmetic and backquoted-quote), each refused, a still holding the claim at the board; green since the fix, in memory and over git, and, on the guard of main at 73619e3, red for G11a and G1 at steps 1 to 12, each lease taken, and for G1 at the board, step 18, steps 9 to 12 red on the guard of the fix before its second review (060c7b1) too, steps 13 and 14 on the guard of the fix before its third review (4204817) and steps 15 to 17 on the guard before its fourth (629440f), each played alone; and red for G11a and G1 at step 2 under "a quote that never closes hiding the command" (plain_text read strictly), at step 3 under "no here-document's body told" (here_document_bodies telling none), at step 4 under "a here-document in a quoted substitution read as text" (takes_here_document refusing a << in any double quotes), at step 5 under "an expanded body's substitutions cut out with it" (substitutions_in finding none), at step 6 under "a body in a substitution ended only by its delimiter's own line" (closes_substitution never true), at step 7 under "an expanded body's backslash-newline kept" (continues never true) at step 8 under "every double parenthesis read as arithmetic" (arithmetic_end as built), at step 9 under "a body that waits for a newline in the substitution that closed" (leave moving nothing), at step 10 under "an expanded body's substitution kept with its own bodies" (substitutions_in cutting none of them), at step 11 under "a case pattern's parenthesis closing its substitution" (case_step reading no case) at step 12 under "arithmetic's parentheses counted inside quotes" (matching_parenthesis counting every one), at step 13 under "a parameter expansion read as text" (expansion_step reading ${ as text) and at step 14 under "a backquoted span read as text" (backquoted reading no span), at step 15 under "$$ read as a $ before what follows" (expansion_step reading $$ as text), at step 16 under "old-style arithmetic read as text" (expansion_step reading $[ as text) and at step 17 under "a backquoted span's quotes read as the quoting around it" (backquoted_span finding no span), validation/mutants.py, 169 in all; step 1, the finding's first shape, both readings read, so no one mutant takes it. The guard grammar holds the sixth wrapper (section 8.3): 51,260 commands, where the guard of 0.5.0 and main's at 73619e3 let 5,670 of the new wrapper's forbidden pushes through and the fix none. tests/test_guard.py: `ReadingTest` (a push beside a quote that never closes, read; the lenient pass and the strict one, which gives what the pass gave before the fix on 3,000 random commands; the bodies told as bash tells them and none where bash opens none; an expanded body's substitutions run and nothing else of it; a body line joined after a backslash, by the parity of the run, and <<-'s tabs off the joined line's start alone; a (( arithmetic only where bash reads it so, its parentheses counted past quotes; a body whose substitution closes on its line read from the next; a substitution in an expanded body read with its own bodies cut out; a case pattern's ) closing nothing; bodies nested past 16 levels and commands past 262,144 characters refused unread; the reading's time bounded on the review's long commands; each shape above refused and passed by the guard as built; and a body misread hiding nothing the whole reading shows), `PlaceTest` (a push after a body placed as each reading places it), `CloneTest` (a lease after a body holding a cd judged where the command starts too, now by the reading with the body cut out) and `OtherCloneTest` (three of the leases refused, a dry run showing git would take a's claim, each passed under the guard as built).
- **The choice made.** A second reading beside the whole, never the cut reading alone: cutting bodies out in place of reading them was tried once and dropped, since telling a here-document from an arithmetic shift, a quoted marker or an opener inside a substitution takes more of the shell than the hook can read, and each misreading cut real commands out of sight. Beside the whole reading a body misread hides nothing the whole reading shows and refuses too much at worst. A third reading, of each line alone, was weighed and left out: with a quote that never closes read leniently it finds no push the two miss, and what it alone would read is text inside quotes that close, as a commit message given by -m "...", which it would refuse wherever a line starts with a push. simple_commands stays strict, since the template's shim, profile and metrics read it and their tests pin it; the fix reaches the template's hook once that shim calls guard.refused_push.
- **What it costs.** On a command with no << and no quote that fails to close, one reading as before; on one with here-documents, a scan of the text and a second lexing. The whole reading still reads each body as commands, so a body line that starts a push to main is refused and the person rephrases it or puts the text in a file, as before; a line bash refuses whole is read too, so `git push origin main; echo "unclosed`, which bash runs nothing of, is refused, answered by a rephrasing. So is a push written in a file's text by here-document, as `cat > notes.md <<'EOF'`, Don't forget:, `git push --force origin main`, `EOF`, which bash runs nothing of: the whole reading reads the body's line as a push and the cut reading the rest, by design, since the whole reading is what keeps a body misread from hiding a push; the person writes the file another way. A command whose bodies nest in the substitutions of bodies the shell expands more than 16 deep is refused unread, since reading each level again would take the hook out of time, and so is any command longer than 262,144 characters, a file's text written by here-document beside a push among them; the person writes the file first. A command the guard cannot read and judge in 10 seconds is refused as well, which no command at the bound does on the shapes timed, the slowest about 2 seconds; on a machine slow enough to reach it, the person writes the text to a file first.
- **What it leaves.** The quoting inside a command substitution, backquotes, `<( )` and `>( )` is still read as the quoting around it (F24), and bash's $'...' as a $ and a quoted span, so a push inside $'...', or after one that holds a backslash before an apostrophe, still passes. The second review and its fix's fuzzing left four shapes for later pull requests, each a candidate for a finding of its own (an F28 and after), none a shape a session writes: a delimiter holding a substitution, as `<<E$(x)F`, which bash reads by its text and the guard by a word it ends at the `$(`, so `<<E$(x)F git push --force origin main`, then a line `E$(x)F`, passes; a $'...' holding \' before a later push, read as a quote closed early (above); a # after a line continuation at a word's start, which bash reads as a comment and the guard as a word, so a quote on that line hides the next line's push; and a command word made of a substitution's empty output and text, as `"$(true)"git push`, which bash runs as git and the guard reads as a word of its own; the third review's fuzzing found the same with a backquoted span, `` `true`git push ``, and with a parameter expansion's default, `${y:-git push --force origin main}`, whose words bash runs where y is unset. Branch protection stands behind each.
- **For the template.** Its tools/guard_push.py finds the pushes itself, from simple_commands and push_arguments, which still read a command whole and strictly, so this fix reaches its hook only once that shim calls guard.refused_push, as F21's and F26's clone placement do; the plugin marketplace runs guard.py's own run, which takes the whole fix with the release.

**F26. The push guard reads a wrapper's long option only by its whole name.** Status: fixed after 0.5.0 (the owner's decision of 2026-10-13, section 14: the library fix on every finding); found by the fourth review of F21's fix. GNU's env, nice and timeout, and GNU time, read their own options with getopt_long, which takes a long option by its exact name, else by any start of its name that names no other option of the program's, as git push reads its own (F3's `--al`), and refuses a start of several names or of none, running nothing (coreutils exits 125); and it takes a value as the option says: one the option needs, attached with = or as the next word, one it may take only attached, and none for a flag. The guard compared a wrapper's long option word, in full, with the names of the options that take a value (Wrapper.long_values of 0.5.0) and read every other word that starts with -- as a flag, so the value after a start of such a name was read as the command the wrapper runs. It now reads them as getopt_long does: guard.long_option_of resolves a word, read up to any =, to the option of exactly its name, else to the one option whose name it starts, else to none; guard.long_value_is_next takes the next word as the value only for an option that needs one and has none attached; past_options and env_directory read through both, and env_directory takes --chdir by any start of its name too (guard.names_chdir), attached or as the next word. WRAPPERS holds every long option of each wrapper by what it takes, FLAG, VALUE or OPTIONAL as git push's are read, the flags among them, since whether a start names one option can be told only against all of a wrapper's names: GNU coreutils' env, nice, nohup and timeout as 9.4's --help lists them, with env's -a and --argv0, added in 9.5, its --env0-from, added in 9.12, and its --quoting-style, which coreutils' NEWS lists for the release after 9.12; GNU time as its src/time.c reads them in 1.9 and 1.10, where the option the table called --output is --output-file, the manual's `--output` a start of it; and bash's command and exec, whose builtins read no long option, and bash's own time, a keyword that takes only -p. No rendered string changes: the refusals, their advice and the board read as before, and what changes is which commands reach a refusal.
- **What happened.** Until the fix, `env --uns HOME git push --force origin main`, `nice --adj 5 git push --force origin main` and `timeout --sig KILL 60 git push --force origin main` were read as running HOME, 5 and 60, so the guard passed a forced push to main that env, nice and timeout 9.4 run, as bash shows; `time --output-file x git push --force origin main` passed behind GNU time the same way. `env --ch ../other git push --force-with-lease origin claude/1` was read as running ../other, so the push was not judged at all, and `env --c=../other git push --force-with-lease origin claude/1`, whose push the guard did find, was judged where env was run, where F21's fix judged `env --chdir=../other` in ../other: from a session's clone that never fetched claude/1, both passed a lease git takes in ../other, as F21's did before its fix. A start of several names, as env's `--d` (--debug and --default-signal) or `--i` (--ignore-environment and --ignore-signal), env refuses and runs nothing, so nothing was lost there.
- **Evidence.** validation/traces/F26.json: a claims #1; the guard on `env --uns FOO git push origin main`, `nice --adj 5 git push origin +claude/7`, `timeout --sig KILL 60 git push origin HEAD:board` and `env --def git push origin main`, each a command of the guard grammar judged by git's recorded answer, each refused; and b's lease from its session's clone, which never fetched a's claim, by `env --ch ../b-other` (verbs.ELSEWHERE's env), refused, a still holding the claim at the board; green since the fix, in memory and over git, and, on the guard of main at ff75657, red for G11a at steps 1, 2 and 3, for G11a and G1 at step 5, the lease taken, and for G1 at the board, step 6; and red for G11a at step 1 under "a long option read only in full" (long_option_of taking the exact name alone, as the guard did; steps 2, 3 and 5 with it), at step 4 under "an optional value taken from the next word" (long_value_is_next taking the next word for an option that may take a value) and at step 5 under "env's abbreviated --chdir not read" (names_chdir comparing --chdir in full), validation/mutants.py, 137 in all. The guard grammar holds the four prefixes (section 8.3): 42,872 commands, the 1,398 argument lists unchanged, judged in about half a second more than before, 33,870 agreed, the 5,670 let through all inside `bash -c` (G11b) and 3,332 harmless refusals listed, where the guard of 0.5.0 and main's at ff75657 let 471 of the new commands' forbidden pushes through. tests/test_guard.py: `WrapperTest` (a long option by any start that names it alone, behind env, nice, timeout and time, env's -a and its newer options among them; an exact name before a start, and a start of several names or of none naming nothing; the next word taken as a value only for an option that needs one; the table holding each option that the installed coreutils' env, nice, nohup and timeout list in their --help, each by what it takes; and each installed wrapper run before printf, the guard reading printf as the command wherever the wrapper runs it, and reading on past a word the wrapper refuses, exit 125), `PlaceTest` (env's --ch, --c=DIR and --chd read as --chdir, after --uns with its value and --def with none, the last directory env is given, and --uns taking --ch as its value, each place where env 9.4 runs pwd) and `OtherCloneTest` (`env --uns HOME --c=../other git push origin HEAD` from s refused as a push of other's main, and `env --ch ../other git push --force-with-lease origin claude/7` refused, a dry run showing git would take a's claim from other, each passed under the reading of the whole name alone or of --chdir alone); tests/test_validation_guard_grammar.py holds the 471 and that the fix lets none through.
- **The choice made.** A word that starts the names of several options of the wrapper, or of none, is read as taking no value, and the words after it as the wrapper would read them, rather than refusing the command or reading it both ways. GNU's wrapper refuses such a word and runs nothing, so whatever the guard reads there, no push runs, and reading on refuses a push behind it that the rules forbid, which costs only the rephrasing of a command that would have run nothing. A wrapper of an older version, which lacks one of the options a shared start names, reads the word as the other, as env before 8.31 reads `--i` as --ignore-environment, and no start that options of one wrapper share here names an option that needs a value, so reading on is how such a version reads the word too. The table is the union of the versions' options for the same reason: a start that names one option in the union names that option or nothing in any version, and a version without the option refuses the word and runs nothing, so the guard, reading every word as the newest version does, passes no push that any version up to the release after 9.12 runs. And reading on is how the guard read every long option it did not know before the fix, so it adds no rule: refusing every command such a word stands in would be a rule of its own, and reading the word both ways a second reading of the command, each to cover only an option no release has yet (What it leaves).
- **What it costs.** On a command with no wrapper, nothing; behind one, a look through the wrapper's table for each long option word, at most fifteen names. A push behind a word the wrapper refuses is judged though the wrapper would run nothing: `env --d git push origin main` is refused as a push to main, answered by a rephrasing; and `command --help git push origin main`, which bash answers with command's help, was refused so before the fix and still is.
